# 73:20: 25 questions

## 73:20/q01 What does أدنى mean here?  [أَدْنَىٰ · meaning]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q01/p1 Adnā means less than the stated portion. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): Adna means less than two-thirds of the night. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Adnā, literally nearer, is used here for less: when two things are closer, fewer intervals lie between them. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Adnā is used metaphorically for less, since what is nearer to a thing is less distant from it. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Adnā means less; “nearer” is used metaphorically because shorter distance leaves fewer intervening spaces. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Adnā here means 'less than'; using 'nearer' for 'less' is a metonymy, since less distance leaves fewer intervening spaces. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): أدنى literally means nearer, from دنو; it is metaphorically used for less because the distance to the nearest thing is small, just as farther can denote more. MUQATIL: Adna means less; the Prophet and believers prayed a part of the night, half or a third. WAHIDI-BASIT: Ibn Abbas and Muqatil say adna means less, citing 2:61. BAGHAWI-FULL: Adna means less than two-thirds. KASHSHAF-FULL: Adna means less by metaphor from nearness and fewer intervening spaces. [10 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; BURSEVI; IBNASHUR-FULL; MUQATIL; WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن عباس ومقاتل); BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 73:20/q01/p2 Adnā means nearer to the stated portion. — MAJAZ: Adna means closer or nearer. JISHUMI: Adna means nearer, like worldly life being nearer than afterlife. [2 notes: MAJAZ; JISHUMI]
- 73:20/q01/p3 Ibn Kathīr and the Kūfans read naṣfahu and thuluthahu in the accusative, coordinated with adnā. — BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (ابن كثير والكوفيون): Ibn Kathīr and the Kūfans read naṣfahu and thuluthahu in the accusative, coordinated with adnā. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL(ابن كثير والكوفيون)]
- 73:20/q01/p4 In the reading under discussion, wa-nisfahu and wa-thuluthahu are accusative and coordinated with adna. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): In the reading under discussion, wa-nisfahu and wa-thuluthahu are accusative and coordinated with adna. [1 notes: ELMALILI]

## 73:20/q02 How are the night fractions read and understood?  [ثُلُثَىِ ٱلَّيْلِ وَنِصْفَهُۥ وَثُلُثَهُۥ · readings]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q02/p1 The half and third are genitive and share the preceding preposition. — TABRISI, d. 548 (الباقون): The remaining readers use the genitive for half and a third. TABRISI, d. 548 (أبو الحسن): For the genitive reading, Abū al-Ḥasan understands a concession to pray less than the required amount. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): An objection says the genitive reading in 73:20 means the Prophet rose for less than one third, apparently leaving a required third undone. | mentions 73:3 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): With the accusative reading, the phrase means the Prophet rises less than two thirds and also rises half and a third; with genitive, less than each of two thirds, half, and a third. | mentions 73:2 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (قراءة العامة): The common genitive reading coordinates “half” and “third” with “two-thirds”: stand less than two-thirds, less than half, or less than a third; Abū ʿUbayd and Abū Ḥātim prefer it. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Al-Samin notes that the genitive parse poses a syntactic difficulty, which the following discussion addresses. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Kufans and Ibn Kathir read ونصفه وثلثه in the accusative; the remaining readers use genitive. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The reading with genitive half and third is incompatible with an analysis that allows exactly half; if the less-than-half clause already implies half, mentioning increase is likewise unnecessary. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (العربيان ونافع): The genitive reading coordinates half and third with the two-thirds phrase, meaning less than two-thirds, less than half, and less than a third. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Nâfi‘, Ebû Amr, İbn Âmir, Ebû Ca‘fer, Ya‘kūb): These readers construe the fractions in genitive coordination with two-thirds, yielding: less than two-thirds, half, and one-third of the night. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (نافع وابن عامر وأبو عمرو وأبو جعفر ويعقوب): These readers put ونصفه وثلثه in the genitive, coordinating them with ثلثي الليل: the vigil is for less than half or less than a third. QIRAAT-ER (CE): The CE genitive niṣfihi is coordinated with thuluthayī, so both are governed by min: 'less than two-thirds and its half and its third.' QIRAAT-ER (CE): The CE genitive thuluthihi parallels the niṣfihi variant; both are governed by min through thuluthayī. FARRA: Al-Farra prefers accusative half and third over genitive because they specify the lesser amount. TAB-FULL: Medina and Basra readers use genitive for less than two-thirds, half, or third. NAHHAS: Hasan, Abu Amr, Abu Ja'far, Shayba, and Nafi use genitive fractions. NAHHAS: Abu Ubayd prefers genitive because the verse says worshippers cannot measure night. NAHHAS: Both communal readings parse soundly: genitive means less than each fraction, accusative separate half and third; measuring argument proves nothing. NAHHAS: Farra allows mixed parsing of accusative third and genitive half based on report that Prophet reached a third. FARISI-HUJJA: Abu Amr, Nafi, and Ibn Amir use genitive; others accusative. FARISI-HUJJA: Abu al-Hasan objects that genitive implies less than prescribed third. FARISI-HUJJA: Genitive can show worshippers failed to complete prescribed fractions. WAHIDI-BASIT: Genitive joins fractions to min, less than each. WAHIDI-BASIT: Abu al-Hasan objects that genitive suggests failing the required third. WAHIDI-BASIT: Verse arranger says genitive minimum was one quarter, not less. JISHUMI: These readers use genitive, with adna applying to all three fractions. BAGHAWI-FULL: Other readers use genitive and kasra on final ha. KASHSHAF-FULL: Genitive gives less than each of two-thirds, half, or third; periods include quarter. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Abu Amr, Nafi, and Ibn Amir use genitive; inability concerns time count. [28 notes: TABRISI(أبو الحسن,الباقون); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(قراءة العامة); SAMIN-DURR; ALUSI-FULL(العربيان ونافع); ELMALILI(Nâfi‘, Ebû Amr, İbn Âmir, Ebû Ca‘fer, Ya‘kūb); IBNASHUR-FULL(نافع وابن عامر وأبو عمرو وأبو جعفر ويعقوب); QIRAAT-ER(CE); FARRA; TAB-FULL(قراء المدينة والبصرة); NAHHAS(أبو عبيد,الحسن، أبو عمرو، أبو جعفر، شيبة، نافع,الفراء); FARISI-HUJJA(أبو الحسن,أبو عمرو ونافع وابن عامر); WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو الحسن,صاحب النظم,من قرأ بالجر); JISHUMI(أبو جعفر ونافع وابن عافر وأبو عمرو ويعقوب); BAGHAWI-FULL(الآخرون); KASHSHAF-FULL(قراءة غير مسماة); IBNATIYYA-FULL(أبو عمرو ونافع وابن عامر); ALUSI-FULL-]
- 73:20/q02/p2 The half and third are accusative and describe separate vigil amounts. — TABRISI, d. 548 (ابن كثير وأهل الكوفة): Ibn Kathīr and the Kufans read half and a third in the accusative. TABRISI, d. 548 (أبو علي): Abū ʿAlī says the accusative reading attaches half and a third to adnā, itself accusative. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): With the accusative reading, the phrase means standing half and a third of the night. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن كثير والكوفيون): Ibn Kathīr and the Kūfans read “half” and “third” in the accusative, coordinating them with “less”: stand less than two-thirds, or stand half or a third. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الفراء): Al-Farrāʾ prefers the accusative because the verse first says “less than two-thirds” and then gives the actual lesser amounts, half and a third. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (أبو عبد الله الفاسي): Al-Fasi resolves an apparent problem with the accusative by taking half on one occasion, a third on another, and less than half or a third on another. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The accusative half repeats the opening verse's half-night option. | mentions 73:3 SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari explains accusative as praying under two-thirds plus exactly half or a third, matching the earlier choices of half, less, or more. | mentions 73:3 BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (ابن كثير والكوفيون): Ibn Kathir and the Kufan readers put half and third in the accusative, specifying the amounts included under less than two-thirds. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The accusative half and third are coordinated with adnā, describing praying less than two-thirds and praying a half or a third of the night. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (العربيان ونافع): The accusative reading coordinates half and third with adnā: the verse describes standing less than two-thirds, then standing half and a third. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): أدنى is an adverbial accusative with تقوم: the Prophet stands for a period shorter than two thirds, meaning more than half, as 73:4 indicates. | mentions 73:4 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (ابن كثير وعاصم وحمزة والكسائي وخلف): These readers put ونصفه وثلثه in the accusative as objects of تقوم, describing standing for two thirds, half, or a third, without going below half or a third. TAB-FULL: Mecca and Kufa readers use accusative for also standing half or third. ZAJJAJ: Accusative half and third clarify the lesser portion meant by less than two-thirds. NAHHAS: Asim, A'mash, Hamza, and Kisa'i use accusative half and third. NAHHAS: Farra prefers accusative specifying the small amount. FARISI-HUJJA: Accusative attaches to adna, nearer, yielding under two-thirds plus half or third. WAHIDI-BASIT: Wahidi prefers accusative half and third, meaning under two-thirds plus half or third. JISHUMI: Other readers use accusative, implying stand half and third, with adna only two-thirds. BAGHAWI-FULL: Mecca and Kufa use accusative with implied verb. KASHSHAF-FULL: Accusative gives under two-thirds plus half or third, matching earlier options. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Other Seven use accusative; inability takes another sense. [23 notes: TABRISI(أبو علي,ابن كثير وأهل الكوفة); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن كثير والكوفيون); SAMIN-DURR(أبو عبد الله الفاسي,الزمخشري); BIQAI-FULL(ابن كثير والكوفيون); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(العربيان ونافع); IBNASHUR-FULL(ابن كثير وعاصم وحمزة والكسائي وخلف); TAB-FULL(قراء مكة والكوفة); ZAJJAJ; NAHHAS(الفراء,عاصم، الأعمش، حمزة، الكسائي); FARISI-HUJJA; WAHIDI-BASIT; JISHUMI(الباقون); BAGHAWI-FULL(أهل مكة والكوفة); KASHSHAF-FULL(قراءة غير مسماة); IBNATIYYA-FULL(باقي السبعة); QURTUBI-FULL(الفراء)+]
- 73:20/q02/p3 The lām of the third fraction is quiescent. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن السميقع وأبو حيوة وهشام): Ibn al-Sumayqa, Abū Ḥaywa, and Hishām in the Syrian reading tradition use a sukūn on the lām of thuluthay. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The majority pronounce the lām in ثلثه with ḍamma, while Ibn Kathīr in Shibl's transmission leaves it quiescent. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الحسن وشيبة وأبو حيوة وابن السمقيع وهشام وابن مجاهد عن قنبل): These readers, as cited through al-Kāmil, read thulthay with a sukūn on the lām; al-Lawāmiʿ also reports it from Nāfiʿ and Ibn ʿĀmir. QIRAAT-ER (skn): The skn reading places sukūn on lām for a lighter pronunciation; the meaning of the fraction is unchanged. FARISI-HUJJA: Ibn Kathir through Shibl has quiescent lam in the third. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Ibn Kathir through Shibl reads third with still lam. [6 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن السميقع وأبو حيوة وهشام); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ALUSI-FULL(الحسن وشيبة وأبو حيوة وابن السمقيع وهشام وابن مجاهد عن قنبل); QIRAAT-ER(skn); FARISI-HUJJA(ابن كثير); IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن كثير)]
- 73:20/q02/p4 The lām of the third fraction retains its vowel. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الجمهور): The majority read ثلثي with a vowelled lām, its original form. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Most read lam of third with damma. [2 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(الجمهور); IBNATIYYA-FULL(جمهور القراء والناس)]
- 73:20/q02/p5 Abū ʿUbayda says adnā means nearer, yielding the sense that one also stands half and a third of the night. — TABRISI, d. 548 (أبو عبيدة): Abū ʿUbayda says adnā means nearer, yielding the sense that one also stands half and a third of the night. [1 notes: TABRISI(أبو عبيدة)]
- 73:20/q02/p6 Abū al-Ḥasan objects that this cannot mean nearer than half and a third; he understands it as less than those amounts. — TABRISI, d. 548 (أبو الحسن): Abū al-Ḥasan objects that this cannot mean nearer than half and a third; he understands it as less than those amounts. [1 notes: TABRISI(أبو الحسن)]
- 73:20/q02/p7 Adnā means nearer to, or less than, two thirds; the pronoun refers to night, with prayer near two thirds on some nights and near half or a third on others. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Adnā means nearer to, or less than, two thirds; the pronoun refers to night, with prayer near two thirds on some nights and near half or a third on others. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 73:20/q02/p8 Another view makes the pronoun refer to two thirds, making the prayer nearer to half or a third of that amount. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Another view makes the pronoun refer to two thirds, making the prayer nearer to half or a third of that amount. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 73:20/q02/p9 Al-Qushayrī suggests they could regularly manage a third or half and sometimes more, but the burden of two-thirds kept them from attaining it. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (القشيري): Al-Qushayrī suggests they could regularly manage a third or half and sometimes more, but the burden of two-thirds kept them from attaining it. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(القشيري)]
- 73:20/q02/p10 Al-Qushayrī also suggests they were commanded to pray half the night, with permission to increase toward two-thirds or decrease toward one-third. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (القشيري): Al-Qushayrī also suggests they were commanded to pray half the night, with permission to increase toward two-thirds or decrease toward one-third. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(القشيري)]
- 73:20/q02/p11 The closing explanation clarifies the earlier instruction: half matches نصفه, the decrease can reach one third, and increasing a little beyond half remains below two thirds. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The closing explanation clarifies the earlier instruction: half matches نصفه, the decrease can reach one third, and increasing a little beyond half remains below two thirds. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 73:20/q02/p12 The variant readings do not conflict because people in fact prayed differing portions, including near two thirds, half, and one third. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The variant readings do not conflict because people in fact prayed differing portions, including near two thirds, half, and one third. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 73:20/q02/p13 Slightly exceeding half leaves less than two-thirds for prayer, matching أدنى من ثلثي الليل; the earlier phrase نصفه أو انقص منه قليلا clarifies قم الليل إلا قليلا. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Slightly exceeding half leaves less than two-thirds for prayer, matching أدنى من ثلثي الليل; the earlier phrase نصفه أو انقص منه قليلا clarifies قم الليل إلا قليلا. | mentions 73:3 [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 73:20/q02/p14 On the general reading, the Prophet stood for less than two-thirds on some nights and less than the half and third on others. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): On the general reading, the Prophet stood for less than two-thirds on some nights and less than the half and third on others. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q02/p15 Both readings match the earlier choice to pray a full half, less than a half (a third), more than a half (two-thirds), or an even smaller portion (a quarter). — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Both readings match the earlier choice to pray a full half, less than a half (a third), more than a half (two-thirds), or an even smaller portion (a quarter). | mentions 73:2, 73:3 [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q02/p16 Qatada glosses the durations as less than two thirds, less than half, and less than a third of the night. — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (قتادة): Qatada glosses the durations as less than two thirds, less than half, and less than a third of the night. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(قتادة)]
- 73:20/q02/p17 On this construction the maximum is below two-thirds, the middle amount below half, and the minimum below one-third, about a quarter. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): On this construction the maximum is below two-thirds, the middle amount below half, and the minimum below one-third, about a quarter. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q02/p18 The reported vigil durations indicate a maximum slightly under two thirds of the night, a middle amount of one half, and a minimum of one third. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The reported vigil durations indicate a maximum slightly under two thirds of the night, a middle amount of one half, and a minimum of one third. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q02/p19 The waw is additive: on some nights the Prophet stands just under two-thirds, on others half, and on others a third. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The waw is additive: on some nights the Prophet stands just under two-thirds, on others half, and on others a third. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q02/p20 Verse 20 later describes practice as close to two-thirds of the night, half of it, or a third. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Verse 20 later describes practice as close to two-thirds of the night, half of it, or a third. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 73:20/q02/p21 A rare unrecited construal takes prayer as reaching one third but not less; al-Farra says the phrase can mean the third. — FARRA: A rare unrecited construal takes prayer as reaching one third but not less; al-Farra says the phrase can mean the third. [1 notes: FARRA]
- 73:20/q02/p22 Established reciters know interpretation better than hadith transmitters. — FARRA: Established reciters know interpretation better than hadith transmitters. [1 notes: FARRA]
- 73:20/q02/p23 This verse illustrates coordinated time expressions for prayer near two-thirds and half. — TAB-FULL: This verse illustrates coordinated time expressions for prayer near two-thirds and half. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 73:20/q02/p24 Tabari accepts both readings as sound and correct. — TAB-FULL: Tabari accepts both readings as sound and correct. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 73:20/q02/p25 A reading joins both fractions to mean less than half and less than a third. — ZAJJAJ: A reading joins both fractions to mean less than half and less than a third. [1 notes: ZAJJAJ(قارئ)]
- 73:20/q02/p26 Ibn Kathir reads the two words with a nominative ending omitted for heaviness. — NAHHAS: Ibn Kathir reads the two words with a nominative ending omitted for heaviness. [1 notes: NAHHAS(ابن كثير)]
- 73:20/q02/p27 Nahhas rejects Farra's explanation: if merely explanatory of less, the conjunction waw is unsuitable. — NAHHAS: Nahhas rejects Farra's explanation: if merely explanatory of less, the conjunction waw is unsuitable. [1 notes: NAHHAS]
- 73:20/q02/p28 Neither sound communal reading should outrank another; both derive from the Prophet. — NAHHAS: Neither sound communal reading should outrank another; both derive from the Prophet. [1 notes: NAHHAS]
- 73:20/q02/p29 Nahhas rejects an unrecited construction or hadith as proof against communal reading. — NAHHAS: Nahhas rejects an unrecited construction or hadith as proof against communal reading. [1 notes: NAHHAS]
- 73:20/q02/p30 Ibn Dhakwan reports heavy form for the third. — FARISI-HUJJA: Ibn Dhakwan reports heavy form for the third. [1 notes: FARISI-HUJJA(ابن ذكوان عن ابن عامر)]
- 73:20/q02/p31 Hisham reports light two-thirds and heavy third. — FARISI-HUJJA: Hisham reports light two-thirds and heavy third. [1 notes: FARISI-HUJJA(الحلواني عن هشام عن ابن عامر)]
- 73:20/q02/p32 Heavy form is supported by 4:11; Arabic permits light and heavy pairs. — FARISI-HUJJA: Heavy form is supported by 4:11; Arabic permits light and heavy pairs. [1 notes: FARISI-HUJJA]
- 73:20/q02/p33 Ibn Abbas says standing half and a third. — WAHIDI-BASIT: Ibn Abbas says standing half and a third. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن عباس)]
- 73:20/q02/p34 The Prophet prays and recites less than two-thirds, half, and one-third. — WAHIDI-WAJIZ: The Prophet prays and recites less than two-thirds, half, and one-third. [1 notes: WAHIDI-WAJIZ]
- 73:20/q02/p35 Prophet and community varied near two-thirds, half, and below a third; human sleep prevents accurate measurement. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Prophet and community varied near two-thirds, half, and below a third; human sleep prevents accurate measurement. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]

## 73:20/q03 Who is the group with the Prophet, and how is the phrase construed?  [وَطَآئِفَةٌۭ مِّنَ ٱلَّذِينَ مَعَكَ · referent]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q03/p1 The group consists of believers who followed the Prophet's vigil. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): The group accompanying the Prophet consists of the believers. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The group with the Prophet refers to his companions, who also rose for the stated portion of the night. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): A group of the Prophet's companions also rose for that amount of the night. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (سعيد بن جبير): Sa'id ibn Jubayr says the Prophet and a group of companions maintained the commanded vigil for ten years, until the final verse eased it. | mentions 73:20 IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): The Prophet and some companions varied between these portions without aiming at exact fractions, because maintaining the prescribed vigil was difficult. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Those with the Prophet are his companions in words and deeds, meaning Muslims; the broader phrase avoids implying that those who did not pray were not Muslims. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (البحر): Al-Baḥr argues that mentioning only a group with the Prophet shows the vigil was not obligatory for everyone, since universal obligation would call for wording about all his companions. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Because this exact vigil is beyond everyone, even those able to keep it, such as the Prophet and some Companions, suffer by adding extra time as a precaution. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبو حنيفة وأحمد في رواية): Abu Hanifa and one report from Ahmad hold that even a short single verse suffices and al-Fātiḥa is not specifically required; they cite 55:64 as an example, while Abu Hanifa’s two companions disagree on both points. | mentions 55:64 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (صاحبا أبي حنيفة): Abu Hanifa’s two companions disagree with him on both whether one short verse suffices and whether al-Fātiḥa is required. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The difficulty applies to the obligated group collectively, not every person: the Prophet and some companions could keep the portions, so others receive a concession while the original option remains. STUDYQURAN (author): After the first sentence, the address shifts from the Prophet alone to the Prophet and his Companions together. WAHIDI-BASIT: Ibn Abbas and Muqatil say Prophet's believing companions prayed half or third with him. BAGHAWI-FULL: Believers kept vigil with Prophet. KASHSHAF-FULL: Prophet's companions kept vigil. [14 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL(سعيد بن جبير); BIQAI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL(البحر); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL(أبو حنيفة وأحمد في رواية); TABATABAI; STUDYQURAN; WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن عباس ومقاتل); BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL(صاحبا أبي حنيفة)-]
- 73:20/q03/p2 The group includes ʿAlī and Abū Dharr. — TABRISI, d. 548 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās identifies the accompanying group as Abū ʿAlī and Abū Dharr. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas, in a report cited by al-Majma through al-Haskani, al-Kalbi, and Abu Salih, identifies the accompanying group as Ali and Abu Dharr. [2 notes: TABRISI(ابن عباس); TABATABAI(ابن عباس)]
- 73:20/q03/p3 “A group” is coordinated with the pronoun in “you rise”; the coordination needs no emphatic pronoun because intervening words separate them. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): “A group” is coordinated with the pronoun in “you rise”; the coordination needs no emphatic pronoun because intervening words separate them. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 73:20/q03/p4 The phrase وطائفة coordinates with the hidden subject of تقوم; its mention suggests the vigil was not obligatory for every individual, unless all prayed but some separately at home. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The phrase وطائفة coordinates with the hidden subject of تقوم; its mention suggests the vigil was not obligatory for every individual, unless all prayed but some separately at home. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 73:20/q03/p5 طائفة is coordinated with the pronoun in يقوم; the intervening adverb and its complement permit this coordination. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): طائفة is coordinated with the pronoun in يقوم; the intervening adverb and its complement permit this coordination. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]

## 73:20/q04 What does God's measuring of night and day convey?  [وَٱللَّهُ يُقَدِّرُ ٱلَّيْلَ وَٱلنَّهَارَ · interpretation]
- 73:20/q04/p1 God knows and determines the precise measures of night and day. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): God apportions night and day so people can act during them as commanded. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): God knows the lengths of night and day and therefore knows how much of the night people can pray. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Only God knows the exact measures of the divisions of night and day. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن العربي): Ibn al-ʿArabī says created measure itself carries no ruling; God connects to it whichever duties He wills. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): God knows the exact measures of night and day, whereas people estimate through effort and may be mistaken. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Only God knows the exact measures of night and day; placing God's name first signals exclusivity, supported by the statement that people cannot enumerate it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): On the genitive reading, prayer varied from night to night and people could not accurately measure the hours while sleeping; God alone knows them and eased the command to what was manageable. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī says putting God's name first in the sentence expresses His exclusive control of measuring night and day; Abū Ḥayyān says exclusivity comes from context, not subject-first word order alone. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari says placing Allah first as subject signals that He alone determines the night. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The genitive reading describes varying prayer amounts, each below two-thirds, half, or one-third as appropriate, since sleep and human limits prevent precise measurement. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): God measures the night and day so they sometimes match and sometimes one lengthens at the expense of the other. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): God alone measures night and day with exact precision and knows every minute because He created them; the verse recalls 67:14. | mentions 67:14 BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The fronted divine subject with the verb 'determines' makes God's exclusive ability to measure night and day emphatic. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (الراغب): Al-Raghib defines taqdīr as making the quantity of a thing clear. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): God alone knows the exact hours of night and day and can fulfill the right of worship in each; people can only estimate, and their effort may err. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Only God knows the precise durations and hours of night and day; placing His name as subject before yaquduru signals exclusivity, as Jar Allah argues. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): God alone measures and knows the true amount of night and day, and knows all time. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The interpretation that the pronoun refers to God's measure of the night, adopted by al-Kashshāf and many others, is sound in itself, but can wrongly suggest the initial command was ill-judged. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The parenthetical statement that God measures night and day fits here because He knows all the possible durations and has made them known; readings therefore do not alter the point. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The construction subtly marks the different status and measure of the Prophet’s vigil and the group’s; 9:3 likewise distinguishes God’s disavowal from the Messenger’s public dealings with polytheists. | mentions 9:3 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The opening statement prepares for the ensuing explanation that they cannot keep the vigil’s full measure. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): والله يقدر الليل والنهار is a parenthesis between God’s knowledge of the vigil and the statement that they cannot fully measure it; its relevance was explained above. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The pronoun in lan tuhsuhu may refer to measuring night or to standing for its prescribed thirds, half, or near two-thirds. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): God knows because He is the creator and determiner; determining night and day entails determining their thirds, halves, and two-thirds. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The verse names Allah in connection with measurement because determining measures is a matter of creation, and all creation belongs to Him. NAHHAS: God measures night and day hours. MAWARDI-FULL: God determines night and day hours for servants' acts or divine decree. WAHIDI-BASIT: God knows all night divisions and times. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: God measures night and day and their periods. DAMGHANI: Yaqduru can mean to know; God knows night and day hours. BAGHAWI-FULL: Ata says God misses no deed and knows night, day, and vigil. KASHSHAF-FULL: Only God measures night and day exactly; fronting name emphasizes exclusivity. [32 notes: TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(ابن العربي); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري); SAMIN-DURR(الزمخشري); IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI(الراغب); ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI; NAHHAS; MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT; WAHIDI-WAJIZ; DAMGHANI; BAGHAWI-FULL(عطاء); KASHSHAF-FULL]

## 73:20/q05 What can they not encompass or endure?  [عَلِمَ أَن لَّن تُحْصُوهُ · interpretation]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q05/p1 They cannot calculate the night portions exactly. — TABRISI, d. 548 (مقاتل): Muqātil says people prayed at night fearing they would fail to reach the commanded amount, so God says they cannot calculate it precisely. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Razi answers that people estimated a third by judgment and might fall slightly short of the exact divine measure, which explains the statement that they could not calculate it. | mentions 73:3 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The pronoun refers to an implied verbal noun: you cannot calculate each portion of night and day exactly, nor secure the measure by caution without great hardship. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masʿud reports that whoever patiently and devoutly brings goods to a Muslim city and sells at the day's price is counted by God among the martyrs. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): People cannot calculate the time intervals or keep precise count of the hours. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Only God can calculate the night and day and know their hours; placing His name first signals His exclusive power to measure them. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The pronoun in لن تحصوه most naturally refers to the measurement of night and day, though some take it to refer to the vigil itself. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet said, “Be upright, and you will not encompass it,” cited to illustrate human inability to calculate fully. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The pronoun in 'you will not calculate it' refers to the implied act of measuring; people cannot determine the times exactly or keep every hour precisely. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (تاج المصادر): Taj al-Masadir glosses iḥṣāʾ as knowing and counting exhaustively and being able to do so. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (عبد الله بن مسعود): Ibn Masʿud says that whoever patiently brings goods from a Muslim city and sells them at the day's market price for God's reward is counted among the martyrs. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Another explanation includes spending on one's household; a hadith says food a Muslim gives himself and his family counts as charity when done with good intention. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The pronoun refers to God's measuring: people cannot precisely calculate or track the hours, so they would have to take a broad precautionary interval, which is burdensome. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الشافعي ومالك): Another argument says omitting al-Fātiḥa without necessity forfeits an agreed extra merit and is therefore religiously blameworthy, based on the report that Muslims’ good and bad judgments count with God. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): One sense of not being able to calculate it is that people cannot precisely tally every night's hours and fractions, since day and night vary and sleep prevents discernment. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Variant readings are especially relevant to tafsir because one reading can clarify its counterpart or yield an additional meaning; different readings often expand a single verse’s meanings. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): أن and its clause fill both object positions of علم; the sense is that He knew their inability to keep count would in fact occur. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Ihsa means obtaining a measure or number and encompassing the thing. MUQATIL: People cannot calculate the exact first two-thirds, half, or third of the night. FARRA: They could not keep track of appointed times of night. TAB-FULL: Qatada takes the burden as believers' prescribed night vigil. NAHHAS: Farra says they could not keep count. MAWARDI-FULL: Dahhak says they cannot calculate half, third, or quarter. WAHIDI-BASIT: Muqatil says some prayed all night fearing error; an is reduced emphatic particle. JISHUMI: Ihsa means encompassing something by calculation. BAGHAWI-FULL: Muqatil says some prayed all night fearing wrong measure. KASHSHAF-FULL: Pronoun refers to God's measuring; people cannot track hours without burdensome precaution. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Farra and Mundhir gloss tuhsuhu as keeping count. [28 notes: TABRISI(مقاتل); RAZI-FULL(ابن مسعود); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); BURSEVI(تاج المصادر,عبد الله بن مسعود); ALUSI-FULL(الشافعي ومالك); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI; MUQATIL; FARRA; TAB-FULL(قتادة); NAHHAS(الفراء); MAWARDI-FULL(الضحاك); WAHIDI-BASIT(مقاتل); JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL(مقاتل); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(الفراء ومنذر)]
- 73:20/q05/p2 They cannot sustain the demanding night vigil. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (مقاتل): Muqatil says they could not endure praying two-thirds, one-third, or half of every night. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (بعضهم): Some cite لن تحصوه as evidence that people were commanded to do what they could not bear; al-Razi replies it means the task is difficult, not impossible. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الحسن): On the accusative reading, al-Hasan interprets تحصوه as تطيقوه, meaning to manage or bear it. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): God knew people could neither calculate the night's measure precisely nor sustain the prescribed practice. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الحسن وسعيد بن جبير): Al-Hasan and Saʿid ibn Jubayr explain that you would not be able to sustain or measure it. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): God eternally knew that believers could not all sustain the demanding vigil; the initial command was temporary preparation for the future, and the time had now come to ease it. | mentions 73:1 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The Prophet rose, along with a group of his Companions; this shows that not everyone rose, either because the duty was not obligatory for all or because some could not endure it. IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB: Lan tuhsuhu means unable to manage or endure it. TAB-FULL: Hasan says they could not bear night prayer. TAB-FULL: Sa'id says they could not sustain night prayer. TAB-FULL: Sufyan says they could not sustain night prayer. NAHHAS: Hasan and Sa'id say they could not endure vigil. MAWARDI-FULL: Hasan says worshippers cannot sustain vigil. WAHIDI-BASIT: People cannot know exact measures or sustain required vigil. BAGHAWI-FULL: Hasan says swollen feet show they could not endure or measure vigil. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Accusative fractions show people can estimate time, so inability means sustaining hard vigil. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Hasan and Ibn Jubayr gloss tuhsuhu as obeying it. [17 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(مقاتل); RAZI-FULL(بعضهم); SAMIN-DURR(الحسن); BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(الحسن وسعيد بن جبير); ELMALILI; IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB; TAB-FULL(الحسن,سعيد,سفيان); NAHHAS(الحسن، سعيد بن جبير); MAWARDI-FULL(الحسن); WAHIDI-BASIT; BAGHAWI-FULL(الحسن); IBNATIYYA-FULL(الحسن وابن جبير)]
- 73:20/q05/p3 Al-Raghib defines iḥṣāʾ as collection or attainment by number. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (الراغب): Al-Raghib defines iḥṣāʾ as collection or attainment by number. [1 notes: BURSEVI(الراغب)]
- 73:20/q05/p4 The object pronoun in تحصوه refers to the night standing understood from أنك تقوم. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The object pronoun in تحصوه refers to the night standing understood from أنك تقوم. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]

## 73:20/q06 What does God's turning toward them mean?  [فَتَابَ عَلَيْكُمْ · interpretation]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q06/p1 God graciously eased or lifted the measured vigil burden. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Alternatively, the phrase means no liability is imposed, or that God eased the burden. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿīd ibn Jubayr says the Prophet and Companions prayed at night for ten years before verse 20 came down and eased the burden. | mentions 73:20 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Here أن is the lightened form of أنَّ, meaning “He knew that it would happen.” QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The particle an is a lightened form of the emphatic particle; exact calculation was beyond them, since exceeding the measure would burden them and falling short would distress them. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Since tawba means return, the phrase describes a return from heavy obligation to relief and from hardship to ease. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): “An” is lightened from the emphatic particle, the sīn replaces its shadda, and its subject is omitted. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الحسن وابن جبير): Al-Hasan and Ibn Jubayr take لن تحصوه to mean you cannot sustain it; God eased the burden out of mercy because they could not endure the extensive vigil, not because He lacked knowledge of its measure. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): A ṭāʾifah is a group whose members are suited to gathering around the Prophet and turning toward one another. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Tāba ʿalaykum is a metaphorical extension from accepting repentance to granting a dispensation; tabʿah means the harm that follows an act. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): God's turning toward them means He permits them to leave the prescribed vigil and lifts its burden; this is a metaphor comparing permission to acceptance of repentance. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (سعيد): Sa‘id reports that the Prophet and a group of companions continued the commanded vigil for ten years, until this passage was revealed and lightened the duty. | mentions 73:2 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): تاب is metaphorical for withholding blame before a lapse occurs: anticipated shortfall resembles an actual sin, so language of repentance describes the lifting of liability. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Here God's tawba toward people means His merciful turning to them through easing the burden. ISLAHI-TADABBUR (author): With the verb tāba, the preposition ʿalā gives the sense “to turn to someone graciously.” MUQATIL: God eased the earlier command to rise most of the night. IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL: God knew they could neither calculate nor sustain the vigil, so allowed feasible night prayer without fixed duration. TAB-FULL: God knew worshippers could not sustain vigil and eased the obligation. NAHHAS: God turned them toward ease; tawba means returning. MAWARDI-FULL: God lightened the obligation. WAHIDI-BASIT: Ibn Abbas says God excused uncomprehended amounts. WAHIDI-BASIT: Muqatil says God passed over them by easing duty. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: God eased a vigil people could not sustain. KASHSHAF-FULL: God licensed leaving vigil and removed liability, as 2:187. IBNATIYYA-FULL: God turned them from burden to feasible recitation. [24 notes: TABRISI; QURTUBI-FULL(سعيد بن جبير); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الحسن وابن جبير); BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI(سعيد); IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI; ISLAHI-TADABBUR; MUQATIL; IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL; TAB-FULL; NAHHAS; MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن عباس,مقاتل); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 73:20/q06/p2 God pardoned their prior shortfall. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Fatā ʿalaykum can mean God pardoned them, implying they had left some of what was commanded; it can also mean He turned from imposing the prayer when they could not bear it. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The wording recalls 2:187, which similarly says God turned toward them and pardoned them. | mentions 2:187 MAWARDI-FULL: God pardoned earlier shortcomings and directed feasible recitation. WAHIDI-BASIT: God turned to them with pardon and relief. BAGHAWI-FULL: God returned with pardon and relief. [5 notes: QURTUBI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT; BAGHAWI-FULL]

## 73:20/q07 What does the command to recite what is easy require?  [فَٱقْرَءُوا۟ مَا تَيَسَّرَ مِنَ ٱلْقُرْءَانِ · interpretation]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q07/p1 One hundred verses are the manageable amount. — TABRISI, d. 548 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās says the manageable amount is one hundred verses. TABRISI, d. 548 (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan says whoever reads one hundred verses in a night will not have the Qurʾan litigate against him. TABRISI, d. 548 (كعب): Kaʿb says reading one hundred verses in a night counts a person among the devout. TABRISI, d. 548 (السدي): Al-Suddī says the amount is two hundred verses. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (السدي): One view takes the command literally as Qurʾān recitation during night prayer; al-Suddī specifies one hundred verses. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan says someone who recites one hundred verses in a night will not have the Qurʾān plead against him. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (كعب): Kaʿb says one who recites one hundred verses at night is recorded among the devout; al-Qurṭubī prefers this report’s view. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي): A report from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr says ten verses keep a worshipper from being counted heedless, one hundred make him devout, and one thousand make him among the muqaṇṭarīn; al-Qurṭubī cites it to support Kaʿb’s hundred-verse view. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the amount is one hundred verses. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (السدي): Al-Suddi says the amount is two hundred verses. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (أبو هريرة): Abū Hurayra reports that reciting one hundred verses at night keeps one from being counted among the heedless, and two hundred makes one among the devout. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (ابن عباس): A report attributed to the Prophet through Ibn Abbas interprets the manageable recitation as one hundred verses; Ibn Kathir later calls this report very strange and says he found it only in al-Tabarani’s collection. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص): The Prophet said that praying with ten verses removes heedlessness, one hundred marks devotion, and one thousand brings the rank of the richly rewarded; Abu Dawud, Ibn Khuzaymah, and Ibn Hibban transmit it. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): A report from the Prophet glosses what is easy to recite as one hundred verses. ITQAN, d. 911 (النبي): Al-Ṭabarānī reports from Ibn ʿAbbās that the Prophet interpreted “recite what is easy of it” as one hundred verses; Ibn Kathīr calls the report very strange. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): One report promises a special merit for reciting one hundred verses in a night; the quoted reward wording is unclear in the source text. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (النبي): Ibn Abi Hatim, al-Tabarani, and Ibn Mardawayh report from Ibn Abbas that the Prophet explained what is easy as one hundred verses. STUDYQURAN (some): Some held that those who know the Quran should still recite at least fifty or one hundred verses. FARRA: The manageable recitation is one hundred verses or more. TAB-FULL: Suddi says the amount is one hundred verses. TAB-FULL: Hasan says one hundred nightly verses keep the Quran from arguing against the reader. TAB-FULL: Ka'b says one hundred verses count a person among worshippers. IBNABIHATIM: Ibn Abbas reports the Prophet explained feasible recitation as one hundred verses. MAWARDI-FULL: Suddi sets two hundred verses. MAWARDI-FULL: Ibn Abbas sets one hundred verses. WAHIDI-BASIT: Hasan and Suddi identify one hundred verses. KASHSHAF-FULL: Alternative literal recitation of fifty or one hundred verses, with hundred-verse merit reports. [27 notes: TABRISI(ابن عباس,الحسن,السدي,كعب); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عباس,الحسن,السدي,النبي); NASAFI-FULL(أبو هريرة); IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن عباس); BIQAI-FULL(عبد الله بن عمرو بن العاص); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); ITQAN(النبي); ALUSI-FULL; TABATABAI(النبي); STUDYQURAN(some); FARRA; TAB-FULL(الحسن,السدي,كعب); IBNABIHATIM(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس,السدي); WAHIDI-BASIT(الحسن والسدي); KASHSHAF-FULL(قيل); QURTUBI-FULL(كعب)+]
- 73:20/q07/p2 Recitation stands for manageable night prayer. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): One view takes recitation to mean prayer, naming the whole by one of its parts, since Quranic recitation is part of prayer. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Al-Qurṭubī prefers actual recitation because it follows the apparent wording; taking it as prayer is figurative, naming the whole act by one of its parts. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The phrase means people cannot know the exact measure or fulfill it; an alternative says they cannot endure night prayer, but the first is stronger because the whole night was never imposed. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): It may mean perform whatever night prayer is easy, with Qurʾān naming prayer by one of its pillars; another view says the phrase means recite the Qurʾān itself as easily as possible. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (قيل): Some interpret “the Qurʾān” here as prayer, because recitation is one of its pillars; the command is to pray what night prayer is manageable. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Reciting what is easy can stand for prayer because recitation is one of its pillars, like standing, bowing, and prostration; it means pray at night as much as manageable. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): If this means recitation within prayer, the apparent rule is that any recitation that is easy and manageable suffices. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): God knew the believers could not keep exact count of the required night portion, so He eased the duty and allowed whatever recitation is manageable, meaning night prayer without a fixed duration. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Recitation here stands for prayer, as in 17:110, where not raising one’s voice in prayer means not raising it in recitation. | mentions 17:110 BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The phrase means perform whatever night prayer is easy, without a fixed fraction of the night; even a brief amount counts, perhaps four or two rakʿahs. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Recitation names the whole prayer by one of its parts, a metonymic use of part for whole. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): On the contextual reading, recitation stands for night prayer through one of its constituent acts; literal Qur'an recitation is less fitting to context. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (أبو حنيفة): Abū Ḥanīfa infers that any Qurʾānic recitation fulfills the prayer requirement, since recitation can stand for prayer; the minimum is reported as one verse by al-Māwardī or a third by Ibn al-ʿArabī. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Müfessirînden birçokları): Many exegetes take recitation as a part-for-whole reference to prayer, since recitation is one of its pillars, and understand the command as praying at night as much as is easy. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): He preserves the literal sense of Qur'an as recitation while retaining prayer: the earlier command means rise for prayer, and here the easy Quranic portion is read within that prayer; the easing is conveyed by context. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): If it means Quran recitation alone, then some Quran must be recited every night, whether during prayer or outside it. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): If معك means figurative association, fellowship, and agreement rather than physical attendance at night prayer, the group includes several Companions and Zaynab bint Jaḥsh, whose night-prayer reports occur separately in Sahih al-Bukhari. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): فاقرؤوا ما تيسر من القرآن means perform as much prayer as is manageable, since prayer necessarily includes recitation. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (müfessirler): One interpretation takes “recite what is easy” to mean praying as much tahajjud as is easy during the night. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Ebû Bekir İbnü’l-Arabi): Ibn al-Arabi reports both that “recitation” stands for prayer and that the passage means recitation itself. MAWARDI-FULL: Prayer is called Quran because it contains recitation. [21 notes: RAZI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL(قيل); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(أبو حنيفة); ELMALILI(Müfessirînden birçokları); IBNASHUR-FULL; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Ebû Bekir İbnü’l-Arabi,müfessirler); MAWARDI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL+; ALUSI-FULL+; ELMALILI+; QURTUBI-FULL+]
- 73:20/q07/p3 Fifty verses are the manageable amount. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (قيل): Another view sets the amount at fifty verses. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (سعيد): Saʿīd says the amount is fifty verses. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (ابن جبير وجماعة): Ibn Jubayr and a group regard it as obligatory, requiring at least fifty verses. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A further report specifies fifty verses as a measure of nightly recitation. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Diğer bir kısım müfessirîn): Another group takes the imperative as ordinary Quran recitation, not night prayer, and says at least fifty verses should be read each night. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Mutlak kıraat mânasına hamledenler): Exegetes who take this as unrestricted recitation appeal to customary practice and the predecessors' habit to set a minimum of fifty verses. TAB-FULL: Hasan condemns a memorizer neglecting night recitation and says fifty verses suffice. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Ibn Jubayr's group requires fifty verses. TABRISI: Sa'id ibn Jubayr says manageable amount is fifty verses. [9 notes: RAZI-FULL(قيل); QURTUBI-FULL(سعيد); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن جبير وجماعة); ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI(Diğer bir kısım müfessirîn,Mutlak kıraat mânasına hamledenler); TAB-FULL(الحسن); IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن جبير وجماعة); TABRISI(سعيد بن جبير)]
- 73:20/q07/p4 Recite a manageable portion of Qur'an in prayer. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): The instruction is to recite whatever is manageable in prayer, without setting a fixed duration. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (İmâm-ı A‘zam Ebû Hanîfe): One report from Abu Hanifa sets the minimum obligatory recitation in prayer at one short verse. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The phrase concerns as much night prayer as manageable, not the minimum Quranic recitation required in prayer; even if read as recitation, the amount is unspecified here and clarified by the hadith requiring al-Fātiḥa. STUDYQURAN (author): God relented by reducing the vigil requirement and letting them recite as much Quran in prayer as was easy. MAWARDI-FULL: Recitation in prayer is obligatory. MAWARDI-FULL: Malik and Shafi'i require full Opening Sura in prayer. WAHIDI-BASIT: Muqatil says recite manageable Quran in prayer without a fixed amount. BAGHAWI-FULL: Recite manageable Quran in prayer; Hasan specifies Maghrib and Isha. [8 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD; ELMALILI(İmâm-ı A‘zam Ebû Hanîfe); IBNASHUR-FULL; STUDYQURAN; MAWARDI-FULL(مالك والشافعي); WAHIDI-BASIT(مقاتل); BAGHAWI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p5 The Fātiḥa is required in prayer. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (مالك والشافعي): Malik and al-Shafi'i require reciting the Fatiha in prayer; neither leaving it for another passage nor reciting only part suffices. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The author endorses Malik and al-Shafi'i's requirement of reciting the Fatiha in prayer. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (مالك والشافعي): Malik and al-Shafi'i identify required recitation as the Fatiha, which cannot be replaced or reduced to only part of it. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (مالك والشافعي وأحمد بن حنبل): Malik, al-Shafii, Ahmad, their followers, and most scholars require al-Fatiha specifically and consider prayer without it invalid, citing reports that the prayer is deficient or invalid without it. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (الشافعي): Al-Shafii and some scholars require al-Fatiha in every rakah. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (آخرون): Other scholars say al-Fatiha is required in most rakahs rather than every rakah. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (الحسن وأكثر البصريين): Al-Hasan and most Basrans hold that al-Fatiha is required in only one rakah, relying on the unrestricted hadith wording. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (أحدها): The first view makes al-Fatiha obligatory for the follower just as it is for the imam, relying on the general hadiths already cited. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (İmam Mâlik ve Şâfiî): Malik and al-Shafi‘i require at least al-Fatiha, citing the prophetic report that there is no prayer without the Opening of the Book. JISHUMI: Teachers say solitary report cannot fix Fatiha against Quran's apparent wording. BAGHAWI-FULL: Ibn Abbas read Fatiha and one Baqara verse per rak'a and cited this verse. [11 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(مالك والشافعي); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(مالك والشافعي); IBNKATHIR-FULL(آخرون,أحدها,الحسن وأكثر البصريين,الشافعي,مالك والشافعي وأحمد بن حنبل); ELMALILI(İmam Mâlik ve Şâfiî); JISHUMI(بعض مشايخنا); BAGHAWI-FULL(قيس بن أبي حازم); QURTUBI-FULL+]
- 73:20/q07/p6 The command concerns Qur'an recitation outside prayer. — TABRISI, d. 548 (أكثر المفسرين): Most exegetes treat recitation outside prayer as recommended, not obligatory, since obligation would entail memorization. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الماوردي): If this means Quran recitation outside prayer, al-Mawardi says the command may mean obligation or recommendation; most recommend it because a duty to recite would entail a duty to memorize. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Reciting what is easy in prayer is obligatory, while reciting it outside prayer is recommended. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Recite whatever is easy from the Qur'an, in or outside prayer and by night or day. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (القوم): Commentators disagree whether the original night command meant prayer or Quran recitation outside prayer. [5 notes: TABRISI(أكثر المفسرين); QURTUBI-FULL(الماوردي); NASAFI-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; TABATABAI(القوم)]
- 73:20/q07/p7 The command concerns literal Qur'an recitation. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (قيل): Another view takes the command as literal Quran recitation, not a metonym for prayer. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): إحصاء literally means counting, from حصى because pebbles were used to count; here it metaphorically means being able to sustain the many acts of bowing, prostration, and recitation. ISLAHI-TADABBUR (author): The general instruction to recite as much as possible refers here to Qur’an recitation in tahajjud; measured recitation in that prayer brings special blessings, though recitation itself is rewarding. [3 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل); IBNASHUR-FULL; ISLAHI-TADABBUR]
- 73:20/q07/p8 Three verses are sufficient. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو خالد الكناني): Abu Khalid al-Kinani says the amount is three verses, the length of the shortest surah. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa requires at least one verse from anywhere in the Quran; another report from him sets the minimum at three verses, the shortest surah's length. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (İmâm-ı A‘zam Ebû Hanîfe): A third report requires at least three short verses or one long verse; this is also the two disciples' position and the relied-upon fatwa. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (İmameyn): The view requiring three short verses or one long verse is also held by the two disciples and is the position used for fatwa. MAWARDI-FULL: Abu Khalid sets three verses, the shortest sura. [5 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو حنيفة,أبو خالد الكناني); ELMALILI(İmâm-ı A‘zam Ebû Hanîfe); MAWARDI-FULL(أبو خالد الكناني); ELMALILI(İmameyn)+]
- 73:20/q07/p9 Five verses are sufficient. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (القشيري): Al-Qushayri says the easy recitation may be five verses or more. STUDYQURAN (IK): Another view lowers the minimum recitation for those who know the Quran to five verses. QUSHAYRI: Manageable recitation may be five verses or more or ten or more. [3 notes: BIQAI-FULL(القشيري); STUDYQURAN(IK); QUSHAYRI]
- 73:20/q07/p10 A third of the Qur'an is the manageable amount. — TABRISI, d. 548 (جويبر): Juwaybir says it is a third of the Qurʾan, since God made recitation easy for His servants. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (جوبير): Juwair reports that the required amount is one third of the Quran. [2 notes: TABRISI(جويبر); QURTUBI-FULL(جوبير)]
- 73:20/q07/p11 Any Qur'anic passage suffices in prayer. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa and his adherents hold that al-Fatiha is not specifically required in prayer; any Quran recitation suffices, based on the general wording of this verse. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (أبو حنيفة وأصحابه والثوري والأوزاعي): Abu Hanifa, his adherents, al-Thawri, and al-Awzai hold that al-Fatiha is not specifically required and another passage suffices, citing this verse. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (أصحاب الإمام أبي حنيفة): Hanafi jurists use this verse and the report telling a poor-performing worshipper to read what is easy to argue that al-Fatiha is not required and even one verse suffices. JISHUMI: Any Quranic passage suffices in prayer; Iraqi jurists reject fixing Fatiha. [4 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(أبو حنيفة,أبو حنيفة وأصحابه والثوري والأوزاعي,أصحاب الإمام أبي حنيفة); JISHUMI]
- 73:20/q07/p12 Ten verses are sufficient. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (قيل): An unnamed view sets the easy minimum at ten verses or more. FARRA: A report says ten verses prevent one from being counted heedless. [2 notes: BIQAI-FULL(قيل); FARRA]
- 73:20/q07/p13 The apparent meaning is an amount of recitation that a person wants and finds agreeable. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): The apparent meaning is an amount of recitation that a person wants and finds agreeable. [1 notes: TABRISI+]
- 73:20/q07/p14 Another explanation is that they could not sustain night prayer consistently and would fall short. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Another explanation is that they could not sustain night prayer consistently and would fall short. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 73:20/q07/p15 Most exegetes take the command as reciting whatever is manageable in night prayer. — TABRISI, d. 548 (أكثر المفسرين): Most exegetes take the command as reciting whatever is manageable in night prayer. [1 notes: TABRISI(أكثر المفسرين)]
- 73:20/q07/p16 Some say to pray whatever is manageable, calling prayer Qurʾan because it includes recitation. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Some say to pray whatever is manageable, calling prayer Qurʾan because it includes recitation. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 73:20/q07/p17 Some hold recitation obligatory because it reveals the Qurʾan's inimitability and proofs of monotheism and prophethood, while memorization remains recommended. — TABRISI, d. 548 (بعضهم): Some hold recitation obligatory because it reveals the Qurʾan's inimitability and proofs of monotheism and prophethood, while memorization remains recommended. [1 notes: TABRISI(بعضهم)]
- 73:20/q07/p18 Hasan says this refers to the recitation in the Maghrib and Isha prayers. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (الحسن): Hasan says this refers to the recitation in the Maghrib and Isha prayers. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الحسن)]
- 73:20/q07/p19 Fatā ʿalaykum means God licensed them to leave the measured night vigil, like His pardon in 2:187. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Fatā ʿalaykum means God licensed them to leave the measured night vigil, like His pardon in 2:187. | mentions 2:187 [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p20 Others consider a short surah sufficient because night vigil was reduced to remove hardship, and lengthy recitation would recreate hardship. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (آخرون): Others consider a short surah sufficient because night vigil was reduced to remove hardship, and lengthy recitation would recreate hardship. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(آخرون)]
- 73:20/q07/p21 In Samura ibn Jundub's vision report, a man whose head was crushed had taken the Quran and rejected it and slept through an obligatory prayer. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): In Samura ibn Jundub's vision report, a man whose head was crushed had taken the Quran and rejected it and slept through an obligatory prayer. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q07/p22 Ibn Umar thereafter slept little; the author says that if missing night prayer were sinful, the angel would not have reassured him. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Ibn Umar thereafter slept little; the author says that if missing night prayer were sinful, the angel would not have reassured him. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p23 Another view makes Quran reading obligatory so a person encounters its inimitability, proofs of divine unity, and evidence for the messengers; memorization remains recommended, not obligatory. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (قوم): Another view makes Quran reading obligatory so a person encounters its inimitability, proofs of divine unity, and evidence for the messengers; memorization remains recommended, not obligatory. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(قوم)]
- 73:20/q07/p24 The whole Qur'an is meant. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak says the amount meant is the whole Quran, which God has made easy for His servants. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الضحاك)]
- 73:20/q07/p25 A group of the Prophet's companions also performed the night vigil. — BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): A group of the Prophet's companions also performed the night vigil. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p26 God grants concession to leave the prescribed amount of night vigil and lifts the liability for doing so. — BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): God grants concession to leave the prescribed amount of night vigil and lifts the liability for doing so. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p27 The passage indicates that some had failed to fulfill part of the command; another gloss describes a shift from burden to ease and permission to leave the prescribed night vigil. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The passage indicates that some had failed to fulfill part of the command; another gloss describes a shift from burden to ease and permission to leave the prescribed night vigil. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p28 The majority regard the command to recite what is easy as permissive. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الجمهور): The majority regard the command to recite what is easy as permissive. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الجمهور)]
- 73:20/q07/p29 Abu Hanifa’s side also cites the Prophet’s instruction to a man who prayed badly to recite whatever Quran was easy, without naming al-Fatiha. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa’s side also cites the Prophet’s instruction to a man who prayed badly to recite whatever Quran was easy, without naming al-Fatiha. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(أبو حنيفة)]
- 73:20/q07/p30 Ibn Kathir explains khidaj in the report about omitting al-Fatiha as deficient, meaning not complete. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir explains khidaj in the report about omitting al-Fatiha as deficient, meaning not complete. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p31 Reports from Ubada ibn al-Samit and Abu Hurayra say there is no prayer without al-Fatiha or the Mother of the Quran; Ibn Kathir notes many reports on the issue. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Reports from Ubada ibn al-Samit and Abu Hurayra say there is no prayer without al-Fatiha or the Mother of the Quran; Ibn Kathir notes many reports on the issue. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q07/p32 Ibn Kathir sets out three views on whether a person praying behind an imam must recite al-Fatiha. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir sets out three views on whether a person praying behind an imam must recite al-Fatiha. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p33 A report attributed to the Prophet says that when someone has an imam, the imam’s recitation is his recitation; Ibn Kathir calls its chain weak and notes Malik reports it as Jabir’s own statement. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (جابر بن عبد الله): A report attributed to the Prophet says that when someone has an imam, the imam’s recitation is his recitation; Ibn Kathir calls its chain weak and notes Malik reports it as Jabir’s own statement. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(جابر بن عبد الله)]
- 73:20/q07/p34 The third view requires the follower to recite in quiet prayers but not audible ones; Ibn Kathir says the evidence supports this position, an old view of al-Shafii and a report from Ahmad. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (القول الثالث): The third view requires the follower to recite in quiet prayers but not audible ones; Ibn Kathir says the evidence supports this position, an old view of al-Shafii and a report from Ahmad. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(القول الثالث)+]
- 73:20/q07/p35 Reports from Abu Musa and Abu Hurayra instruct worshippers to listen silently when the imam recites; Muslim authenticated the report and Ibn Kathir uses it to support the third view. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Reports from Abu Musa and Abu Hurayra instruct worshippers to listen silently when the imam recites; Muslim authenticated the report and Ibn Kathir uses it to support the third view. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q07/p36 Ibn Kathir raises these disputes to show that al-Fatiha has rulings not shared by the other suras. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir raises these disputes to show that al-Fatiha has rulings not shared by the other suras. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p37 Umar reports hearing Hisham recite al-Furqan in unfamiliar forms; each said the Prophet taught him, and the Prophet validated both recitations as revealed, declaring the Quran came in seven ahruf and to recite what is easy. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عمر): Umar reports hearing Hisham recite al-Furqan in unfamiliar forms; each said the Prophet taught him, and the Prophet validated both recitations as revealed, declaring the Quran came in seven ahruf and to recite what is easy. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(عمر)]
- 73:20/q07/p38 Two hadiths urge the people of Qur'an to pray witr, one saying to do so and another warning that whoever omits witr is not of the community. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Two hadiths urge the people of Qur'an to pray witr, one saying to do so and another warning that whoever omits witr is not of the community. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p39 Qays ibn Abi Hazim reports praying behind Ibn Abbas, who recited al-Fatihah and one verse of al-Baqarah in each of the first two units. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (قيس بن أبي حازم): Qays ibn Abi Hazim reports praying behind Ibn Abbas, who recited al-Fatihah and one verse of al-Baqarah in each of the first two units. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(قيس بن أبي حازم)]
- 73:20/q07/p40 Al-Mundhiri says the thousand verses begin at Surat al-Mulk and continue to the end of the Qur'an. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (المنذري): Al-Mundhiri says the thousand verses begin at Surat al-Mulk and continue to the end of the Qur'an. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(المنذري)]
- 73:20/q07/p41 These additional excuses explain why God repeats the permission: each person should recite whatever is easy from the same Qur'an. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): These additional excuses explain why God repeats the permission: each person should recite whatever is easy from the same Qur'an. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p42 Mujahid says the command to recite what is easy eased night prayer; inability to reckon it refers to sustaining the night vigil, and God then disclosed believers' qualities. — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (مجاهد): Mujahid says the command to recite what is easy eased night prayer; inability to reckon it refers to sustaining the night vigil, and God then disclosed believers' qualities. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(مجاهد)]
- 73:20/q07/p43 Qays recounts Ibn Abbas reciting al-Fatiha and the opening verse of al-Baqara in one rakʿa, then citing this verse as authority for the amount recited. — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (قيس بن أبي حازم): Qays recounts Ibn Abbas reciting al-Fatiha and the opening verse of al-Baqara in one rakʿa, then citing this verse as authority for the amount recited. | mentions 2:1 [1 notes: DURR-FULL(قيس بن أبي حازم)]
- 73:20/q07/p44 Abu Saʿid reports that the Prophet ordered them to recite al-Fatiha and whatever else was easy. — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو سعيد): Abu Saʿid reports that the Prophet ordered them to recite al-Fatiha and whatever else was easy. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(أبو سعيد)]
- 73:20/q07/p45 Ṭāʾifatun is nominative, coordinated with the subject implied in taqūmu; min may be explanatory, so the phrase does not prove that night prayer was not prescribed for everyone. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Ṭāʾifatun is nominative, coordinated with the subject implied in taqūmu; min may be explanatory, so the phrase does not prove that night prayer was not prescribed for everyone. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q07/p46 A group of the Prophet's Companions joins him in night prayer; this wording promises them favor, and Qut al-qulub notes their inclusion with him in gratitude and good reward. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): A group of the Prophet's Companions joins him in night prayer; this wording promises them favor, and Qut al-qulub notes their inclusion with him in gratitude and good reward. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q07/p47 God eased the rule by permitting people to leave the prescribed measure of night prayer and lifting the resulting liability. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): God eased the rule by permitting people to leave the prescribed measure of night prayer and lifting the resulting liability. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q07/p48 Given the concessions and competing demands, believers should recite as much Quran as is manageable without imposing hardship. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Given the concessions and competing demands, believers should recite as much Quran as is manageable without imposing hardship. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q07/p49 The Companions were burdened by keeping to the prescribed measure of night prayer, not by night worship itself; it may also have felt heavy before the concession. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The Companions were burdened by keeping to the prescribed measure of night prayer, not by night worship itself; it may also have felt heavy before the concession. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q07/p50 notes that Uthman and Tamim al-Dari each completed the Quran in one rakah. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (عثمان وتميم الداري): The author notes that Uthman and Tamim al-Dari each completed the Quran in one rakah. [1 notes: BURSEVI(عثمان وتميم الداري)]
- 73:20/q07/p51 In a report through Saʿd ibn Hisham, ʿAʾisha says the Prophet's character was the Qur'an; vigil was made obligatory at the sūra's opening, observed for a year by him and his Companions, then eased at its… — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (عائشة): In a report through Saʿd ibn Hisham, ʿAʾisha says the Prophet's character was the Qur'an; vigil was made obligatory at the sūra's opening, observed for a year by him and his Companions, then eased at its end and became voluntary. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(عائشة)]
- 73:20/q07/p52 Another report says that whoever recites one verse at night is counted among the devoutly obedient. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Another report says that whoever recites one verse at night is counted among the devoutly obedient. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p53 Abū ʿUthmān al-Nahdī reports from Abū Hurayra that the Prophet sent him to announce that prayer requires recitation, even if only al-Fātiḥa; another report from Abū Hurayra says prayer requires al-Fātiḥa. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (رسول الله صلى الله تعالى عليه وسلم): Abū ʿUthmān al-Nahdī reports from Abū Hurayra that the Prophet sent him to announce that prayer requires recitation, even if only al-Fātiḥa; another report from Abū Hurayra says prayer requires al-Fātiḥa. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله تعالى عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q07/p54 The opposing logical proof says that if recitation is obligatory but al-Fātiḥa is not, general recitation would still be obligatory, forcing the conclusion that al-Fātiḥa is obligatory. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The opposing logical proof says that if recitation is obligatory but al-Fātiḥa is not, general recitation would still be obligatory, forcing the conclusion that al-Fātiḥa is obligatory. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p55 The reply denies that non-obligation of al-Fātiḥa entails general recitation: its absence is impossible, and an impossible premise can entail an impossible result; a further response disputes the alleged entailment itself. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The reply denies that non-obligation of al-Fātiḥa entails general recitation: its absence is impossible, and an impossible premise can entail an impossible result; a further response disputes the alleged entailment itself. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p56 Given these hardships and the circumstances warranting relief, believers should recite whatever Qurʾān is manageable without imposing hardship. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Given these hardships and the circumstances warranting relief, believers should recite whatever Qurʾān is manageable without imposing hardship. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p57 Ednâ means nearer or less; here it describes the amount of night vigil as less than the stated fractions. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Ednâ means nearer or less; here it describes the amount of night vigil as less than the stated fractions. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q07/p58 Al-Tabari refers the pronoun to the night vigil itself: in the future, none of you will manage to complete it fully; the author finds this simpler and smoother. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Taberî): Al-Tabari refers the pronoun to the night vigil itself: in the future, none of you will manage to complete it fully; the author finds this simpler and smoother. [1 notes: ELMALILI(Taberî)+]
- 73:20/q07/p59 The easing does not abolish night vigil or recitation: rise and recite whatever is easy, without a fixed fraction of the night or a requirement of prolonged, measured recitation. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The easing does not abolish night vigil or recitation: rise and recite whatever is easy, without a fixed fraction of the night or a requirement of prolonged, measured recitation. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q07/p60 Given the many legitimate needs that prevent night vigil, believers are told to recite whatever is easy from the Quran. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Given the many legitimate needs that prevent night vigil, believers are told to recite whatever is easy from the Quran. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q07/p61 By agreement, min in from the Quran is explanatory: the easy amount is a portion still validly called Quran, such as a sura or one or several verses. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): By agreement, min in from the Quran is explanatory: the easy amount is a portion still validly called Quran, such as a sura or one or several verses. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q07/p62 A report from Ibn Masud says the Prophet promised a station with God to anyone who brings food to a Muslim town and sells it at that day's price; he then recited these verses. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Resûlullah (İbn Mes‘ûd rivayeti)): A report from Ibn Masud says the Prophet promised a station with God to anyone who brings food to a Muslim town and sells it at that day's price; he then recited these verses. [1 notes: ELMALILI(Resûlullah (İbn Mes‘ûd rivayeti))]
- 73:20/q07/p63 An exegete should explain the mutawatir readings because their differences usually expand the verse’s meanings, with multiple readings functioning like multiple Quranic words. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): An exegete should explain the mutawatir readings because their differences usually expand the verse’s meanings, with multiple readings functioning like multiple Quranic words. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p64 Alternatively, Quranic wording may intentionally accommodate several readings, each adding meanings like multiple verses; he compares this to inclusion, double entendre, and implications of composition. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Alternatively, Quranic wording may intentionally accommodate several readings, each adding meanings like multiple verses; he compares this to inclusion, double entendre, and implications of composition. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p65 The inconsistent narratives may have confused the initial imposition of night vigil with later encouragement to perform it. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The inconsistent narratives may have confused the initial imposition of night vigil with later encouragement to perform it. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p66 The emphatic إن marks the statement as important and indirectly expresses that God approves the Prophet’s night vigil, preparing for the later easing as mercy and honor. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The emphatic إن marks the statement as important and indirectly expresses that God approves the Prophet’s night vigil, preparing for the later easing as mercy and honor. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p67 Al-Hasan and Sufyan explain إحصاء here as ability to manage the many actions of night prayer. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الحسن وسفيان): Al-Hasan and Sufyan explain إحصاء here as ability to manage the many actions of night prayer. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(الحسن وسفيان)]
- 73:20/q07/p68 The verse’s opening praises the Prophet for faithfully fulfilling the night vigil and praises the group who followed him. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The verse’s opening praises the Prophet for faithfully fulfilling the night vigil and praises the group who followed him. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p69 Calling prayer Qurʾān, as in 17:78’s قرآن الفجر for the dawn prayer, concisely encourages both night worship and recitation. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Calling prayer Qurʾān, as in 17:78’s قرآن الفجر for the dawn prayer, concisely encourages both night worship and recitation. | mentions 17:78 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p70 The cited Prophetic hadith states that prayer is not valid for one who does not recite the Opening of the Book. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The cited Prophetic hadith states that prayer is not valid for one who does not recite the Opening of the Book. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q07/p71 A report in the Ṣaḥīḥ says the Prophet became hoarse while reciting and bowed in the middle of a surah, illustrating an exception for night prayer or need. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report in the Ṣaḥīḥ says the Prophet became hoarse while reciting and bowed in the middle of a surah, illustrating an exception for night prayer or need. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q07/p72 The detailed rules specifying when an imam, follower, or person praying alone must recite are treated in legal manuals. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The detailed rules specifying when an imam, follower, or person praying alone must recite are treated in legal manuals. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
