- 73:20/q07/p73 A hadith reports that the Prophet fell ill and missed the night vigil for one or two nights. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A hadith reports that the Prophet fell ill and missed the night vigil for one or two nights. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q07/p74 Night prayer remains governed by the earlier command in 73:2 and the Sunnah’s encouragement of it; Ibn ʿĀshūr says he explained this already. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Night prayer remains governed by the earlier command in 73:2 and the Sunnah’s encouragement of it; Ibn ʿĀshūr says he explained this already. | mentions 73:2 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p75 The pronoun in منه refers to the Quran. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The pronoun in منه refers to the Quran. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p76 The measure of night prayer is entrusted to what is manageable for all Muslims despite their differing excuses. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The measure of night prayer is entrusted to what is manageable for all Muslims despite their differing excuses. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p77 The concession was warranted because night prayer is not one of Islam’s pillars, so its religious benefit was not weighty enough to require it universally. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The concession was warranted because night prayer is not one of Islam’s pillars, so its religious benefit was not weighty enough to require it universally. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p78 Another reported view recommends night vigil for everyone. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (القوم): Another reported view recommends night vigil for everyone. [1 notes: TABATABAI(القوم)]
- 73:20/q07/p79 The command eases the amount of night vigil for the generality of believers because they cannot calculate the specified portions; it does not cancel the original rule for those able to keep it. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The command eases the amount of night vigil for the generality of believers because they cannot calculate the specified portions; it does not cancel the original rule for those able to keep it. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q07/p80 Abu Ja'far's report says the Prophet observed and announced the night vigil, people struggled to calculate the portions and sometimes stayed until dawn fearing error, and God eased it; it adds that every prophet prayed at… — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (أبو جعفر): Abu Ja'far's report says the Prophet observed and announced the night vigil, people struggled to calculate the portions and sometimes stayed until dawn fearing error, and God eased it; it adds that every prophet prayed at night, never beginning early in the evening. [1 notes: TABATABAI(أبو جعفر)]
- 73:20/q07/p81 Those with the Prophet means his fellow believers, and min is partitive; some of them also kept night vigil. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Those with the Prophet means his fellow believers, and min is partitive; some of them also kept night vigil. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q07/p82 The phrase about not counting it refers to measuring the length of the night vigil. — ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): The phrase about not counting it refers to measuring the length of the night vigil. [1 notes: ASAD-NOTES]
- 73:20/q07/p83 Asad identifies this as the Qur'an's earliest instance of zakah and refers readers to his note 34 on surah 2 for an explanation. — ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): Asad identifies this as the Qur'an's earliest instance of zakah and refers readers to his note 34 on surah 2 for an explanation. | mentions 2:34 [1 notes: ASAD-NOTES]
- 73:20/q07/p84 The verse praises the Prophet for fulfilling night prayer, which God knows and will reward. — ISLAHI-TADABBUR (author): The verse praises the Prophet for fulfilling night prayer, which God knows and will reward. [1 notes: ISLAHI-TADABBUR]
- 73:20/q07/p85 It also praises Companions who voluntarily adopted the Prophet’s tahajjud practice as mandatory for themselves and eagerly followed his example. — ISLAHI-TADABBUR (author): It also praises Companions who voluntarily adopted the Prophet’s tahajjud practice as mandatory for themselves and eagerly followed his example. [1 notes: ISLAHI-TADABBUR]
- 73:20/q07/p86 God alone measures night and day, knows people’s circumstances, and judges that the Companions cannot sustain the specified night-prayer fractions, so they should recite what they can. — ISLAHI-TADABBUR (author): God alone measures night and day, knows people’s circumstances, and judges that the Companions cannot sustain the specified night-prayer fractions, so they should recite what they can. [1 notes: ISLAHI-TADABBUR]
- 73:20/q07/p87 Relief was given because ordinary Muslims could not sustain the taxing directive; the Prophet continued it until death, while for others tahajjud remains optional and its length follows their zeal. — ISLAHI-TADABBUR (author): Relief was given because ordinary Muslims could not sustain the taxing directive; the Prophet continued it until death, while for others tahajjud remains optional and its length follows their zeal. [1 notes: ISLAHI-TADABBUR]
- 73:20/q07/p88 The night vigil in this verse is the form of vigil commanded in verses 1–8. — STUDYQURAN (author): The night vigil in this verse is the form of vigil commanded in verses 1–8. | mentions 73:1, 73:2, 73:3, 73:4, 73:5, 73:6, 73:7, 73:8 [1 notes: STUDYQURAN]
- 73:20/q07/p89 Recite whatever Qur'an is easy in the five daily prayers, with no fixed amount. — MUQATIL: Recite whatever Qur'an is easy in the five daily prayers, with no fixed amount. [1 notes: MUQATIL]
- 73:20/q07/p90 Recite manageable Quran during night prayer instead of earlier measured vigil. — TAB-FULL: Recite manageable Quran during night prayer instead of earlier measured vigil. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p91 Scholars recommend even a little night prayer; a Prophetic report reinforces it. — NAHHAS: Scholars recommend even a little night prayer; a Prophetic report reinforces it. [1 notes: NAHHAS]
- 73:20/q07/p92 The prayer may be voluntary prayer or five prayers replacing vigil. — MAWARDI-FULL: The prayer may be voluntary prayer or five prayers replacing vigil. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p93 Easy describes strength, weakness, health, illness, not the fixed five-prayer count. — MAWARDI-FULL: Easy describes strength, weakness, health, illness, not the fixed five-prayer count. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p94 The wording may plainly mean whatever Quran is easy. — MAWARDI-FULL: The wording may plainly mean whatever Quran is easy. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p95 Abu Hanifa requires one verse from any Quranic passage. — MAWARDI-FULL: Abu Hanifa requires one verse from any Quranic passage. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(أبو حنيفة)]
- 73:20/q07/p96 Recitation outside prayer might be obligatory or recommended. — MAWARDI-FULL: Recitation outside prayer might be obligatory or recommended. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p97 An obligatory reading acquaints people with inimitability, monotheism, and prophethood without obligatory memorization of entire Quran. — MAWARDI-FULL: An obligatory reading acquaints people with inimitability, monotheism, and prophethood without obligatory memorization of entire Quran. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p98 Most recommend outside recitation because if obligatory, memorization would be obligatory. — MAWARDI-FULL: Most recommend outside recitation because if obligatory, memorization would be obligatory. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(الأكثرون)]
- 73:20/q07/p99 Dahhak says whole Quran is made easy and encompassed. — MAWARDI-FULL: Dahhak says whole Quran is made easy and encompassed. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(الضحاك)]
- 73:20/q07/p100 Juyaybir says one third of Quran. — MAWARDI-FULL: Juyaybir says one third of Quran. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(جويبر)]
- 73:20/q07/p101 Hasan identifies Maghrib and Isha prayers. — WAHIDI-BASIT: Hasan identifies Maghrib and Isha prayers. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الحسن)]
- 73:20/q07/p102 Pray and recite what is possible without fixed amount or duration. — WAHIDI-WAJIZ: Pray and recite what is possible without fixed amount or duration. [1 notes: WAHIDI-WAJIZ]
- 73:20/q07/p103 Abu Yusuf and Muhammad require three short or one long verse. — JISHUMI: Abu Yusuf and Muhammad require three short or one long verse. [1 notes: JISHUMI(أبو يوسف ومحمد)]
- 73:20/q07/p104 Abu Hanifa may allow a single verse. — JISHUMI: Abu Hanifa may allow a single verse. [1 notes: JISHUMI(أبو حنيفة)]
- 73:20/q07/p105 Recite whatever Quran is manageable. — BAGHAWI-FULL: Recite whatever Quran is manageable. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p106 Kashshaf prefers prayer named by recitation; this abrogated opening and was later superseded by five prayers. — KASHSHAF-FULL: Kashshaf prefers prayer named by recitation; this abrogated opening and was later superseded by five prayers. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 73:20/q07/p107 Majority treat manageable Quran recitation as permission. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Majority treat manageable Quran recitation as permission. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(الجمهور)]
- 73:20/q07/p108 Some say two rak'as after Maghrib with witr satisfy command. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Some say two rak'as after Maghrib with witr satisfy command. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(بعض العلماء)]
- 73:20/q07/p109 The Shaykh argues that subject-first order alone does not signal exclusivity, as in Zayd memorizes the Quran; context supplies the exclusive sense here. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الشيخ): The Shaykh argues that subject-first order alone does not signal exclusivity, as in Zayd memorizes the Quran; context supplies the exclusive sense here. [0 notes: SAMIN-DURR(الشيخ)-]
- 73:20/q07/p110 A proposed counter-proof that whatever entails its own nonexistence must exist is rejected: the conditional used is not true in reality, only under the assumption that Fātiḥa recitation is obligatory. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A proposed counter-proof that whatever entails its own nonexistence must exist is rejected: the conditional used is not true in reality, only under the assumption that Fātiḥa recitation is obligatory. [0 notes: ALUSI-FULL-]
- 73:20/q07/p111 If recitation is detached from prayer or vigil, it cannot ease the hardship of the earlier night-vigil command; at most it would address the command to recite with measured cadence, contrary to the passage's relieving context. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): If recitation is detached from prayer or vigil, it cannot ease the hardship of the earlier night-vigil command; at most it would address the command to recite with measured cadence, contrary to the passage's relieving context. ## 73:20/q08 Why are the sick named?  [مَّرْضَىٰ · interpretation]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q08/p1 The prospect of illness is one reason God gives for easing night prayer. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): The prospect of illness is one reason God gives for easing night prayer. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 73:20/q08/p2 Illness is mentioned first as the broadest excuse and one over which the sick person has no control. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Illness is mentioned first as the broadest excuse and one over which the sick person has no control. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q08/p3 Marḍā is the plural of marīḍ, illness being departure from the human being's proper balance. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Marḍā is the plural of marīḍ, illness being departure from the human being's proper balance. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q08/p4 Abu Dharr reports that attending a lesson surpasses a thousand units of prayer, a thousand funerals, and a thousand visits to the sick; asked whether Quran recitation is also surpassed, he answers that recitation without action… — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (أبو ذر): Abu Dharr reports that attending a lesson surpasses a thousand units of prayer, a thousand funerals, and a thousand visits to the sick; asked whether Quran recitation is also surpassed, he answers that recitation without action does not benefit. [1 notes: BURSEVI(أبو ذر)]
- 73:20/q08/p5 The address from تحصوه onward targets Muslims who kept night vigil, either by a shift from third to second person or by a general expression narrowed by context; the Prophet himself could count, while illness and… — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The address from تحصوه onward targets Muslims who kept night vigil, either by a shift from third to second person or by a general expression narrowed by context; the Prophet himself could count, while illness and other duties are mentioned. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q08/p6 The clause about illness and other circumstances is an elaboration of the inability to keep the measure, and gives another easing for additional conditions. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The clause about illness and other circumstances is an elaboration of the inability to keep the measure, and gives another easing for additional conditions. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q08/p7 The first broad category of reasons for easing is impaired health, represented by the mention of the sick. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The first broad category of reasons for easing is impaired health, represented by the mention of the sick. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q08/p8 The sick, travelers seeking provision, and fighters cannot sustain night prayer and receive relief. — MUQATIL: The sick, travelers seeking provision, and fighters cannot sustain night prayer and receive relief. [1 notes: MUQATIL]
- 73:20/q08/p9 Illness, trade travel, and fighting prompted relief of the night obligation and manageable recitation. — TAB-FULL: Illness, trade travel, and fighting prompted relief of the night obligation and manageable recitation. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 73:20/q08/p10 Traveler and fighter clauses coordinate with clause about sick. — NAHHAS: Traveler and fighter clauses coordinate with clause about sick. [1 notes: NAHHAS]
- 73:20/q08/p11 Illness incapacitates and explains easing. — MAWARDI-FULL: Illness incapacitates and explains easing. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 73:20/q08/p12 Illness and travel lift vigil for people with these excuses. — QUSHAYRI: Illness and travel lift vigil for people with these excuses. [1 notes: QUSHAYRI]
- 73:20/q08/p13 The sick cannot manage night prayer. — WAHIDI-BASIT: The sick cannot manage night prayer. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT]
- 73:20/q08/p14 Illness, trade travel, and fighting explain relief. — WAHIDI-WAJIZ: Illness, trade travel, and fighting explain relief. [1 notes: WAHIDI-WAJIZ]
- 73:20/q08/p15 Wa-akharun elliptically means there will be others. — JISHUMI: Wa-akharun elliptically means there will be others. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 73:20/q08/p16 Sick cannot keep vigil. — BAGHAWI-FULL: Sick cannot keep vigil. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL]
- 73:20/q08/p17 Sick, traders, and fighters need easing; lawful earners and fighters are equated. — KASHSHAF-FULL: Sick, traders, and fighters need easing; lawful earners and fighters are equated. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 73:20/q08/p18 He knew begins new sentence explaining reason for abrogation. — KASHSHAF-FULL: He knew begins new sentence explaining reason for abrogation. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 73:20/q08/p19 Illness, trade travel, and fighting impede vigil and cause easing. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Illness, trade travel, and fighting impede vigil and cause easing. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]

## 73:20/q09 Who are those traveling in search of God's bounty?  [وَءَاخَرُونَ يَضْرِبُونَ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ يَبْتَغُونَ مِن فَضْلِ ٱللَّهِ · referent]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q09/p1 Ibn Masʿūd says a patient, God-seeking merchant who brings goods to a Muslim city and sells at the day's price is like the martyrs. — TABRISI, d. 548 (عبد الله بن مسعود): Ibn Masʿūd says a patient, God-seeking merchant who brings goods to a Muslim city and sells at the day's price is like the martyrs. [1 notes: TABRISI(عبد الله بن مسعود)]
- 73:20/q09/p2 Ibn ʿUmar says that after dying in God's path, he would most wish to die traveling while seeking God's bounty. — TABRISI, d. 548 (ابن عمر): Ibn ʿUmar says that after dying in God's path, he would most wish to die traveling while seeking God's bounty. [1 notes: TABRISI(ابن عمر)]
- 73:20/q09/p3 Ibn ʿAbbās explains traveling in God's bounty as journeys for trade and profit. — TABRISI, d. 548 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās explains traveling in God's bounty as journeys for trade and profit. [1 notes: TABRISI(ابن عباس)]
- 73:20/q09/p4 Exegetes give ḍarb the sense of traveling in these verses about setting out in God’s cause or across the land. — IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (أهل التفسير): Exegetes give ḍarb the sense of traveling in these verses about setting out in God’s cause or across the land. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(أهل التفسير)]
- 73:20/q09/p5 The sick, travelers seeking provision through trade, and fighters in God’s path may be unable to keep the night vigil. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): The sick, travelers seeking provision through trade, and fighters in God’s path may be unable to keep the night vigil. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD]
- 73:20/q09/p6 The easing is explained by illness and the demands on travelers seeking livelihood and fighters: these groups could not sustain the earlier night-vigil requirement. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The easing is explained by illness and the demands on travelers seeking livelihood and fighters: these groups could not sustain the earlier night-vigil requirement. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p7 The ill cannot manage night worship; travelers and fighters already perform strenuous daytime work, so little sleep would make hardship accumulate. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The ill cannot manage night worship; travelers and fighters already perform strenuous daytime work, so little sleep would make hardship accumulate. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p8 The verse notably places fighters and lawful earners on equal footing. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The verse notably places fighters and lawful earners on equal footing. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p9 One reported measure is a hundred verses; another says that reading a hundred verses earns the status of the devout. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (قيل): One reported measure is a hundred verses; another says that reading a hundred verses earns the status of the devout. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(قيل)]
- 73:20/q09/p10 The verse explains the easing: illness, travel for trade, and campaigning could make night prayer difficult and risk missing prayer, so God lightened the duty for all. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The verse explains the easing: illness, travel for trade, and campaigning could make night prayer difficult and risk missing prayer, so God lightened the duty for all. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p11 The verse places lawful earners who support themselves and their families and do good alongside fighters, indicating that earning money can rank like jihad. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The verse places lawful earners who support themselves and their families and do good alongside fighters, indicating that earning money can rank like jihad. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p12 The Prophet said that a food importer who carries provisions between towns and sells at that day's price has the rank of martyrs with God. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet said that a food importer who carries provisions between towns and sells at that day's price has the rank of martyrs with God. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q09/p13 Ibn Masud said a patient trader who brings goods to a Muslim city and sells at the day's price earns the rank of martyrs with God. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masud said a patient trader who brings goods to a Muslim city and sells at the day's price earns the rank of martyrs with God. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن مسعود)]
- 73:20/q09/p14 Ibn Umar said that, after dying in God's cause, he preferred no death to dying on his saddle while travelling to seek God's bounty. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar said that, after dying in God's cause, he preferred no death to dying on his saddle while travelling to seek God's bounty. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عمر)]
- 73:20/q09/p15 A pious merchant instructed his agent to sell grain on reaching Basra; after the agent delayed for a higher price, the owner ordered all proceeds given to Basra's poor, fearing hoarding. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (بعض السلف): A pious merchant instructed his agent to sell grain on reaching Basra; after the agent delayed for a higher price, the owner ordered all proceeds given to Basra's poor, fearing hoarding. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(بعض السلف)]
- 73:20/q09/p16 Those traveling to seek God's bounty are traveling for trade and for acquiring knowledge. — BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Those traveling to seek God's bounty are traveling for trade and for acquiring knowledge. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p17 The easing is explained by the hardship night vigil would impose on the sick, travelers, and those fighting in God's path. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The easing is explained by the hardship night vigil would impose on the sick, travelers, and those fighting in God's path. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p18 Travelers seek God's bounty through trade or the pursuit of knowledge. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Travelers seek God's bounty through trade or the pursuit of knowledge. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p19 God places the fighter and the lawful earner on equal footing, because earning lawful provision is jihad. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): God places the fighter and the lawful earner on equal footing, because earning lawful provision is jihad. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p20 Ibn Masʿūd says a patient, reward-seeking merchant who brings goods to a Muslim town and sells at that day's price is counted with the martyrs before God. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masʿūd says a patient, reward-seeking merchant who brings goods to a Muslim town and sells at that day's price is counted with the martyrs before God. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL(ابن مسعود)]
- 73:20/q09/p21 Ibn ʿUmar says that, after death in God's cause, he prefers dying while traveling to earn God's bounty. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (ابن عمر): Ibn ʿUmar says that, after death in God's cause, he prefers dying while traveling to earn God's bounty. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL(ابن عمر)]
- 73:20/q09/p22 The first وآخرون coordinates with مرضى; يضربون and يبتغون describe them, though يبتغون may instead be circumstantial of the subject of يضربون. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The first وآخرون coordinates with مرضى; يضربون and يبتغون describe them, though يبتغون may instead be circumstantial of the subject of يضربون. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 73:20/q09/p23 Accusative نصفه and ثلثه coordinate with أدنى, understood as a nearer time; spatial nearness is metaphorically applied to time. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Accusative نصفه and ثلثه coordinate with أدنى, understood as a nearer time; spatial nearness is metaphorically applied to time. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 73:20/q09/p24 Adnā means less in duration here; nearness and lower rank both imply a shorter distance, explaining the word's shared senses. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Adnā means less in duration here; nearness and lower rank both imply a shorter distance, explaining the word's shared senses. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p25 God spells out why the former night-prayer measure was eased: illness, travel, and fighting make exact observance difficult. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): God spells out why the former night-prayer measure was eased: illness, travel, and fighting make exact observance difficult. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p26 Ibn Umar says that, after martyrdom, he would prefer to die traveling and seeking God's bounty. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (عبد الله بن عمر): Ibn Umar says that, after martyrdom, he would prefer to die traveling and seeking God's bounty. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(عبد الله بن عمر)]
- 73:20/q09/p27 Travelers are described as striking the earth because a diligent walker strikes it with his foot. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Travelers are described as striking the earth because a diligent walker strikes it with his foot. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p28 Travel for trade follows illness as another common reason that prevents exact night observance. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Travel for trade follows illness as another common reason that prevents exact night observance. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p29 Seeking God's bounty includes pursuing profit in trade or acquiring knowledge; God's provision exceeds people's hopes. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Seeking God's bounty includes pursuing profit in trade or acquiring knowledge; God's provision exceeds people's hopes. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p30 Al-Baghawi transmits Ibn Masud's view that a patient trader who imports goods to a Muslim city and sells at that day's price is ranked with martyrs. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (ابن مسعود): Al-Baghawi transmits Ibn Masud's view that a patient trader who imports goods to a Muslim city and sells at that day's price is ranked with martyrs. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(ابن مسعود)]
- 73:20/q09/p31 Umar says that, after jihad, he would prefer to die while travelling with his saddle bags seeking God's bounty, then recites the verse about travel and fighting. — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عمر بن الخطاب): Umar says that, after jihad, he would prefer to die while travelling with his saddle bags seeking God's bounty, then recites the verse about travel and fighting. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(عمر بن الخطاب)]
- 73:20/q09/p32 Ibn Masʿud reports the Prophet saying that a food merchant supplying a Muslim town and selling at the day's market price has the standing of a martyr with God. — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عبد الله بن مسعود): Ibn Masʿud reports the Prophet saying that a food merchant supplying a Muslim town and selling at the day's market price has the standing of a martyr with God. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(عبد الله بن مسعود)]
- 73:20/q09/p33 Others travel for trade and profit; 'God's bounty' explicitly acknowledges that earnings come from Him, and seeking knowledge is also among the finest gains. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Others travel for trade and profit; 'God's bounty' explicitly acknowledges that earnings come from Him, and seeking knowledge is also among the finest gains. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q09/p34 Those fighting in God's path are another group; the verse signals that permission to fight enemies will come and equates their merit with lawful earners supporting themselves, dependents, and the needy. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Those fighting in God's path are another group; the verse signals that permission to fight enemies will come and equates their merit with lawful earners supporting themselves, dependents, and the needy. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q09/p35 The verse places lawful trade for family support and charity on a level with striving in God's path. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The verse places lawful trade for family support and charity on a level with striving in God's path. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q09/p36 Adnā means less in duration than two-thirds; as the comparative of d-n-w, nearness is figuratively used for smallness, with metonymy the closer explanation. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Adnā means less in duration than two-thirds; as the comparative of d-n-w, nearness is figuratively used for smallness, with metonymy the closer explanation. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p37 God’s knowledge that some would be ill, travel, or fight is a further reason for relief, alongside the difficulty of calculating night hours; travelers seek trade and profit. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): God’s knowledge that some would be ill, travel, or fight is a further reason for relief, alongside the difficulty of calculating night hours; travelers seek trade and profit. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p38 The travelers seek God’s bounty through trade and profit; al-Ālūsī broadens seeking bounty to include acquiring knowledge, and calls the clause circumstantial. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The travelers seek God’s bounty through trade and profit; al-Ālūsī broadens seeking bounty to include acquiring knowledge, and calls the clause circumstantial. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p39 “Others fighting in God’s way” refers to the warriors; placing them beside those traveling for livelihood suggests that the travelers share a comparable reward. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): “Others fighting in God’s way” refers to the warriors; placing them beside those traveling for livelihood suggests that the travelers share a comparable reward. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p40 ʿUmar wished to die either in battle or while seeking God’s bounty between two mountain slopes, and recited this verse. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (عمر): ʿUmar wished to die either in battle or while seeking God’s bounty between two mountain slopes, and recited this verse. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(عمر)]
- 73:20/q09/p41 Ibn Masʿūd reports that the Prophet praised a food importer who sells to Muslims at the day’s market price, then recited this verse. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (النبي صلى الله تعالى عليه وسلم): Ibn Masʿūd reports that the Prophet praised a food importer who sells to Muslims at the day’s market price, then recited this verse. - 73:20/q09/p42 God knew in advance that some believers would be ill, others travel seeking His bounty, and others fight in His way, making the demanding night vigil difficult or impossible. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): God knew in advance that some believers would be ill, others travel seeking His bounty, and others fight in His way, making the demanding night vigil difficult or impossible. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q09/p43 Mentioning traders seeking God's bounty beside fighters in His way signals that their rewards are close to one another. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Mentioning traders seeking God's bounty beside fighters in His way signals that their rewards are close to one another. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q09/p44 Umar said that, after jihad, his most beloved death would come while seeking God's bounty between two mountain branches, and he recited this verse. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Hazret-i Ömer): Umar said that, after jihad, his most beloved death would come while seeking God's bounty between two mountain branches, and he recited this verse. [1 notes: ELMALILI(Hazret-i Ömer)]
- 73:20/q09/p45 The opening praises the Prophet's faithful, earnest fulfillment of the command: he regularly prayed the prescribed half night or slightly less, and went as far as nearly two-thirds. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The opening praises the Prophet's faithful, earnest fulfillment of the command: he regularly prayed the prescribed half night or slightly less, and went as far as nearly two-thirds. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p46 The second category is livelihood work—trade, crafts, farming, and similar necessities—summarized as traveling in search of God’s provision. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The second category is livelihood work—trade, crafts, farming, and similar necessities—summarized as traveling in search of God’s provision. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p47 Some Companions infer the virtue of trade and travel from God’s placing lawful earners alongside fighters; these two reasons honor such activities, while other excused needs resemble illness. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Some Companions infer the virtue of trade and travel from God’s placing lawful earners alongside fighters; these two reasons honor such activities, while other excused needs resemble illness. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p48 Ibn ʿUmar says that after death in God’s path, he would prefer to die between his travel packs while journeying in search of God’s bounty. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (ابن عمر): Ibn ʿUmar says that after death in God’s path, he would prefer to die between his travel packs while journeying in search of God’s bounty. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(ابن عمر)]
- 73:20/q09/p49 يضربون في الأرض means traveling across the earth. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): يضربون في الأرض means traveling across the earth. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p50 The root image is one body striking another; travel is called striking the earth because يضربون incorporates walking, while the striking sense fades and في marks earth as the place traversed. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The root image is one body striking another; travel is called striking the earth because يضربون incorporates walking, while the striking sense fades and في marks earth as the place traversed. | mentions 3:137, 4:101 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p51 Seeking God’s bounty means seeking provision; 2:198 uses the expression for trade during the Hajj period. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Seeking God’s bounty means seeking provision; 2:198 uses the expression for trade during the Hajj period. | mentions 2:198 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p52 Here traveling for God’s bounty means business travel: journeys often occupy both night and day, requiring travelers to sleep by day. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Here traveling for God’s bounty means business travel: journeys often occupy both night and day, requiring travelers to sleep by day. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p53 This is a rule based on the general likelihood of hardship: like travel prayer remaining two rakʿahs because travel usually brings hardship, according to ʿĀʾisha’s report. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): This is a rule based on the general likelihood of hardship: like travel prayer remaining two rakʿahs because travel usually brings hardship, according to ʿĀʾisha’s report. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p54 ʿĀʾisha reports that prayer was first prescribed as two rakʿahs, then three prayers were lengthened at home while travel prayer remained two. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (عائشة): ʿĀʾisha reports that prayer was first prescribed as two rakʿahs, then three prayers were lengthened at home while travel prayer remained two. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(عائشة)]
- 73:20/q09/p55 Doing good in worldly life for its afterlife reward is pictured as a traveler sending ahead luggage, tools, and family to the destination so they are ready on arrival. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Doing good in worldly life for its afterlife reward is pictured as a traveler sending ahead luggage, tools, and family to the destination so they are ready on arrival. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q09/p56 Illness, travel, and fighting provide another reason for easing exact night portions, since calculating them is especially difficult in those circumstances. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Illness, travel, and fighting provide another reason for easing exact night portions, since calculating them is especially difficult in those circumstances. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q09/p57 Traveling in search of God's bounty means journeying between lands for livelihood through trade. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Traveling in search of God's bounty means journeying between lands for livelihood through trade. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q09/p58 Ibn Mardawayh reports Ibn Masud quoting the Prophet: a trader who imports food to a Muslim town and sells at the day's price has the rank of a martyr; the Prophet then recited this verse. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (عبد الله بن مسعود): Ibn Mardawayh reports Ibn Masud quoting the Prophet: a trader who imports food to a Muslim town and sells at the day's price has the rank of a martyr; the Prophet then recited this verse. - 73:20/q09/p59 Adna is a comparative from nearness, conventionally meaning just less than an amount: a count near ten is nine, not eleven. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Adna is a comparative from nearness, conventionally meaning just less than an amount: a count near ten is nine, not eleven. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q09/p60 Spending for God’s cause benefits the believers themselves, is credited to their accounts with God, and is the most profitable trade. — ISLAHI-TADABBUR (author): Spending for God’s cause benefits the believers themselves, is credited to their accounts with God, and is the most profitable trade. [1 notes: ISLAHI-TADABBUR]
- 73:20/q09/p61 Night vigil is difficult for the sick, travelers, and fighters, so they need only perform what is not too taxing. — STUDYQURAN (author): Night vigil is difficult for the sick, travelers, and fighters, so they need only perform what is not too taxing. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN]
- 73:20/q09/p62 Darb can mean traveling. — IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL: Darb can mean traveling. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL]

- 73:20/q09/p64 Ibn Umar wishes, after martyrdom, to die traveling for God's bounty. — THALABI: Ibn Umar wishes, after martyrdom, to die traveling for God's bounty. [1 notes: THALABI(ابن عمر)]
- 73:20/q09/p65 One view identifies travelers and cites 4:101. — MAWARDI-FULL: One view identifies travelers and cites 4:101. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]

- 73:20/q09/p73 Ibn Masud says fair patient trader has martyr's rank. — BAGHAWI-FULL: Ibn Masud says fair patient trader has martyr's rank. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL(عبد الله بن مسعود)]
- 73:20/q09/p74 Ibn Masud says fair patient importer is counted among martyrs. — KASHSHAF-FULL: Ibn Masud says fair patient importer is counted among martyrs. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL(عبد الله بن مسعود)]

## 73:20/q10 Who are those fighting in God's path and why are they named?  [وَءَاخَرُونَ يُقَٰتِلُونَ فِى سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ · referent]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q10/p1 Tawus likened caring for a widow and a poor person to fighting in God's path. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (طاوس): Tawus likened caring for a widow and a poor person to fighting in God's path. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(طاوس)]
- 73:20/q10/p2 The phrase refers to people fighting in God's path. — BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The phrase refers to people fighting in God's path. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 73:20/q10/p3 The phrase places the fighting within God's path and removes moral and physical obstacles that block that path. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The phrase places the fighting within God's path and removes moral and physical obstacles that block that path. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q10/p4 The third category is work for the community: fighting, frontier watch and garrison duty, army management, and sending envoys or ambassadors to spread Islam; it includes the Prophet’s fighting and illness. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The third category is work for the community: fighting, frontier watch and garrison duty, army management, and sending envoys or ambassadors to spread Islam; it includes the Prophet’s fighting and illness. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q10/p5 Prayer and zakah are commonly paired in revelation; Abu Bakr inferred that zakah refusal warrants fighting, telling ʿUmar he would fight anyone who separated them. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Prayer and zakah are commonly paired in revelation; Abu Bakr inferred that zakah refusal warrants fighting, telling ʿUmar he would fight anyone who separated them. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q10/p6 Others see the fighting as foreshadowing the trials faced by the early Muslim community. — STUDYQURAN (others): Others see the fighting as foreshadowing the trials faced by the early Muslim community. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN(others)]
- 73:20/q10/p7 Fighters are engaged in jihad for God's obedience. — MAWARDI-FULL: Fighters are engaged in jihad for God's obedience. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 73:20/q10/p8 Fighters in God's way cannot manage vigil. — WAHIDI-BASIT: Fighters in God's way cannot manage vigil. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT]
- 73:20/q10/p9 Others fight in God's path. — WAHIDI-WAJIZ: Others fight in God's path. [1 notes: WAHIDI-WAJIZ]
- 73:20/q10/p10 Fighters cannot keep vigil. — BAGHAWI-FULL: Fighters cannot keep vigil. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL]
- 73:20/q10/p11 Fighters in God's path justify easing night prayer. — TABRISI: Fighters in God's path justify easing night prayer. [1 notes: TABRISI]

## 73:20/q11 Why is the recitation command repeated?  [فَٱقْرَءُوا۟ مَا تَيَسَّرَ مِنْهُ · rhetoric]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q11/p1 The repeated command to recite what is easy reinforces the concession. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The repeated command to recite what is easy reinforces the concession. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 73:20/q11/p2 The second main view takes the phrase as Quran recitation itself, for studying it and preventing it from being forgotten. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The second main view takes the phrase as Quran recitation itself, for studying it and preventing it from being forgotten. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 73:20/q11/p3 A second view takes “recite” to mean “pray,” since prayer is called Qurʾān, as in the expression for the dawn prayer at 17:78; Ibn al-ʿArabī says this better fits the subject of the verse. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن العربي): A second view takes “recite” to mean “pray,” since prayer is called Qurʾān, as in the expression for the dawn prayer at 17:78; Ibn al-ʿArabī says this better fits the subject of the verse. | mentions 17:78 [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن العربي)]
- 73:20/q11/p4 This is a new explanation of the ruling, giving a further reason for its concession; the ruling is repeated in light of it. — BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): This is a new explanation of the ruling, giving a further reason for its concession; the ruling is repeated in light of it. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 73:20/q11/p5 The command to recite what is manageable is repeated because the Companions were extremely scrupulous. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The command to recite what is manageable is repeated because the Companions were extremely scrupulous. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 73:20/q11/p6 The ill, travelers seeking trade, and fighters explain the easing of the earlier night-vigil requirement; the repeated recitation command emphasizes it. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The ill, travelers seeking trade, and fighters explain the easing of the earlier night-vigil requirement; the repeated recitation command emphasizes it. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 73:20/q11/p7 The second view requires no recitation by the follower in either quiet or audible prayer; Ibn Kathir says the prophetic chains for its proof are all unsound. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (الثاني): The second view requires no recitation by the follower in either quiet or audible prayer; Ibn Kathir says the prophetic chains for its proof are all unsound. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(الثاني)]
- 73:20/q11/p8 The repeated instruction means to perform as much night prayer as is manageable. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): The repeated instruction means to perform as much night prayer as is manageable. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q11/p9 Fighting in God's way means attacking God's enemies; the repeated phrase exalts jihad and avoids confusion with a return to commerce. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Fighting in God's way means attacking God's enemies; the repeated phrase exalts jihad and avoids confusion with a return to commerce. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q11/p10 A second interpretation takes the wording literally as a command to recite Qur'an itself, though the author says this sits poorly with context. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): A second interpretation takes the wording literally as a command to recite Qur'an itself, though the author says this sits poorly with context. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q11/p11 A second report from Abu Hanifa requires enough recitation for the name Quran to apply and for it not to resemble ordinary human speech. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (İmâm-ı A‘zam Ebû Hanîfe): A second report from Abu Hanifa requires enough recitation for the name Quran to apply and for it not to resemble ordinary human speech. [1 notes: ELMALILI(İmâm-ı A‘zam Ebû Hanîfe)]
- 73:20/q11/p12 Abu Hanifa distinguishes wajib from fard: the hadith makes al-Fatiha wajib, while this verse makes unspecified Quran recitation fard; deliberate omission requires repeating the prayer, but accidental omission without prostration of forgetfulness does not require repeating… — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (İmâm-ı A‘zam Ebû Hanîfe): Abu Hanifa distinguishes wajib from fard: the hadith makes al-Fatiha wajib, while this verse makes unspecified Quran recitation fard; deliberate omission requires repeating the prayer, but accidental omission without prostration of forgetfulness does not require repeating it after the time has passed, though repetition is recommended. [1 notes: ELMALILI(İmâm-ı A‘zam Ebû Hanîfe)]
- 73:20/q11/p13 The verb علم is repeated rather than coordinated with وأن سيكون to mark this as a distinct consideration in the easing. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The verb علم is repeated rather than coordinated with وأن سيكون to mark this as a distinct consideration in the easing. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q11/p14 The repeated concession to recite what is manageable reinforces the easing. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The repeated concession to recite what is manageable reinforces the easing. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q11/p15 The pronoun in minhu refers to the Quran. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The pronoun in minhu refers to the Quran. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q11/p16 A second interpretation takes it to mean reciting as much of the Qur'an, and whichever verses, as are easy during night prayer. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (müfessirler): A second interpretation takes it to mean reciting as much of the Qur'an, and whichever verses, as are easy during night prayer. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(müfessirler)]
- 73:20/q11/p17 The pronoun minhu refers to the Quran. — TAB-FULL: The pronoun minhu refers to the Quran. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 73:20/q11/p18 Minhu refers to the Quran. — WAHIDI-BASIT: Minhu refers to the Quran. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT]
- 73:20/q11/p19 Repeated recitation command adds emphasis. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Repeated recitation command adds emphasis. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]

## 73:20/q12 What prayer does 'establish prayer' require?  [وَأَقِيمُوا۟ ٱلصَّلَوٰةَ · law]
- 73:20/q12/p1 Establish the prescribed prayers. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Establish prayer according to the limits God made obligatory. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): Establish the five daily prayers at their appointed times. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (البخاري وغيره): Ibn al-Arabi reports that some, including al-Bukhari, held that this verse established a two-rakʿa night-prayer duty. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The prayer to be established is the five obligatory prayers at their appointed times. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The prayer to be established is the obligatory prayer. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The required vigil became an easy portion of recitation and was later superseded by the five obligatory prayers. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The explicit command to establish prayer either favors understanding the prior recitation as independent of formal prayer or specifies that it meant prayer. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Establish the obligatory prayer with all its pillars, conditions, preliminaries, complements, forms, and refinements. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (صاحب الكشف): Al-Kashf says the voluntary view relies on precedent: obligatory prayers have fixed times rather than timing left to each worshipper, a sound argument. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The command to establish prayer reminds believers to guard the obligatory prayers and not neglect them, as stated in 4:103. | mentions 4:103 ISLAHI-TADABBUR (author): To compensate for less night prayer, Muslims should maintain the five prayers diligently and spend for God’s cause, including supporting His word and urgent religious or state needs. MUQATIL: Complete the five prayers and pay obligatory zakat. FARRA: Establish the obligatory prayer. TAB-FULL: Establish five daily prayers and give obligatory alms to rightful recipients. TAB-FULL: Qatada says prayer and zakat bind all without dispensation. MAWARDI-FULL: Establish the five prayers at appointed times. QUSHAYRI: Establish obligatory prayer; good deeds return better than worldly possessions. WAHIDI-BASIT: Muqatil says pray at proper times; vigil lifted for believers but retained for Prophet. WAHIDI-BASIT: Ibn Abbas says obligations at proper places and times. JISHUMI: Prayer and zakat are obligatory despite generic definite form. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Prayer and zakat are prescribed duties. [21 notes: TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; QURTUBI-FULL(البخاري وغيره); NASAFI-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL; ISLAHI-TADABBUR; MUQATIL; FARRA; TAB-FULL(قتادة); MAWARDI-FULL; QUSHAYRI; WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن عباس,مقاتل); JISHUMI; IBNATIYYA-FULL; ALUSI-FULL(صاحب الكشف)+]

## 73:20/q13 What does the zakat command require?  [وَءَاتُوا۟ ٱلزَّكَوٰةَ · law]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q13/p1 Zakat here is obligatory almsgiving. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): The zakat commanded here is obligatory alms. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عكرمة وقتادة): Ikrima and Qatada understand zakat here as the obligatory alms due on one's property. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The command is to pay the obligatory alms. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The zakat to be paid is obligatory alms. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (أكثر المفسرين): Most commentators understand the prayer and alms here as the prescribed duties, apparently the five prayers and the familiar obligatory zakat. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The commands require performing the obligatory prayers at their appointed times and paying zakat. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The zakat commanded is obligatory zakat. STUDYQURAN (some): Some interpret zakāh here as the obligatory alms. MAWARDI-FULL: Qatada and Ikrima take it as full wealth due. [9 notes: TABRISI; QURTUBI-FULL(عكرمة وقتادة); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL(أكثر المفسرين); ELMALILI; TABATABAI; STUDYQURAN(some); MAWARDI-FULL(قتادة وعكرمة)]
- 73:20/q13/p2 Zakat here is the alms due at Ramadan's end. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الحارث العكلي): Al-Harith al-Akli says this refers to the alms at the end of Ramadan, since wealth-zakat was prescribed later. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (بعضهم): One reported interpretation takes the zakat here as zakat al-fitr, since Mecca had no other zakat then, and says it became obligatory later. MAWARDI-FULL: Harith al-Ukli takes zakat as alms at Ramadan's end. KASHSHAF-FULL: Zakat is obligatory alms or zakat al-fitr; formal zakat was not required in Mecca. [4 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الحارث العكلي); BURSEVI(بعضهم); MAWARDI-FULL(الحارث العكلي); KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 73:20/q13/p3 The phrase can mean all charitable giving, zakat paid excellently, or every good deed involving one's person or wealth. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The phrase can mean all charitable giving, zakat paid excellently, or every good deed involving one's person or wealth. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 73:20/q13/p4 On the zakat reading, excellence means giving the best, most useful property, with sincere intention, seeking God's pleasure, and paying an eligible recipient. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): On the zakat reading, excellence means giving the best, most useful property, with sincere intention, seeking God's pleasure, and paying an eligible recipient. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 73:20/q13/p5 Give the required zakat from the good wealth God has bestowed; it is the financial pillar alongside prayer. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Give the required zakat from the good wealth God has bestowed; it is the financial pillar alongside prayer. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q13/p6 The Prophet is cited as saying that wealth carries a due beyond zakat; giving it well means choosing excellent, useful property for the poor with sincere intention. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (النبي عليه السلام): The Prophet is cited as saying that wealth carries a due beyond zakat; giving it well means choosing excellent, useful property for the poor with sincere intention. [1 notes: BURSEVI(النبي عليه السلام)]
- 73:20/q13/p7 The later prescription and payment of zakat signals that God would fulfill His promise to the Messenger, establish His religion, and make it prevail. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The later prescription and payment of zakat signals that God would fulfill His promise to the Messenger, establish His religion, and make it prevail. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q13/p8 Pairing prayer, zakat, and charity signals that religious duties remain in force; the easing does not spread to every obligation, as in 58:13. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Pairing prayer, zakat, and charity signals that religious duties remain in force; the easing does not spread to every obligation, as in 58:13. | mentions 58:13 [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q13/p9 Some say zakat was imposed in Mecca without specified thresholds, while Medina later specified its thresholds. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (قيل): Some say zakat was imposed in Mecca without specified thresholds, while Medina later specified its thresholds. [1 notes: TABATABAI(قيل)]
- 73:20/q13/p10 Generous spending can mean any charitable spending, but alongside zakāh it denotes expenditure for war or an emergency need. — ISLAHI-TADABBUR (author): Generous spending can mean any charitable spending, but alongside zakāh it denotes expenditure for war or an emergency need. [1 notes: ISLAHI-TADABBUR]
- 73:20/q13/p11 The closing section commands proper prayer, zakat, good works, charity, and compassionate help for the poor, promising multiplied reward with God in the afterlife. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The closing section commands proper prayer, zakat, good works, charity, and compassionate help for the poor, promising multiplied reward with God in the afterlife. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 73:20/q13/p12 Instead, the verse aims to cultivate awareness of the duties of prayer and zakat and begin their earliest practices. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Instead, the verse aims to cultivate awareness of the duties of prayer and zakat and begin their earliest practices. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR+]
- 73:20/q13/p13 This is the first mention of alms (zakāh) in revelation’s chronological order. — STUDYQURAN (author): This is the first mention of alms (zakāh) in revelation’s chronological order. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN]
- 73:20/q13/p14 Because zakāh’s specific form was not yet institutionalized, the verse may refer to charity generally. — STUDYQURAN (author): Because zakāh’s specific form was not yet institutionalized, the verse may refer to charity generally. | mentions 23:4 [1 notes: STUDYQURAN]
- 73:20/q13/p15 Ibn Abbas takes zakat as obedience and sincerity. — MAWARDI-FULL: Ibn Abbas takes zakat as obedience and sincerity. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس)]

## 73:20/q14 What is lending God a good loan?  [وَأَقْرِضُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ قَرْضًا حَسَنًۭا · interpretation]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q14/p1 The good loan is voluntary charity beyond zakat. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the goodly loan means acts beyond zakat, such as maintaining kinship ties and feeding guests. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Lending to God means voluntary good works; the root of qarḍ is cutting, as the giver cuts that amount from personal wealth and gives it away. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The conjunction marks distinct duties: zakat is obligatory payment, while lending to God means voluntary charity. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The goodly loan includes voluntary acts of charity and worship with body and wealth, offered eagerly and beautifully while healthy and prosperous; God preserves and multiplies it. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The good loan means voluntary charitable spending, distinct from obligatory duties whose repayment is assured; the command urges supererogatory giving. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Lending to God means voluntary charity; giving to the poor is likened to a loan because God promises a generous return in the reward of the Last Day. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Lending God a good loan means financial donations in His cause beyond obligatory zakat. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (سماعة): In al-Qummi's tafsir, Zur'ah transmits Sama'ah's answer that a goodly loan is something other than zakat. MUQATIL: The good loan is voluntary giving willingly after obligatory alms. TAB-FULL: Ibn Zayd says the loan is voluntary giving beyond zakat. NAHHAS: Ibn Zayd says loan is voluntary giving beyond zakat. MAWARDI-FULL: Ibn Zayd takes loan as voluntary deeds after obligations. WAHIDI-BASIT: Ibn Abbas says voluntary giving beyond zakat includes kinship and hosting guests. BAGHAWI-FULL: Ibn Abbas says loan includes charity beyond zakat, kinship, guests. KASHSHAF-FULL: Loan may be voluntary charity, excellent zakat, or any personal or financial good. [15 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI; IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI(سماعة); MUQATIL; TAB-FULL(ابن زيد); NAHHAS(ابن زيد); MAWARDI-FULL(ابن زيد); WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن عباس); BAGHAWI-FULL(ابن عباس); KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 73:20/q14/p2 The good loan is spending for God's cause. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Lending God a good loan means spending in God's cause and in causes He commands or encourages. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عمر بن الخطاب): Umar ibn al-Khattab interprets the good loan as spending in God's path. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (بعضهم): Some interpret the loan as reciting glorification, praise, the testimony of divine unity, and takbir, as well as spending in God's cause, following Umar's explanation. MAWARDI-FULL: Umar takes it as spending in God's cause. [4 notes: TABRISI; QURTUBI-FULL(عمر بن الخطاب); BURSEVI(بعضهم); MAWARDI-FULL(عمر)]
- 73:20/q14/p3 The good loan is excellent payment of zakat. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The zakat command requires giving, whereas the loan command additionally qualifies the gift as good; calling charity a loan likens its return to repayment with increase. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Lending God a good loan means spending generously in good works or paying zakat in the best manner and for the greatest benefit of the poor. [2 notes: BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q14/p4 The good loan includes spending on one's family. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (زيد بن أسلم): Zayd ibn Aslam interprets the good loan as spending on one's family. MAWARDI-FULL: Zayd ibn Aslam takes it as family spending. [2 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(زيد بن أسلم); MAWARDI-FULL(زيد بن أسلم)]
- 73:20/q14/p5 Ibn al-Jawzi refers to his earlier explanation of a goodly loan at al-Hadid 57:18. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): Ibn al-Jawzi refers to his earlier explanation of a goodly loan at al-Hadid 57:18. | mentions 57:18 [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD]
- 73:20/q14/p6 A good loan to God is spending wholesome wealth solely to seek God's approval. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): A good loan to God is spending wholesome wealth solely to seek God's approval. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 73:20/q14/p7 says the good-loan explanation was already given in Surat al-Hadid. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The author says the good-loan explanation was already given in Surat al-Hadid. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 73:20/q14/p8 The good loan means other charitable spending, or paying zakāt in the best manner; the promise of return encourages it. — BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The good loan means other charitable spending, or paying zakāt in the best manner; the promise of return encourages it. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 73:20/q14/p9 The loan is attributed to God so donors do not lord it over the poor: the poor assist them in worship, so the donor owes them gratitude rather than claiming a favor. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The loan is attributed to God so donors do not lord it over the poor: the poor assist them in worship, so the donor owes them gratitude rather than claiming a favor. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 73:20/q14/p10 A good loan is given from lawful wealth and with sincerity. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): A good loan is given from lawful wealth and with sincerity. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 73:20/q14/p11 Lending God a good loan means giving charity, for which God grants the best and most abundant reward; Ibn Kathir links this to 2:245. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Lending God a good loan means giving charity, for which God grants the best and most abundant reward; Ibn Kathir links this to 2:245. | mentions 2:245 [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q14/p12 Qard literally denotes cutting; a loan is so called because the money is cut off from its owner's property subject to repayment in kind. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Qard literally denotes cutting; a loan is so called because the money is cut off from its owner's property subject to repayment in kind. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q14/p13 Lend God a goodly loan means spend for good causes with sincere intention, as though lending in order to receive the reward later. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Lend God a goodly loan means spend for good causes with sincere intention, as though lending in order to receive the reward later. | mentions 2:245, 57:11, 57:18, 64:17 [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q14/p14 A good loan is charity given solely for God’s sake and free of reminders or injury; goodness varies by degree and by the qualities valued in each kind of charity. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A good loan is charity given solely for God’s sake and free of reminders or injury; goodness varies by degree and by the qualities valued in each kind of charity. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q14/p15 The good loan includes all rewarded acts of obedience. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn ʿĀshūr links this expression to the earlier parallels in 2:245 and 64:17, where lending God a good loan is promised multiplied reward. | mentions 2:245, 64:17 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q14/p16 Although يقرض is quadriliteral and its verbal noun is إقراض, the Quran uses the triliteral noun قرض, which can also mean money. — SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Although يقرض is quadriliteral and its verbal noun is إقراض, the Quran uses the triliteral noun قرض, which can also mean money. [1 notes: SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT]
- 73:20/q14/p17 If قرض means the act of lending, it is a cognate accusative; if it means money, it is a direct object. — SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): If قرض means the act of lending, it is a cognate accusative; if it means money, it is a direct object. [1 notes: SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT]
- 73:20/q14/p18 قرضا حسنا combines lending sincerely for God's reward with giving good, lawful money. — SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): قرضا حسنا combines lending sincerely for God's reward with giving good, lawful money. [1 notes: SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT]
- 73:20/q14/p19 Lending a goodly loan means spending effort and wealth for religion’s cause; in 5:12 and here it is linked with prayer and almsgiving. — STUDYQURAN (author): Lending a goodly loan means spending effort and wealth for religion’s cause; in 5:12 and here it is linked with prayer and almsgiving. | mentions 2:245, 5:12, 57:11, 57:18, 64:17 [1 notes: STUDYQURAN]
- 73:20/q14/p20 Lending to God means spending wealth in His cause. — TAB-FULL: Lending to God means spending wealth in His cause. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 73:20/q14/p21 Ibn Hibban takes it as four formulas of glorification, praise, unity, and magnification. — MAWARDI-FULL: Ibn Hibban takes it as four formulas of glorification, praise, unity, and magnification. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(ابن حبان)]
- 73:20/q14/p22 Mawardi includes every rewarded act of obedience. — MAWARDI-FULL: Mawardi includes every rewarded act of obedience. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 73:20/q14/p23 Muqatil says loan means willingly and cheerfully given zakat. — WAHIDI-BASIT: Muqatil says loan means willingly and cheerfully given zakat. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(مقاتل)]
- 73:20/q14/p24 Lending to God means good deeds advanced for Him. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Lending to God means good deeds advanced for Him. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]

## 73:20/q15 What good is sent ahead and what reward is promised?  [وَمَا تُقَدِّمُوا۟ لِأَنفُسِكُم مِّنْ خَيْرٍۢ تَجِدُوهُ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ · interpretation]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q15/p1 Good sent ahead is better than wealth left or deferred to death. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says good deeds stored with God are better and more rewarding than wealth deferred for a bequest at death. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عمر بن الخطاب): Umar took the promise to mean that good sent ahead is better than what is left behind, inherited, or withheld through miserliness. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): What is advanced is better and more rewarding than what is deferred to a bequest at death, or than worldly goods. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): What people find with God is the reward of their good deeds; the clause answers the conditional. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): What is sent ahead is better than what is left behind, and brings greater, more abundant reward. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عبد الله): The Prophet contrasts wealth sent ahead with wealth left to heirs: a person’s real wealth is what they have given or prepared for the afterlife, while the heir receives what was deferred. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The promised reward exceeds that of a bequest made during terminal illness, whose recompense might come in worldly life. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Whatever good a person sends ahead, whether already mentioned or not, will be found with God as a better and greater reward than what is deferred until a deathbed bequest. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (النبي عليه السلام): A Prophetic report says every person arrives according to what he sent ahead and regrets what he left behind. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (النبي عليه السلام): At death, the person asks what he left behind while the angels point to what he sent ahead. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (عمر رضي الله عنه): Passing al-Baqi cemetery, Umar greeted its dead and said their wives had remarried, homes occupied, and wealth divided; a voice replied that what was sent ahead was found, what was spent profited, and what was left behind was lost. STUDYQURAN (Ṭ): Whatever good believers send ahead for themselves comprises all their good deeds in this life. | mentions 5:80, 75:13, 82:5 STUDYQURAN (IK): Spending in God’s way during life is better than what one keeps for oneself or leaves as inheritance. [13 notes: RAZI-FULL(ابن عباس); QURTUBI-FULL(عمر بن الخطاب); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL(عبد الله); BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI(النبي عليه السلام,عمر رضي الله عنه); STUDYQURAN(IK,Ṭ)]
- 73:20/q15/p2 Good deeds sent ahead are preserved and rewarded by God. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): What people send ahead is obedience; they will find its reward with God, better than miserliness and shortcoming. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The general command covers every good deed, bodily or financial, sent ahead for the afterlife when people can no longer act; God preserves it for them. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Any good deed sent ahead will be found with God, better and more rewarding than what one leaves to bequeath at death. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Whatever good people send ahead for themselves, they will find with God as good and with a still greater reward. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Obedience sent ahead for the afterlife is found with God on the day of meeting, better than anything done or left undone and greater in reward. [5 notes: TABRISI; BIQAI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q15/p3 God multiplies the recompense for good deeds. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Alternatively, the reward is greater because each good deed is repaid tenfold. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): خير is a comparative: the reward found is better than what was sent ahead, meaning the deed’s recompense is multiplied, as 64:17 promises. | mentions 64:17 STUDYQURAN (author): The reward also alludes to the increase of good deeds in the Hereafter, as in the parable of multiplied grain in 2:261 and related verses. | mentions 2:261, 2:245, 4:40, 6:160, 10:26, 64:17 [3 notes: QURTUBI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL; STUDYQURAN]
- 73:20/q15/p4 Reward with God is better than worldly enjoyment. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj says the reward is better than worldly enjoyment; al-Razi prefers Ibn Abbas's interpretation. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (كشف الأسرار): Kashf al-Asrar says the reward found with God is better than worldly enjoyment and greater in recompense, since God rewards believers without reckoning. [2 notes: RAZI-FULL(الزجاج); BURSEVI(كشف الأسرار)]
- 73:20/q15/p5 The greater reward is Paradise. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو هريرة): Abu Hurayra says the greater reward is Paradise. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو هريرة)]
- 73:20/q15/p6 Good sent ahead includes obligatory and voluntary alms; God's reward is greater than what was given. — MUQATIL: Good sent ahead includes obligatory and voluntary alms; God's reward is greater than what was given. [1 notes: MUQATIL]
