## 73:20/q16 How is 'huwa khayran wa-aʿẓama ajran' read and parsed?  [هُوَ خَيْرًۭا وَأَعْظَمَ أَجْرًۭا · grammar]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q16/p1 Huwa may be an emphatic separator, called faṣl by Basrans and ʿimād by Kufans, or an adjective of the object pronoun. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Huwa may be an emphatic separator, called faṣl by Basrans and ʿimād by Kufans, or an adjective of the object pronoun. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 73:20/q16/p2 Al-Zajjaj calls huwa an intervening separator, and the commentators understand khayran as better and more rewarding than what is deferred until a deathbed bequest. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj calls huwa an intervening separator, and the commentators understand khayran as better and more rewarding than what is deferred until a deathbed bequest. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الزجاج)]
- 73:20/q16/p3 The accusative khayran adds emphasis and intensification; Abu al-Sammal instead reads khayrun nominative, as a predicate construction. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The accusative khayran adds emphasis and intensification; Abu al-Sammal instead reads khayrun nominative, as a predicate construction. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p4 أجرا is an accusative of specification (tamyiz). — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): أجرا is an accusative of specification (tamyiz). [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p5 خيرا and أعظم are the second objects of تجدوه; هو is a separating word for Basran grammarians and an ʿimād support word for Kufans, with no syntactic case. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): خيرا and أعظم are the second objects of تجدوه; هو is a separating word for Basran grammarians and an ʿimād support word for Kufans, with no syntactic case. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p6 Khayran is a second object of tajidūhu and either an intensifier or separator, since afʿal min is definite-like and resists the article; a reading has huwa khayrun as a nominal clause. — BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Khayran is a second object of tajidūhu and either an intensifier or separator, since afʿal min is definite-like and resists the article; a reading has huwa khayrun as a nominal clause. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p7 “He is” is an inflectional separator; it is allowed although not between two definite nouns because the comparative resembles a definite noun, being unable to take the article. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): “He is” is an inflectional separator; it is allowed although not between two definite nouns because the comparative resembles a definite noun, being unable to take the article. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p8 The majority read better and greater in reward in the accusative; huwa may be a separator or emphasize the object pronoun in you find it. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الجمهور): The majority read better and greater in reward in the accusative; huwa may be a separator or emphasize the object pronoun in you find it. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الجمهور)]
- 73:20/q16/p9 Al-Zamakhshari, al-Hawfi, and Ibn Atiyya mention only the separator analysis; Abu al-Baqa also allows apposition or emphasis, but Abū Ḥayyān rejects apposition because it would require accusative iyyāhu. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الزمخشري والحوفي وابن عطية): Al-Zamakhshari, al-Hawfi, and Ibn Atiyya mention only the separator analysis; Abu al-Baqa also allows apposition or emphasis, but Abū Ḥayyān rejects apposition because it would require accusative iyyāhu. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري والحوفي وابن عطية)]
- 73:20/q16/p10 Abu al-Sammal and Abu al-Sumayfiʿ read the two words nominative; Abu Zayd says this construction after a separator is the dialect of Banu Tamim. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (أبو زيد): Abu al-Sammal and Abu al-Sumayfiʿ read the two words nominative; Abu Zayd says this construction after a separator is the dialect of Banu Tamim. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو زيد)]
- 73:20/q16/p11 Abū ʿAmr al-Jarmī says Sībawayh cited the line about being stronger than Laylā as an example of a nominative predicate; another transmission has the final word as أقدر. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (أبو عمرو الجرمي): Abū ʿAmr al-Jarmī says Sībawayh cited the line about being stronger than Laylā as an example of a nominative predicate; another transmission has the final word as أقدر. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو عمرو الجرمي)]
- 73:20/q16/p12 Al-Zamakhsharī treats هو as a separator even though it is not between two definite nouns, because an elative adjective is close to a definite expression. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī treats هو as a separator even though it is not between two definite nouns, because an elative adjective is close to a definite expression. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري)]
- 73:20/q16/p13 The common reading has خيرا accusative as the second object of تجدوه; هو is either emphatic for the first object or a separator. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The common reading has خيرا accusative as the second object of تجدوه; هو is either emphatic for the first object or a separator. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 73:20/q16/p14 Abu al-Sammal and Ibn al-Sumayfa read خير nominative, making هو the subject and خير its predicate; the clause is the second object of تجدوه. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (أبو السمال): Abu al-Sammal and Ibn al-Sumayfa read خير nominative, making هو the subject and خير its predicate; the clause is the second object of تجدوه. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR(أبو السمال)]
- 73:20/q16/p15 Abu Zayd says raising the word after a separator is Tamimi usage; Sibawayh's line has أقدر raised, though an accusative variant is also transmitted. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (أبو زيد): Abu Zayd says raising the word after a separator is Tamimi usage; Sibawayh's line has أقدر raised, though an accusative variant is also transmitted. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR(أبو زيد)]
- 73:20/q16/p16 Al-Zamakhshari parses هو as a separator even between indefinite elements because أفعل من resembles a definite in resisting the article; al-Samin notes others allow this with other indefinites too. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari parses هو as a separator even between indefinite elements because أفعل من resembles a definite in resisting the article; al-Samin notes others allow this with other indefinites too. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR(الزمخشري)]
- 73:20/q16/p17 A separator may occur between the indefinite terms because the comparative form afʿal min functions like a definite expression and does not take the definite article. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): A separator may occur between the indefinite terms because the comparative form afʿal min functions like a definite expression and does not take the definite article. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p18 On the main parsing, khayran is the second object of تجدوا after the pronoun object; aʿẓama coordinates with it, and ajran is a specification of the subject relation. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): On the main parsing, khayran is the second object of تجدوا after the pronoun object; aʿẓama coordinates with it, and ajran is a specification of the subject relation. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q16/p19 The accusative reading fits the earlier choice among full half, one-third, and a greater amount below two-thirds; the genitive reading fits a choice among half, third, and quarter. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The accusative reading fits the earlier choice among full half, one-third, and a greater amount below two-thirds; the genitive reading fits a choice among half, third, and quarter. | mentions 73:4 [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p20 “هو” may emphasize the object pronoun in “تجدوه,” serve as a separator despite the following comparative being definite in sense, or be an apposition; Abū Ḥayyān calls the apposition analysis mistaken because it should be “إياه.” — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): “هو” may emphasize the object pronoun in “تجدوه,” serve as a separator despite the following comparative being definite in sense, or be an apposition; Abū Ḥayyān calls the apposition analysis mistaken because it should be “إياه.” [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p21 The passage attributes variants to Abū al-Sammāl al-ʿAdawī and al-Ghanawī, and to Abū al-Sumayfa, who reads “هو خير وأعظم” with both words nominative. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (أبو السمال وأبو السميفع): The passage attributes variants to Abū al-Sammāl al-ʿAdawī and al-Ghanawī, and to Abū al-Sumayfa, who reads “هو خير وأعظم” with both words nominative. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(أبو السمال وأبو السميفع)]
- 73:20/q16/p22 Abū Zayd says Banū Tamīm raise the words after a separator, construing the phrase as a new nominal sentence whose subject and predicate form the second object. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (أبو زيد): Abū Zayd says Banū Tamīm raise the words after a separator, construing the phrase as a new nominal sentence whose subject and predicate form the second object. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(أبو زيد)]
- 73:20/q16/p23 The cited verse by Qays ibn Dhurayḥ illustrates the Banū Tamīm use of a nominative phrase after a separator. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The cited verse by Qays ibn Dhurayḥ illustrates the Banū Tamīm use of a nominative phrase after a separator. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p24 طائفة is nominative, coordinated with إن’s noun after its predicate; this is permitted by treating it as a new clause, as in 9:3. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): طائفة is nominative, coordinated with إن’s noun after its predicate; this is permitted by treating it as a new clause, as in 9:3. | mentions 9:3 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p25 أن is the lightened form of الثقيلة, with an omitted pronoun of شأن and a sentential predicate; the negative particle separates it from its verbal predicate in standard usage. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): أن is the lightened form of الثقيلة, with an omitted pronoun of شأن and a sentential predicate; the negative particle separates it from its verbal predicate in standard usage. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p26 The clause may be a second predicate of إن after the vigil clause, or an explanatory resumption answering the listener’s expectation; after commending them, God eases their burden. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The clause may be a second predicate of إن after the vigil clause, or an explanatory resumption answering the listener’s expectation; after commending them, God eases their burden. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p27 وأعظم أجرا either coordinates with خيرا, extending the separating pronoun’s emphasis, or independently carries that emphasis; أجرا is an accusative specification of أعظم. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): وأعظم أجرا either coordinates with خيرا, extending the separating pronoun’s emphasis, or independently carries that emphasis; أجرا is an accusative specification of أعظم. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p28 The pronoun in تجدوه is the first object of تجدوا and خيرا is its second object. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The pronoun in تجدوه is the first object of تجدوا and خيرا is its second object. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p29 Huwa is either a separator pronoun or an emphatic repetition of the object pronoun in tajiduhu. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Huwa is either a separator pronoun or an emphatic repetition of the object pronoun in tajiduhu. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q16/p30 The same separating-pronoun analysis applies to huwa khayran wa-a'zama ajran. — SIBAWAYH: The same separating-pronoun analysis applies to huwa khayran wa-a'zama ajran. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 73:20/q16/p31 The phrase means finding the good with God; huwa can be omitted. — MAJAZ: The phrase means finding the good with God; huwa can be omitted. [1 notes: MAJAZ]
- 73:20/q16/p32 Khayran is the second object, huwa separator; accusative is best Arabic and required Quranic reading. — ZAJJAJ: Khayran is the second object, huwa separator; accusative is best Arabic and required Quranic reading. [1 notes: ZAJJAJ]
- 73:20/q16/p33 Good found is what was spent; khayran is predicate and huwa extra separator. — NAHHAS: Good found is what was spent; khayran is predicate and huwa extra separator. [1 notes: NAHHAS]
- 73:20/q16/p34 Khayran and a'zama are second objects; Basrans call huwa separator and Kufans support. — THALABI: Khayran and a'zama are second objects; Basrans call huwa separator and Kufans support. [1 notes: THALABI]
- 73:20/q16/p35 Khayran and a'zama are accusative objects; Basran and Kufan grammar terms differ. — JISHUMI: Khayran and a'zama are accusative objects; Basran and Kufan grammar terms differ. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 73:20/q16/p36 Khayran and a'zama are second objects; Basrans and Kufans differ in term for huwa. — BAGHAWI-FULL: Khayran and a'zama are second objects; Basrans and Kufans differ in term for huwa. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p37 Khayran is second object and huwa separator despite indefinite due to comparative resisting article. — KASHSHAF-FULL: Khayran is second object and huwa separator despite indefinite due to comparative resisting article. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 73:20/q16/p38 Abu al-Sammal reads nominative huwa khayrun wa-a'zamu ajran. — KASHSHAF-FULL: Abu al-Sammal reads nominative huwa khayrun wa-a'zamu ajran. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL(أبو السمال)]
- 73:20/q16/p39 Majority read huwa khayran as pronoun of separation. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Majority read huwa khayran as pronoun of separation. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(جمهور الناس)]
- 73:20/q16/p40 Ibn Sumayfi and Abu al-Sammal read nominative huwa khayrun, nominal clause as second object. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Ibn Sumayfi and Abu al-Sammal read nominative huwa khayrun, nominal clause as second object. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(محمد بن السميفع وأبو السمال)]

## 73:20/q17 What does the command to seek forgiveness call for?  [وَٱسْتَغْفِرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ · interpretation]
- 73:20/q17/p1 Believers should seek forgiveness for sins and shortcomings. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): God turned toward them with forgiveness and lightened the requirement. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The command seeks forgiveness for sins and shortcomings, especially those in night vigil. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (مقاتل): The alternative view takes God's forgiveness here to concern those who persist in sin; Muqatil argues this better establishes praise than a promise only to the repentant. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The command means to ask God to forgive one's sins. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Seek God's forgiveness in all circumstances because no person is free of shortcomings. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Ask forgiveness for sins and for falling short in good works. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Seek God’s forgiveness often in every affair, since He forgives and shows mercy to those who ask. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The sūra closes with seeking forgiveness for the disbelievers' earlier resistance and for what obedient believers fail to fulfill, as 73:20 indicates. | mentions 73:20 BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Calling the relief repentance does not imply a prior sin; the heavy obligation could have led people into sin. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The surah's ending returns to its opening: encouragement to act and its reward recall the command to stand before God, while seeking forgiveness stresses the greatness of His station. | mentions 73:2 BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): End deeds by seeking forgiveness: even praised worship can produce self-admiration, and no one can render God His due, so servants must acknowledge shortcomings. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Seek God's forgiveness continually, at every time and in every condition, because people seldom escape negligence or shortcoming. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (السلف الصالح): The righteous early Muslims prayed until dawn, then sat seeking forgiveness until the dawn prayer. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): He recommends formulas of forgiveness drawn from Quranic divine names, including 'I ask forgiveness of God, for God is Oft-Returning' and 'O Lord, forgive and show mercy.' BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Knowing that God's forgiveness is not daunted by any sin should make a person ask forgiveness more often; the request is sound with humility, complete with repentance, and empty without either. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): He says writing the master formula of forgiveness and giving it to someone struggling to die will loosen the person's tongue and ease death, a remedy he says has been tried repeatedly. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): He gives the master formula: acknowledge God as Lord and creator, affirm servitude and covenant, seek refuge from one's deeds, confess favors and sin, and ask forgiveness from God alone. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Seek forgiveness in every state, since people rarely avoid shortcomings relative to their own standing. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): It is suggested that this is why the command to seek forgiveness follows the commands to pray, give zakat, and lend God a good loan. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Omitting the object of “forgiving” and “merciful” indicates that the forgiveness and mercy extend generally. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Believers should seek God's forgiveness in all circumstances because He is forgiving and merciful and grants the supplicant success through His mercy. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The closing supplication asks God to forgive and show mercy to the author and the believers who preceded them in faith, by the sanctity of what He revealed to His Messenger. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): When تاب means accepting repentance, it takes على by implication of مِن; when it means turning away from and regretting sin, it takes a preposition suited to that sense. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The waw begins an explanatory resumption: after easing night prayer, God directs believers to seek forgiveness in the night’s late hours as another devotional practice. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Late-night forgiveness seeking accords with 51:18 and with two Prophetic reports about the last third of the night and waking from sleep. | mentions 51:18 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet reports that each night, in its final third, God calls for people to pray, ask, or seek forgiveness, promising response, giving, and forgiveness. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says that one who wakes at night, recites the stated remembrance, and then asks forgiveness or supplicates will have the request answered. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): God’s great forgiveness and mercy explain the command to seek forgiveness and encourage it as likely to be answered; the intensive descriptions hint at that promise. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): A distinct usage is God's return to people after their repentance, whose effect is forgiveness of their sins. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The closing command is to seek God's forgiveness; His description as forgiving and merciful intimates a promise of pardon and mercy. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The author allows that seeking forgiveness may mean performing all acts of obedience, since they are means to God's forgiveness. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (علي): Ali's hadith of the four hundred advises frequent seeking of forgiveness to attract provision and advancing whatever good one can; its final phrase derives from 73:20. | mentions 73:20 ISLAHI-TADABBUR (author): Alongside the other duties, believers should seek forgiveness for their faults and hope for God’s forgiveness and mercy. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Since people cannot avoid mistakes despite their care in worldly life, the sūra ends by commanding them to ask God's forgiveness. MUQATIL: Seek forgiveness for sins; God forgives askers and grants the concession mercifully. TAB-FULL: Ask God to forgive sins. NAHHAS: Seek forgiveness for sins and shortcomings; God forgives repentant people. SULAMI: Seek forgiveness in every circumstance. MAWARDI-FULL: Seek forgiveness for sins. WAHIDI-BASIT: Seek forgiveness for sins. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: Ask forgiveness; God forgives believers and shows mercy. JISHUMI: Seeking forgiveness and repentance are always obligatory due to common shortcomings. BAGHAWI-FULL: Ask forgiveness for sins; God forgives and shows mercy. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Some derive forgiveness seeking after prayer from verse and 51:17. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Author's father sought forgiveness three times after each obligatory prayer, citing hadith. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Post-prayer forgiveness repairs thoughts and shortcomings in prayer. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Early pious prayed to dawn then sought forgiveness until morning prayer. [47 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL(مقاتل); QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI(السلف الصالح); ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); TABATABAI(علي); ISLAHI-TADABBUR; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; MUQATIL; TAB-FULL; NAHHAS; SULAMI; MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT; WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(السلف الصالح,القاضي أبو محمد,بعض العلماء)]

## 73:20/q18 What do God's forgiveness and mercy entail?  [إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ غَفُورٌۭ رَّحِيمٌۢ · theology]
- 73:20/q18/p1 God forgives and shows mercy to those who seek Him. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Seeking forgiveness means asking God to forgive; He covers sins, pardons, and shows mercy. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (مقاتل): Muqatil holds that God is forgiving of all sins, including those of persistent sinners; the wording includes both repentant and persistent sinners, and forgiveness limited to penitents would be an obligatory act rather than praise. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The final description means God forgives believers' sins and is merciful to them. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (سعيد بن جبير): Sa'id ibn Jubayr says God forgives what preceded repentance and shows mercy to people afterward. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): God is forgiving and merciful. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): God is merciful by easing matters for those who exert themselves in worship. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): God's forgiveness covers sins and their effects until no blame or punishment remains; His mercy then adds generous honor and favor. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Ghafur forgives sins other than associating partners with God, while Rahim turns bad deeds into good deeds. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (عين المعاني): Ayn al-Maʻani glosses Ghafur as covering the failings of the ignorant and negligent, and Rahim as easing their burden. TAB-FULL: God forgives repentant servants and mercifully does not punish them. MAWARDI-FULL: God forgives what preceded repentance. MAWARDI-FULL: Sa'id says God shows mercy after repentance. WAHIDI-BASIT: Muqatil says God forgives and has mercy on believers. WAHIDI-BASIT: Ibn Abbas says God forgives those not persisting in sin. [14 notes: TABRISI; RAZI-FULL(مقاتل); QURTUBI-FULL(سعيد بن جبير); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI(عين المعاني); TAB-FULL; MAWARDI-FULL(سعيد بن جبير); WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن عباس,مقاتل)]

## 73:20/q19 When and where was this closing passage revealed?  [* · sciences]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q19/p1 The closing passage was revealed in Mecca. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Another view says prayer and zakat were already obligatory in Mecca, so the verse is Meccan. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar found a Meccan youth who regularly attended the mosque selling food; he urged him toward livestock, saying food sellers welcome scarcity while herders welcome rain. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The dual and third fractions are accusative for Makkan and Kufan readers, linked to adnā; those reading them genitive link them to “two thirds.” IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): God foreknows that illness, travel for livelihood, and fighting in His path will excuse some Muslims from night vigil; since this Meccan surah preceded legislation of fighting, the prediction is a sign of prophethood. ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن الغرس): Ibn al-Ghars reports a view that the passage beginning “Your Lord knows” through the end of al-Muzzammil is Meccan. | mentions 73:20 ITQAN, d. 911 (author): Al-Suyuti rejects the view that the ending is Meccan, citing Aisha's report that it was revealed later when night prayer was made obligatory. | mentions 73:20 ITQAN, d. 911 (author): Al-Suyuti prefers the agreed Meccan example: the night prayer commanded at the start of al-Muzzammil was abrogated by its end or by the five daily prayers. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAtiyya says the majority considered the sura Makkan except 73:20 to its end; al-Qurtubi attributes the same view to al-Thaʿlabi. | mentions 73:20 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (صاحب الإتقان): The Itqan author cites al-Hakim from ʿAʾisha: the ending was revealed a year after the opening, when night vigil had been imposed before the five prayers, implying the whole sura was Makkan. | mentions 73:20 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (عائشة): Some of ʿAʾisha's reports imply the entire sura is Makkan and its ending came a year after its opening, before obligatory prayer, as transmitted by al-Hakim via the Itqan author. | mentions 73:20 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Early authorities differed over when and where this verse was revealed and how its ruling relates to the preceding verse; the trusted view is that the opening was Makkan. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Reports place this Meccan verse eight months, a year, or ten years after the sura's opening verses. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The claim that Meccan zakat was imposed without specifying thresholds is unsupported. [11 notes: TABRISI; QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عمر); NASAFI-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL; ITQAN(ابن الغرس); IBNASHUR-FULL(ابن عطية,صاحب الإتقان,عائشة); TABATABAI; ITQAN+; ITQAN-; TABATABAI-]
- 73:20/q19/p2 The closing passage was revealed in Medina. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Since the Prophet was present when this verse was revealed, the travelers were not leaving him for instruction; if the surah's ending is Medinan, they could instead be migrating to Medina for learning. The rule also serves later generations. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (من فسرها بالزكاة المفروضة): Those who read this as obligatory zakat regard the sura's final passage as Medinan, treating its ruling as one whose legislation followed its revelation. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (أبو حيان): Some scholars call the verse Medinan; Abu Hayyan describes that as the majority position, but Elmalılı says it does not appear to be the majority view. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Elmalılı considers the mention of fighting a clearer sign that at least one or two clauses are Medinan: permission to fight was agreed to be Medinan, and even the prediction of fighters preparatorily signals that permission and its abrogating circumstances. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The author prefers a Medinan date for 73:20 onward because it mentions fighting in God's path, unless this predicts the unseen miraculously. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): That account implies a wholly Medinan sura: ʿAʾisha lived with the Prophet in Medina, and his angry exit suggests he left his home for its adjoining mosque; other reports support mosque prayer. | mentions 73:20 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The discussion from this point concerns the portion of the sura revealed in Medina, as stated in the introduction. | mentions 73:20 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The author rejects al-Tabari's report from Abu Salama ibn ʿAbd al-Rahman from ʿAʾisha, and the similar report from al-Nakhaʿi about the blanket, as evidence that the opening was Medinan. | mentions 73:1 TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Prayer followed by obligatory zakat and distinguished from voluntary charity suggests zakat here is obligatory, which was instituted in Medina after the Hijra. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Mentioning fighting would not suit the early mission's circumstances, so the verse is apparently Medinan; some others also favor this dating. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (many commentators): Many commentators infer that all of verse 20 was revealed at Medina because fighting in God's cause was sanctioned only after the Hijrah. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (Ibn Kathir): Ibn Kathir reads the future-tense wording as a prediction of later circumstances, the interpretation Asad adopts against the Medinan dating. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): Asad rejects the dating inference as unwarranted: the future tense predicts later circumstances, although jihad was first sanctioned in Medina. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (some authorities): Some authorities call verse 20 Medinan; Asad says this claim has no substance. ISLAHI-TADABBUR (author): Though its subject indicates a Madinan revelation, the final verse returns to the night-prayer directive at the sūrah’s beginning, linking its opening and close; this arrangement reflects guidance from God and His Prophet. | mentions 73:1 STUDYQURAN (some): Some infer from the mention of fighting that this verse dates from the Medinan period, since fighting began there. [12 notes: BURSEVI(من فسرها بالزكاة المفروضة); IBNASHUR-FULL; ASAD-NOTES(many commentators); ISLAHI-TADABBUR; STUDYQURAN(some); ASAD-NOTES(Ibn Kathir)+; ELMALILI+; IBNASHUR-FULL+; TABATABAI+; ELMALILI(أبو حيان)-; IBNASHUR-FULL-; ASAD-NOTES(some authorities)-]
- 73:20/q19/p3 One view says the verse is Medinan because prayer and zakat were not made obligatory in Mecca. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): One view says the verse is Medinan because prayer and zakat were not made obligatory in Mecca. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 73:20/q19/p4 The obligatory alms are understood as zakat; another view takes the reference as zakat al-fitr, since formal zakat was not yet prescribed in Mecca, and those reading it as obligatory zakat infer that the surah's ending… — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The obligatory alms are understood as zakat; another view takes the reference as zakat al-fitr, since formal zakat was not yet prescribed in Mecca, and those reading it as obligatory zakat infer that the surah's ending is Medinan. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 73:20/q19/p5 Some say the duty lasted beyond the Hijra and was abrogated in Medina, since fighting was prescribed there; on that account the Meccan prayer-time verses preceded abrogation by 17:79. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Some say the duty lasted beyond the Hijra and was abrogated in Medina, since fighting was prescribed there; on that account the Meccan prayer-time verses preceded abrogation by 17:79. | mentions 17:79 [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 73:20/q19/p6 Ibn Kathir says the route through Musa ibn Ubayda is weak, and the sound hadith omits the added claim that this surah was revealed then; the report’s wording may suggest a Medinan surah, but it is… — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir says the route through Musa ibn Ubayda is weak, and the sound hadith omits the added claim that this surah was revealed then; the report’s wording may suggest a Medinan surah, but it is Meccan, and its eight-month interval conflicts with Ahmad’s year report. | mentions 73:20 [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q19/p7 Al-Mulk, al-Qalam, al-Haqqa, al-Ma'arij, Nuh, and al-Jinn are Meccan; al-Muzzammil is excepted for two Medinan verses, while al-Muddaththir through its end is Meccan. — ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Al-Mulk, al-Qalam, al-Haqqa, al-Ma'arij, Nuh, and al-Jinn are Meccan; al-Muzzammil is excepted for two Medinan verses, while al-Muddaththir through its end is Meccan. | mentions 73:20 [1 notes: ITQAN(ابن عباس)]
- 73:20/q19/p8 Al-Hakim transmits from Aisha that the closing passage was revealed a year after the start of the surah, when night prayer was prescribed early in Islam. — ITQAN, d. 911 (عائشة): Al-Hakim transmits from Aisha that the closing passage was revealed a year after the start of the surah, when night prayer was prescribed early in Islam. | mentions 73:20 [1 notes: ITQAN(عائشة)]
- 73:20/q19/p9 This reading raises a chronology problem: the sūra is among the earliest Meccan revelations, the five prayers were prescribed after the Night Journey, and zakat was prescribed in Medina. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): This reading raises a chronology problem: the sūra is among the earliest Meccan revelations, the five prayers were prescribed after the Night Journey, and zakat was prescribed in Medina. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q19/p10 One answer treats these verses as Medinan; another says zakat was already generally prescribed in Mecca while its thresholds were fixed in Medina, allowing a Meccan date after the Night Journey. A delayed ruling is also… — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): One answer treats these verses as Medinan; another says zakat was already generally prescribed in Mecca while its thresholds were fixed in Medina, allowing a Meccan date after the Night Journey. A delayed ruling is also suggested but deemed weak. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q19/p11 There is agreement that this verse came later than the opening night-vigil command and softened or adjusted it; scholars differ about whether it was revealed in Mecca or Medina and how the easing or abrogation worked. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): There is agreement that this verse came later than the opening night-vigil command and softened or adjusted it; scholars differ about whether it was revealed in Mecca or Medina and how the easing or abrogation worked. | mentions 73:2 [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q19/p12 Because no other Meccan verse explicitly mentions fighting, this clue seems clearer than zakat; Elmalılı concludes that at least some sentences of the verse must be Medinan, even if not the whole verse. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Because no other Meccan verse explicitly mentions fighting, this clue seems clearer than zakat; Elmalılı concludes that at least some sentences of the verse must be Medinan, even if not the whole verse. [1 notes: ELMALILI+]
- 73:20/q19/p13 The Medinan dating is sometimes inferred from the zakat command; Elmalılı replies that Medina fixed zakat’s recipients and shares, not zakat’s basic obligation, since Meccan sūras also mention it. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The Medinan dating is sometimes inferred from the zakat command; Elmalılı replies that Medina fixed zakat’s recipients and shares, not zakat’s basic obligation, since Meccan sūras also mention it. | mentions 9:60 [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q19/p14 Saʿid ibn Jubayr reports that the Prophet and a group of companions prayed at night for ten years after 73:1; 73:20 through “establish prayer” then came after ten years, hence in Medina if his Meccan stay… — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿid ibn Jubayr reports that the Prophet and a group of companions prayed at night for ten years after 73:1; 73:20 through “establish prayer” then came after ten years, hence in Medina if his Meccan stay lasted ten years. | mentions 73:1, 73:20 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(سعيد بن جبير)]
- 73:20/q19/p15 Ibn ʿAbbas recounts staying with his aunt Maymuna: the Prophet awoke around midnight, performed ablutions, prayed thirteen rakʿas, then slept until the dawn caller; Ibn ʿAbbas was a boy, dating it to about 7 or 8… — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas recounts staying with his aunt Maymuna: the Prophet awoke around midnight, performed ablutions, prayed thirteen rakʿas, then slept until the dawn caller; Ibn ʿAbbas was a boy, dating it to about 7 or 8 AH. | mentions 73:20 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 73:20/q19/p16 After the Hijra, the Prophet’s duties in governing, defending Medina, and preparing armies left less daytime leisure than in Mecca, explaining the easing of obligatory and recommended night prayer. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): After the Hijra, the Prophet’s duties in governing, defending Medina, and preparing armies left less daytime leisure than in Mecca, explaining the easing of obligatory and recommended night prayer. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q19/p17 The Quran meant here is what had been revealed before this Medinan verse: all the Meccan Quran and some Medinan revelation; it differs from the earlier command to recite with measured cadence. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The Quran meant here is what had been revealed before this Medinan verse: all the Meccan Quran and some Medinan revelation; it differs from the earlier command to recite with measured cadence. | mentions 73:4 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q19/p18 If this verse was revealed in Mecca, it foretells the Muslims’ coming independence and ability against their enemies; if Medinan, it excuses the raids and campaigns they had begun. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): If this verse was revealed in Mecca, it foretells the Muslims’ coming independence and ability against their enemies; if Medinan, it excuses the raids and campaigns they had begun. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q19/p19 The prayer to be established is obligatory: if the verse is Medinan, it is the five daily prayers; if Meccan, it is whatever prayers were then prescribed. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The prayer to be established is obligatory: if the verse is Medinan, it is the five daily prayers; if Meccan, it is whatever prayers were then prescribed. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q19/p20 Commentators usually identify the prayer here as the five daily prayers and zakat as obligatory alms; the authors reject that dating because both formal duties became obligatory years later. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (müfessirler): Commentators usually identify the prayer here as the five daily prayers and zakat as obligatory alms; the authors reject that dating because both formal duties became obligatory years later. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(müfessirler)]
- 73:20/q19/p21 Some say payment of alms was required in the Makkan period, while its detailed rules were established only in the Madinan period. — STUDYQURAN (some): Some say payment of alms was required in the Makkan period, while its detailed rules were established only in the Madinan period. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN(some)]
- 73:20/q19/p22 Probably revealed about twelve months after verses 1–8, this verse removed the night vigil’s obligatory status and made it voluntary. — STUDYQURAN (author): Probably revealed about twelve months after verses 1–8, this verse removed the night vigil’s obligatory status and made it voluntary. | mentions 73:1, 73:2, 73:3, 73:4, 73:5, 73:6, 73:7, 73:8 [1 notes: STUDYQURAN]
- 73:20/q19/p23 Ata al-Khurasani says Meccan night command was abrogated by the sura's Medinan ending. — WAHIDI-BASIT: Ata al-Khurasani says Meccan night command was abrogated by the sura's Medinan ending. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(عطاء الخراساني)]
- 73:20/q19/p24 Muqatil says this allowance came in Mecca before the five prayers. — WAHIDI-BASIT: Muqatil says this allowance came in Mecca before the five prayers. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(مقاتل)]
- 73:20/q19/p25 Qadi reports Meccan, Medinan, and mixed sura datings; Hamza excepts this ending from Meccan. — JISHUMI: Qadi reports Meccan, Medinan, and mixed sura datings; Hamza excepts this ending from Meccan. [1 notes: JISHUMI(القاضي)]
- 73:20/q19/p26 Hamza says sura is Meccan except this ending. — JISHUMI: Hamza says sura is Meccan except this ending. [1 notes: JISHUMI(حمزة)]
- 73:20/q19/p27 If wealth zakat is intended, ending is Medinan. — KASHSHAF-FULL: If wealth zakat is intended, ending is Medinan. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 73:20/q19/p28 Ibn Kaysan and Muqatil say command was Meccan before five prayers. — TABRISI: Ibn Kaysan and Muqatil say command was Meccan before five prayers. [1 notes: TABRISI(ابن كيسان ومقاتل)]

## 73:20/q20 How does the passage change the earlier night vigil duty?  [* · sciences]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q20/p1 The night vigil obligation ended and the practice became voluntary. — TABRISI, d. 548 (الجبائي): Al-Jubbāʾī says God made night prayer voluntary rather than obligatory. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The stated reason did not apply to the Prophet, whose daytime remained spacious according to 73:7, so the night-vigil obligation was not abrogated for him. | mentions 73:7 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Some scholars say this command abrogated the fixed night standing, including its half-night amount and permitted increase or decrease. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (بعض العلماء): Those scholars offer two possibilities: this command imposed a new duty replacing the old one, or it was itself later abrogated by 17:79, where night vigil is called a voluntary extra for the Prophet. | mentions 17:79 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الشافعي): Al-Shafi'i held that the obligation of night prayer was abrogated altogether, so it is not obligatory. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (قوم): Some held that the complete abrogation also covered the Prophet, and that calling the prayer a nafl means a genuinely voluntary act. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas said that when the Messenger arrived, the verse ended the obligation of night prayer. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (ابن عباس، وعكرمة، ومجاهد، والحسن، وقتادة): These early authorities say the final verse abrogated the original obligation of night vigil; reports differ on how long that first obligation lasted. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): God lifted the earlier obligation of precisely measured night vigil and licensed people to leave it without blame. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Recitation stands for prayer because it is prayer's greatest pillar, indicating that night vigil is recommended rather than obligatory. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (قتادة): Qatada says night vigil was initially obligatory; Companions stood until their feet swelled. After a year this passage abrogated that duty, while prayer and zakat remained obligatory without concession. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عكرمة): Ikrima says they continued for a year, suffering hardship and swollen feet, until the end of the sūra abrogated the initial night-vigil command. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Night vigil was initially obligatory under the stated options; because it became difficult, this verse abrogated its fixed measure with an easier allowance. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (قيل): A proposed answer says the original order was for the lesser amount, while the companions voluntarily exceeded it as a precaution; God knew the hardship and then abrogated that command, but the author says the issue still needs examination. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): On the literal recitation reading, God permits leaving all night prayer but commands some Qur'an recitation at night as an easier practice with the reward of vigil; this command is recommended rather than obligatory. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Some say the duty remained obligatory while the amount was optional, then was abrogated after ten years; al-Kalbi says a man might pray until dawn for fear of misjudging the interval. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (غير واحد): Some hold the vigil was voluntary, citing the choice of amounts and 17:79, which calls night prayer a special additional devotion for the Prophet. | mentions 17:79 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās reports that night prayer ceased to be obligatory for the Prophet’s companions and became voluntary, while remaining obligatory for the Prophet. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Bir kısım müfessirîn): Some say this meant the evening and night prayers, with the obligation of tahajjud lifted and left voluntary and discretionary. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): If this first command means prayer, its obligation too is abrogated by the later recitation and establish-prayer commands. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (عائشة): A report from Aisha says the verse came eight months after the opening command, restored the originally near-obligatory night vigil to its basic prescribed amount, abrogated the extra burden, and left the excess voluntary. | mentions 73:2 ELMALILI, d. 1361 (الحسن): Hasan reports that Muslims kept night vigil for a year, with some able to do so and others unable, until the concession came; after an obligation, the vigil became voluntary worship. | mentions 73:2 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (العلماء): Scholars address the difficulty in the seven-ahruf hadith through two approaches: some regard it as abrogated, others as still operative. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Night prayer is established as recommended for Muslims; scholars disagree whether its obligation continued for the Prophet, a question the author says need not be pursued. | mentions 73:20 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas says only 73:20 abrogated the night vigil. | mentions 73:20 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Night prayer was not made obligatory, or its obligation was lifted, because widespread excuses make it burdensome; a general presumption of hardship can remove obligation where individuals otherwise might receive only temporary exemptions. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The juxtaposition suggests that the five obligatory prayers discharge believers’ duty, while night prayer is voluntary and greatly beneficial; many transmitted reports support this. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Night vigil is recommended for other believers, although the address to the Prophet appears obligatory. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (القوم): Commentators also disagree whether the easing abrogated the earlier ruling or changed the commanded prayer into reciting what is manageable. ABUDAWUD (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās says the command to stand most of the night in 73:2–3 was abrogated by 73:20’s concession to recite what is easy. | mentions 73:2, 73:3 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Illness, travel for livelihood or other purposes, and jihad for defense, freedom, or independence bring divine ease; night prayer becomes recommended rather than obligatory. [31 notes: TABRISI(الجبائي); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عباس,الشافعي,بعض العلماء,قوم); IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن عباس، وعكرمة، ومجاهد، والحسن، وقتادة); BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(عكرمة,قتادة); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(ابن عباس,بعضهم,غير واحد,قيل); ELMALILI(Bir kısım müfessirîn,الحسن,عائشة); IBNASHUR-FULL(ابن عباس,العلماء); TABATABAI(القوم); ABUDAWUD(ابن عباس); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 73:20/q20/p2 The five daily prayers superseded the earlier night vigil duty. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (المفسرون): The commentators say the night vigil requirement for Muslims was abrogated by the five daily prayers. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (آخرون): Others say night vigil was abrogated in stages: first this verse replaced its obligation with an easier amount, then the five daily prayers replaced that duty. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الشافعي): Al-Shāfiʿī says Sunnah must resolve the two possibilities and finds that the Prophet’s Sunnah establishes no obligatory prayer beyond the five daily prayers. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The command means to pray whatever is possible: an accessible amount of night prayer was first required, then abrogated when the five daily prayers were prescribed. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (قيل): One reported account says the earlier option made night vigil obligatory, this verse abrogated that obligation when it became difficult, and the five daily prayers later abrogated this ruling. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): This command abrogated the first night-prayer command, and the five daily prayers later abrogated it. | mentions 73:2 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (قيل): One view says this command abrogated the earlier one, and both were later abrogated by the five daily prayers. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): This ending relaxed the prescribed night portions while retaining night prayer as obligatory as possible; the obligation itself was later superseded by the five daily prayers, since adding duties beyond five is an addition. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): On the prayer reading, God first required a specified portion of night vigil, then an unspecified portion; both commands were obligatory, before the five daily prayers abrogated the obligation for the community. | mentions 73:2 ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (مقاتل وابن كيسان): Muqatil and Ibn Kaysan say tahajjud was obligatory in Mecca before the five prayers, then abrogated by them except for voluntary prayer; al-Bukhari and Muslim report this from Jabir. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī prefers the view that some night prayer was first obligatory, then its obligation for the community was abrogated by the five daily prayers; it remains Sunnah for them. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The verse reduces the severity and amount of the opening night-vigil duty; after the five daily prayers became obligatory, evening and night prayers remained within the night vigil, while tahajjud changed from obligatory to recommended. | mentions 73:2 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The opening certainly came in Mecca, while 73:20 to the end came in Medina years later and abrogated the Prophet's duty; ʿAʾisha's claim that vigil preceded the five prayers is anomalous. | mentions 73:1, 73:20 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The five prayers did not abrogate the Prophet's night vigil; rather, “recite what is easy” removed the requirement to spend half the night or slightly less. | mentions 73:20 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The first obligation on the community was the five prayers imposed on the Night Journey; if an earlier communal prayer existed, the Miʿraj account would mention its replacement, and the five were equally obligatory on the Prophet. [15 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(المفسرون); RAZI-FULL(آخرون); QURTUBI-FULL(الشافعي); BAYDAWI-FULL(قيل); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(مقاتل وابن كيسان); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; ALUSI-FULL+; IBNASHUR-FULL+]
- 73:20/q20/p3 Night prayer was voluntary from the outset. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (قوم): One account says the required amount was first abrogated, then the remaining duty was ended by the prescribed prayer times; extra prayers beyond the five are voluntary. | mentions 17:78, 30:17 IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عائشة): Aisha reports that night vigil was initially obligatory; the Prophet and companions kept it for a year until their feet swelled, then the surah’s ending eased the duty to voluntary prayer. Ibn Kathir cites Ahmad’s full report and Muslim’s similar version. | mentions 73:20 IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The verse either lifts night prayer’s obligation if it had previously been obligatory, or clarifies that it was never obligatory and that Muslims’ voluntary imitation of the Prophet was not binding. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Şevkânî): Some hold that night prayer was initially obligatory for both the Prophet and the community, then became voluntary for the community while remaining obligatory for the Prophet. [4 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(قوم); IBNKATHIR-FULL(عائشة); IBNASHUR-FULL; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Şevkânî)]
- 73:20/q20/p4 The communal duty ended, while the Prophet's obligation remained. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says night vigil ceased to be obligatory for the Prophet's companions and became voluntary, but remained obligatory for the Prophet. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (القشيري): Al-Qushayrī says the well-known view is that night prayer was abrogated for the community but remained obligatory for the Prophet. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The first command obligated night prayer for the Prophet alone; companions in Mecca followed his example. ʿAʾisha thought the five prayers abrogated a duty on Muslims, but the author calls that an interpretation. | mentions 73:2, 73:20 [3 notes: RAZI-FULL(ابن عباس); QURTUBI-FULL(القشيري); IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q20/p5 Another gloss says God creates night and day in measured proportions, as in 25:2; Ibn al-ʿArabī says created measure has no legal consequence by itself, though God attaches obligations to it. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Another gloss says God creates night and day in measured proportions, as in 25:2; Ibn al-ʿArabī says created measure has no legal consequence by itself, though God attaches obligations to it. | mentions 25:2 [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 73:20/q20/p6 Only the fixed vigil measure was lifted. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (القشيري): Another view reported by al-Qushayrī says the fixed amount was abrogated while the underlying obligation remained. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(القشيري)]
- 73:20/q20/p7 By analogy with the obligatory minimum of sacrificial offering in 2:196, some held that a small amount of night prayer remained obligatory; this is Hasan's school. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (قوم (مذهب الحسن)): By analogy with the obligatory minimum of sacrificial offering in 2:196, some held that a small amount of night prayer remained obligatory; this is Hasan's school. | mentions 2:196 [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(قوم (مذهب الحسن))]
- 73:20/q20/p8 On the view that night prayer is no longer obligatory, the command means to recite and pray if one wishes and finds it easy. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): On the view that night prayer is no longer obligatory, the command means to recite and pray if one wishes and finds it easy. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 73:20/q20/p9 Another account makes 17:79 the abrogating command, addressed to the Prophet and the community, as the original night-prayer command was general too. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (قوم): Another account makes 17:79 the abrogating command, addressed to the Prophet and the community, as the original night-prayer command was general too. | mentions 17:79 [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(قوم)]
- 73:20/q20/p10 Ibn al-Arabi reads unqualified references to prayer in these reports as the obligatory prayer, which undercuts the claim that they establish obligatory night prayer. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن العربي): Ibn al-Arabi reads unqualified references to prayer in these reports as the obligatory prayer, which undercuts the claim that they establish obligatory night prayer. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن العربي)]
- 73:20/q20/p11 Verse 20 explains the earlier command to stand at night and abrogates its obligatory status, as discussed above; taqūmu means “you pray” and adnā means “less.” — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Verse 20 explains the earlier command to stand at night and abrogates its obligatory status, as discussed above; taqūmu means “you pray” and adnā means “less.” [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 73:20/q20/p12 A further possibility from al-Qushayrī is a prescribed half, ranging from one-third to two-thirds; some maintained it and others left it until abrogation. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (القشيري): A further possibility from al-Qushayrī is a prescribed half, ranging from one-third to two-thirds; some maintained it and others left it until abrogation. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(القشيري)]
- 73:20/q20/p13 God's turning toward them means He eased the burden and removed the obligation of night vigil. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): God's turning toward them means He eased the burden and removed the obligation of night vigil. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 73:20/q20/p14 Al-Hasan and Ibn Sirin say night vigil remains obligatory, even for as long as it takes to milk a sheep. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الحسن وابن سيرين): Al-Hasan and Ibn Sirin say night vigil remains obligatory, even for as long as it takes to milk a sheep. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الحسن وابن سيرين)]
- 73:20/q20/p15 Ibn Zayd says attend to daytime needs and devote the night to religion; this applied when night prayer was obligatory, before 73:20 eased it, and Ibn Kathir endorses his account. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says attend to daytime needs and devote the night to religion; this applied when night prayer was obligatory, before 73:20 eased it, and Ibn Kathir endorses his account. | mentions 73:20, 17:79 [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن زيد)+]
- 73:20/q20/p16 Ibn Abbas says the command to the Prophet and believers to rise for most of the night burdened them, then 73:20 relieved and broadened the obligation. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the command to the Prophet and believers to rise for most of the night burdened them, then 73:20 relieved and broadened the obligation. | mentions 73:20 [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 73:20/q20/p17 Al-Hasan condemns a memorizer who prays only the obligatory prayers as one who neglects the Qur'an, and says a little night recitation, even five verses, is required of its bearers. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (الحسن): Al-Hasan condemns a memorizer who prays only the obligatory prayers as one who neglects the Qur'an, and says a little night recitation, even five verses, is required of its bearers. | mentions 12:68 [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(الحسن)]
- 73:20/q20/p18 A hadith says that one who sleeps until morning has Satan urinate in his ear; commentators differ whether this means missing an obligatory prayer or night vigil. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): A hadith says that one who sleeps until morning has Satan urinate in his ear; commentators differ whether this means missing an obligatory prayer or night vigil. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q20/p19 Ibn Kathir notes as an unusually strong position Abu Bakr Abd al-Aziz’s view that Ramadan night vigil is obligatory, adding that God knows best. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (أبو بكر عبد العزيز): Ibn Kathir notes as an unusually strong position Abu Bakr Abd al-Aziz’s view that Ramadan night vigil is obligatory, adding that God knows best. - 73:20/q20/p20 Another view takes the command as bare recitation in place of the former night-prayer obligation, as an added easing. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (قيل): Another view takes the command as bare recitation in place of the former night-prayer obligation, as an added easing. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(قيل)]
- 73:20/q20/p21 Some use 'you will not calculate it' alongside the command to rise at night as proof of impossible obligations; the author answers that the wording means difficulty, as when someone says another person is unbearable to… — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Some use 'you will not calculate it' alongside the command to rise at night as proof of impossible obligations; the author answers that the wording means difficulty, as when someone says another person is unbearable to look at. - 73:20/q20/p22 Qatada says the obligatory vigil lasted a year or two. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (قتادة): Qatada says the obligatory vigil lasted a year or two. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(قتادة)]
- 73:20/q20/p23 The phrase that God relented supports the earlier view that night prayer was initially obligatory for the Prophet and the community; many reports support it. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The phrase that God relented supports the earlier view that night prayer was initially obligatory for the Prophet and the community; many reports support it. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q20/p24 Ibn Jubayr and a group hold that some night prayer is unavoidably obligatory, even if only fifty verses are recited. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن جبير وجماعة): Ibn Jubayr and a group hold that some night prayer is unavoidably obligatory, even if only fifty verses are recited. - 73:20/q20/p25 Al-Ālūsī reads these views as retaining obligation today while abrogating only a specified amount, but says the familiar position differs. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī reads these views as retaining obligation today while abrogating only a specified amount, but says the familiar position differs. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q20/p26 After interpreting recitation as prayer and recounting abrogation, al-Baḥr reports that most scholars take this command as permission. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الجمهور): After interpreting recitation as prayer and recounting abrogation, al-Baḥr reports that most scholars take this command as permission. - 73:20/q20/p27 Al-Ḥasan and Ibn Sīrīn hold that night prayer remains obligatory today, even if only for as long as it takes to milk a sheep. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الحسن وابن سيرين): Al-Ḥasan and Ibn Sīrīn hold that night prayer remains obligatory today, even if only for as long as it takes to milk a sheep. - 73:20/q20/p28 If the verse is read as the later view, it only commands some Qurʾān recitation in place of the former night-prayer duty, so it does not establish obligatory recitation in ritual prayer. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): If the verse is read as the later view, it only commands some Qurʾān recitation in place of the former night-prayer duty, so it does not establish obligatory recitation in ritual prayer. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q20/p29 The imperative's apparent force remains obligation: night vigil and recitation are still obligatory, but need not be performed at an uncountable length and may be as much as is easy. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The imperative's apparent force remains obligation: night vigil and recitation are still obligatory, but need not be performed at an uncountable length and may be as much as is easy. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q20/p30 The verse shows the Prophet and some companions already prayed at night under the opening command; its “He relented toward you” marks relief for all and abrogates that earlier command. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The verse shows the Prophet and some companions already prayed at night under the opening command; its “He relented toward you” marks relief for all and abrogates that earlier command. | mentions 73:2, 73:20 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q20/p31 The only reported group prayer with the Prophet occurred in Medina during Ramadan after he reserved mosque space; companions joined, then he stayed away one night, saying he feared it might be made obligatory. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The only reported group prayer with the Prophet occurred in Medina during Ramadan after he reserved mosque space; companions joined, then he stayed away one night, saying he feared it might be made obligatory. | mentions 73:20 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q20/p32 This shows the companions' joint night prayer was not obligatory and did not continue; if any vigil were mandatory for Muslims, praying with the Prophet would fulfill it and there would be no fear of a… — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): This shows the companions' joint night prayer was not obligatory and did not continue; if any vigil were mandatory for Muslims, praying with the Prophet would fulfill it and there would be no fear of a new duty. | mentions 73:20 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q20/p33 Aisha reports in Sahih al-Bukhari that people joined the Prophet’s mosque prayer on successive Ramadan nights, growing in number; he stayed away later for fear the prayer would be made obligatory. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (عائشة): Aisha reports in Sahih al-Bukhari that people joined the Prophet’s mosque prayer on successive Ramadan nights, growing in number; he stayed away later for fear the prayer would be made obligatory. - 73:20/q20/p34 The majority of jurists require a surah in addition to al-Fātiḥa and consider reciting only part of a surah in obligatory prayer disliked; partial recitation is allowed in night prayer, Ramadan vigil, and necessity. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (جمهور الفقهاء): The majority of jurists require a surah in addition to al-Fātiḥa and consider reciting only part of a surah in obligatory prayer disliked; partial recitation is allowed in night prayer, Ramadan vigil, and necessity. - 73:20/q20/p35 This verse abrogates the set duration of night vigil—half the night or more or less than a third—leaving its amount unmeasured; the vigil’s remaining legal status was explained earlier. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): This verse abrogates the set duration of night vigil—half the night or more or less than a third—leaving its amount unmeasured; the vigil’s remaining legal status was explained earlier. - 73:20/q20/p36 One reported legal view makes night vigil obligatory for both the Prophet and believers. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (القوم): One reported legal view makes night vigil obligatory for both the Prophet and believers. [1 notes: TABATABAI(القوم)]
- 73:20/q20/p37 God knew that the prescribed night amount could not be calculated with ease by the generality of those obligated. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): God knew that the prescribed night amount could not be calculated with ease by the generality of those obligated. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q20/p38 Similar reports through Sunni routes from Ibn Abbas and others also describe abrogation of the opening night-prayer command. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (ابن عباس وغيره): Similar reports through Sunni routes from Ibn Abbas and others also describe abrogation of the opening night-prayer command. [1 notes: TABATABAI(ابن عباس وغيره)]
- 73:20/q20/p39 The verse eases the earlier night-prayer command by allowing what is manageable from the Quran, without abrogating the original instruction to stand the specified portions. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The verse eases the earlier night-prayer command by allowing what is manageable from the Quran, without abrogating the original instruction to stand the specified portions. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q20/p40 The initial requirement to rise during a set part of the night became difficult in practice, so the obligation was eased; alternatively, the command was encouragement rather than a strict requirement to observe a set time… — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The initial requirement to rise during a set part of the night became difficult in practice, so the obligation was eased; alternatively, the command was encouragement rather than a strict requirement to observe a set time and pray. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 73:20/q20/p41 The statement that they would not keep count means the Companions could not all fulfill the full night-vigil obligation. — STUDYQURAN (Ṭ): The statement that they would not keep count means the Companions could not all fulfill the full night-vigil obligation. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN(Ṭ)]
- 73:20/q20/p42 Masruq says the easy recitation instruction abrogated the earlier night standing command. — MUJAHID: Masruq says the easy recitation instruction abrogated the earlier night standing command. [1 notes: MUJAHID(مسروق بن الأجدع)]
- 73:20/q20/p43 Vigil was abrogated for believers but remained obligatory for the Prophet; opening and ending were a year apart. — MUQATIL: Vigil was abrogated for believers but remained obligatory for the Prophet; opening and ending were a year apart. [1 notes: MUQATIL]
- 73:20/q20/p44 Exegetes say the early vigil requirement was later abrogated by five daily prayers. — IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL: Exegetes say the early vigil requirement was later abrogated by five daily prayers. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL(المفسرون)]

- 73:20/q20/p49 The night obligation was abrogated and manageable recitation voluntary; obligation would not be phrased what is easy. — MAWARDI-FULL: The night obligation was abrogated and manageable recitation voluntary; obligation would not be phrased what is easy. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 73:20/q20/p50 Ibn Abbas says companions' vigil became voluntary while Prophet's remained obligatory. — WAHIDI-BASIT: Ibn Abbas says companions' vigil became voluntary while Prophet's remained obligatory. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن عباس)]

- 73:20/q20/p54 Jishumi prefers initial obligation and later abrogation against Abu Ali and Abu Muslim. — JISHUMI: Jishumi prefers initial obligation and later abrogation against Abu Ali and Abu Muslim. [1 notes: JISHUMI]

## 73:20/q21 What reports describe the night vigil and its easing?  [* · reports]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q21/p1 Al-Ḥasan says they prayed until their feet swelled, and God told them they could not truly keep count. — TABRISI, d. 548 (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan says they prayed until their feet swelled, and God told them they could not truly keep count. [1 notes: TABRISI(الحسن)]
- 73:20/q21/p2 Muqatil says people prayed all night because they feared failing to reach the amount of vigil imposed on them. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (مقاتل): Muqatil says people prayed all night because they feared failing to reach the amount of vigil imposed on them. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(مقاتل)]
- 73:20/q21/p3 Muqātil and others report that the initial command burdened the worshippers: unsure of the fractions, they stood until morning, fearing an error, until their feet swelled and faces paled; God then showed mercy and eased it. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (مقاتل): Muqātil and others report that the initial command burdened the worshippers: unsure of the fractions, they stood until morning, fearing an error, until their feet swelled and faces paled; God then showed mercy and eased it. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(مقاتل)]
- 73:20/q21/p4 After they stood until their feet swelled, God knew they could not sustain these measures without severe hardship. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): After they stood until their feet swelled, God knew they could not sustain these measures without severe hardship. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 73:20/q21/p5 Ibn Abbas reports that people initially prayed roughly as much at night as in Ramadan, with close to a year between the surah’s beginning and end; several routes likewise report a year. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas reports that people initially prayed roughly as much at night as in Ramadan, with close to a year between the surah’s beginning and end; several routes likewise report a year. | mentions 73:20 [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 73:20/q21/p6 Abu Abd al-Rahman says the believers kept night vigil for a year until their feet and shins swelled, then 73:20 brought relief; al-Hasan al-Basri agrees. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (أبو عبد الرحمن): Abu Abd al-Rahman says the believers kept night vigil for a year until their feet and shins swelled, then 73:20 brought relief; al-Hasan al-Basri agrees. | mentions 73:20 [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(أبو عبد الرحمن)]
- 73:20/q21/p7 Aisha reports that the Prophet and companions prayed until their feet swelled and that the surah’s ending was held in heaven for sixteen months before it descended. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عائشة): Aisha reports that the Prophet and companions prayed until their feet swelled and that the surah’s ending was held in heaven for sixteen months before it descended. | mentions 73:20 [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(عائشة)]
- 73:20/q21/p8 Qatada says they kept night vigil for a year or two until their legs and feet swelled, after which God sent relief at the end of the surah. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (قتادة): Qatada says they kept night vigil for a year or two until their legs and feet swelled, after which God sent relief at the end of the surah. | mentions 73:20 [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(قتادة)]
- 73:20/q21/p9 Ali reports that after the command to keep night vigil the Prophet prayed all night until his feet swelled; Gabriel told him to put his feet on the ground, and this verse and the easing command… — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (علي): Ali reports that after the command to keep night vigil the Prophet prayed all night until his feet swelled; Gabriel told him to put his feet on the ground, and this verse and the easing command in 73:20 followed. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(علي)]
- 73:20/q21/p10 Night prayer was first prescribed at the opening of the surah; the Prophet and Companions struggled for a year, often praying all night from fear of miscalculating the required portion, until their feet swelled and faces… — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Night prayer was first prescribed at the opening of the surah; the Prophet and Companions struggled for a year, often praying all night from fear of miscalculating the required portion, until their feet swelled and faces paled. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q21/p11 Ali ibn Bakkar said that for forty years nothing grieved him except the arrival of dawn, which ended his night vigil. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (علي بن بكار الشامي): Ali ibn Bakkar said that for forty years nothing grieved him except the arrival of dawn, which ended his night vigil. [1 notes: BURSEVI(علي بن بكار الشامي)]
- 73:20/q21/p12 Ibn Abbas reports that the original vigil was very burdensome and believers kept it like their Ramadan vigil; God then eased it through the clause commanding them to recite what was manageable, graciously widening rather than… — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas reports that the original vigil was very burdensome and believers kept it like their Ramadan vigil; God then eased it through the clause commanding them to recite what was manageable, graciously widening rather than narrowing the duty, about a year later. | mentions 73:2 [1 notes: ELMALILI(ابن عباس)]
- 73:20/q21/p13 Qatada says the believers kept night vigil for one or two years after the opening command, until their feet and legs swelled; the command to recite what was easy then brought them relief. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (قتادة): Qatada says the believers kept night vigil for one or two years after the opening command, until their feet and legs swelled; the command to recite what was easy then brought them relief. | mentions 73:2 [1 notes: ELMALILI(قتادة)]
- 73:20/q21/p14 A hadith says the Prophet prayed at night until his feet swelled. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A hadith says the Prophet prayed at night until his feet swelled. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q21/p15 The group with the Prophet were Companions who copied his practice and reportedly stood until their feet swelled. — STUDYQURAN (author): The group with the Prophet were Companions who copied his practice and reportedly stood until their feet swelled. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN]
- 73:20/q21/p16 Early Muslims prayed half or a third of the night for a year before the five prayers; the burden became hard and God relieved them. — MUQATIL: Early Muslims prayed half or a third of the night for a year before the five prayers; the burden became hard and God relieved them. [1 notes: MUQATIL]
- 73:20/q21/p17 The Prophet and Muslims stood at night before the prayer obligation; difficulty led to this concession. — FARRA: The Prophet and Muslims stood at night before the prayer obligation; difficulty led to this concession. [1 notes: FARRA]
- 73:20/q21/p18 The Prophet and a group held themselves to measured night portions until difficult. — IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL: The Prophet and a group held themselves to measured night portions until difficult. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL]
- 73:20/q21/p19 Ibn Abbas says most-night prayer burdened believers until 73:20 allowed manageable recitation. — TAB-FULL: Ibn Abbas says most-night prayer burdened believers until 73:20 allowed manageable recitation. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 73:20/q21/p20 Sa'id says the Prophet and companions kept vigil ten years until the surah ending relieved it. — TAB-FULL: Sa'id says the Prophet and companions kept vigil ten years until the surah ending relieved it. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(سعيد)]
- 73:20/q21/p21 Qatada says the vigil lasted one or two years until shins and feet swelled. — TAB-FULL: Qatada says the vigil lasted one or two years until shins and feet swelled. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(قتادة)]
- 73:20/q21/p22 Abu Abd al-Rahman says a year of prayer caused swollen feet and shins before relief. — TAB-FULL: Abu Abd al-Rahman says a year of prayer caused swollen feet and shins before relief. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(أبو عبد الرحمن)]
- 73:20/q21/p23 Qatada says Prophet and companions stood a year until swollen feet, then relief made vigil voluntary. — TAB-FULL: Qatada says Prophet and companions stood a year until swollen feet, then relief made vigil voluntary. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(قتادة)]
- 73:20/q21/p24 Aisha says night congregational prayer was feared as a duty; imposed eight months, then verse 20 made it voluntary. — THALABI: Aisha says night congregational prayer was feared as a duty; imposed eight months, then verse 20 made it voluntary. [1 notes: THALABI(عائشة)]
- 73:20/q21/p25 Walibi says the initial night requirement was difficult and easing came with allowance for illness. — WAHIDI-BASIT: Walibi says the initial night requirement was difficult and easing came with allowance for illness. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الوالبي)]
- 73:20/q21/p26 Sulami says a year of standing swelled feet and legs before relief. — WAHIDI-BASIT: Sulami says a year of standing swelled feet and legs before relief. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو عبد الرحمن السلمي)]
- 73:20/q21/p27 Aisha says Prophet and companions kept obligatory vigil one year until swollen feet; then ending made it voluntary. — BAGHAWI-FULL: Aisha says Prophet and companions kept obligatory vigil one year until swollen feet; then ending made it voluntary. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL(عائشة)]
- 73:20/q21/p28 Verse eased continued vigil whether obligatory or recommended. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Verse eased continued vigil whether obligatory or recommended. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 73:20/q21/p29 Aisha says obligatory vigil lasted one year, then ending made it voluntary. — TABRISI: Aisha says obligatory vigil lasted one year, then ending made it voluntary. [1 notes: TABRISI(عائشة)]
- 73:20/q21/p30 Sa'id says ten years separated opening from relief. — TABRISI: Sa'id says ten years separated opening from relief. [1 notes: TABRISI(سعيد بن جبير)]
- 73:20/q21/p31 Ibn Abbas says one year elapsed and believers prayed about as in Ramadan. — TABRISI: Ibn Abbas says one year elapsed and believers prayed about as in Ramadan. [1 notes: TABRISI(ابن عباس)]

## 73:20/q22 How does the passage connect to the sura and other verses?  [* · links]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q22/p1 As explained under 73:2, this phrasing does not entail that the Prophet left an obligatory amount undone. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): As explained under 73:2, this phrasing does not entail that the Prophet left an obligatory amount undone. | mentions 73:2 [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 73:20/q22/p2 The unprayed interval can be a third and some of two-thirds, satisfying the earlier description of leaving only a little. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The unprayed interval can be a third and some of two-thirds, satisfying the earlier description of leaving only a little. | mentions 73:3 [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 73:20/q22/p3 The one-third option aligns with the earlier instruction to reduce the night until the time is one third. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The one-third option aligns with the earlier instruction to reduce the night until the time is one third. | mentions 73:3 [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 73:20/q22/p4 The majority answer that position with the Prophet’s statement that prayer is invalid without the Opening of the Book, reinforced by reports requiring Umm al-Kitab in every prayer. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (الجمهور): The majority answer that position with the Prophet’s statement that prayer is invalid without the Opening of the Book, reinforced by reports requiring Umm al-Kitab in every prayer. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(الجمهور)]
- 73:20/q22/p5 favors the Ashʿarite reading: reward is a pure divine gift, and boasting makes the gift a donation to the recipient rather than a sincere deed offered to God for promised reward. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The author favors the Ashʿarite reading: reward is a pure divine gift, and boasting makes the gift a donation to the recipient rather than a sincere deed offered to God for promised reward. | mentions 73:20, 9:111 [1 notes: BURSEVI+]
- 73:20/q22/p6 ʿAʾisha's report says the ending followed the opening by a year; al-Tabari also reports this timing from Ibn ʿAbbas, while the majority place the ending in Medina years later. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (عائشة): ʿAʾisha's report says the ending followed the opening by a year; al-Tabari also reports this timing from Ibn ʿAbbas, while the majority place the ending in Medina years later. | mentions 73:20 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(عائشة)]
