- 73:20/q22/p7 The readings describe different nightly patterns as nights and days vary in length; all fit the choice granted in 73:2–4, so the intended meaning does not change. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The readings describe different nightly patterns as nights and days vary in length; all fit the choice granted in 73:2–4, so the intended meaning does not change. | mentions 73:2, 73:4 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q22/p8 This closing statement summarizes prior commands and gives an omitted command: do good; whatever good you send ahead for yourselves, you will find with God. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): This closing statement summarizes prior commands and gives an omitted command: do good; whatever good you send ahead for yourselves, you will find with God. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]

## 73:20/q23 What inward readings are drawn from the passage?  [* · inward]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q23/p1 The soul belongs to the subtle, instantly created world of command, while the dense body belongs to the gradual world of creation; the soul is therefore alien to bodily attachments. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The soul belongs to the subtle, instantly created world of command, while the dense body belongs to the gradual world of creation; the soul is therefore alien to bodily attachments. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q23/p2 Remembrance, prayer, and good works help the soul reconnect with its elevated spiritual realm; wisdom means justice and moderation in words and deeds. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Remembrance, prayer, and good works help the soul reconnect with its elevated spiritual realm; wisdom means justice and moderation in words and deeds. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q23/p3 The verse signifies the heart's frequent escape from the night of its nature through turning to God and away from the self; brief veiling remains by wisdom and is itself mercy. The 'group' with the Messenger… — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (التأويلات النجمية): The verse signifies the heart's frequent escape from the night of its nature through turning to God and away from the self; brief veiling remains by wisdom and is itself mercy. The 'group' with the Messenger of the heart means spiritual powers and bodily members. [1 notes: BURSEVI(التأويلات النجمية)]
- 73:20/q23/p4 The spiritual journey from nature's night to reality's day proceeds by God's determination, not the seeker's; arrival depends on divine grace, as many travelers turn back without reaching the goal. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (التأويلات النجمية): The spiritual journey from nature's night to reality's day proceeds by God's determination, not the seeker's; arrival depends on divine grace, as many travelers turn back without reaching the goal. [1 notes: BURSEVI(التأويلات النجمية)]
- 73:20/q23/p5 The verse also signals gathering in pure hearts the realities, subtleties, spiritual knowledges, and insights revealed according to one's readiness, and concealing them from unfit listeners who might accuse the knower of heresy or unionism. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (التأويلات النجمية): The verse also signals gathering in pure hearts the realities, subtleties, spiritual knowledges, and insights revealed according to one's readiness, and concealing them from unfit listeners who might accuse the knower of heresy or unionism. [1 notes: BURSEVI(التأويلات النجمية)]
- 73:20/q23/p6 The sick signify hearts veiled by ego and occupied with worldly love and desire, on which none of the Quran's hidden realities become manifest. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The sick signify hearts veiled by ego and occupied with worldly love and desire, on which none of the Quran's hidden realities become manifest. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q23/p7 Sanāʾī's poem says a spiritually blind person receives only Quranic letters, just as a blind eye gets only warmth from the sun; Quran's unveiled bride is for one whose faith is cleared of turmoil. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (سنائي): Sanāʾī's poem says a spiritually blind person receives only Quranic letters, just as a blind eye gets only warmth from the sun; Quran's unveiled bride is for one whose faith is cleared of turmoil. [1 notes: BURSEVI(سنائي)]
- 73:20/q23/p8 The claim about hidden motives concerns people still on the path of spiritual aspiration; those annihilated from will, the most complete, have no motive, a state known only to their peers or through God's disclosure. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The claim about hidden motives concerns people still on the path of spiritual aspiration; those annihilated from will, the most complete, have no motive, a state known only to their peers or through God's disclosure. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q23/p9 Al-Ghazali transmits al-Baqillani's view that claiming complete freedom from self-interest is unbelief, since freedom from motive is a divine prerogative; apparent God-directed action may conceal a motive. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (الغزالي): Al-Ghazali transmits al-Baqillani's view that claiming complete freedom from self-interest is unbelief, since freedom from motive is a divine prerogative; apparent God-directed action may conceal a motive. [1 notes: BURSEVI(الغزالي)]
- 73:20/q23/p10 The Sufis count a worshipper’s awareness of or regard for their own worship as a shortcoming. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الصوفية): The Sufis count a worshipper’s awareness of or regard for their own worship as a shortcoming. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(الصوفية)]
- 73:20/q23/p11 A report from al-Rida, his father, and his grandfather says what is easy to recite is what brings humility of heart and purity of the inner self. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (الرضا عن أبيه عن جده): A report from al-Rida, his father, and his grandfather says what is easy to recite is what brings humility of heart and purity of the inner self. [1 notes: TABATABAI(الرضا عن أبيه عن جده)]
- 73:20/q23/p12 The closing passage returns to the opening verses' theme of the spiritual value of night prayer. — ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): The closing passage returns to the opening verses' theme of the spiritual value of night prayer. [1 notes: ASAD-NOTES]
- 73:20/q23/p13 Al-Wasiti says people cannot sustain worship or keep deeds defect-free; God accepts by grace. — SULAMI: Al-Wasiti says people cannot sustain worship or keep deeds defect-free; God accepts by grace. [1 notes: SULAMI(الواسطي)]
- 73:20/q23/p14 Al-Wasiti warns that fixation on God's gifts can veil the Giver's Essence. — SULAMI: Al-Wasiti warns that fixation on God's gifts can veil the Giver's Essence. [1 notes: SULAMI(الواسطي)]
- 73:20/q23/p15 Ja'far says recitation should bring heart humility and inner clarity. — SULAMI: Ja'far says recitation should bring heart humility and inner clarity. [1 notes: SULAMI(جعفر بن محمد)]
- 73:20/q23/p16 Charity can be driven by show, desire, or affliction; pure intention is rare. — SULAMI: Charity can be driven by show, desire, or affliction; pure intention is rare. [1 notes: SULAMI]
- 73:20/q23/p17 Ali al-Rida says easy recitation yields heart humility and inner purity. — THALABI: Ali al-Rida says easy recitation yields heart humility and inner purity. [1 notes: THALABI(علي بن موسى الرضا)]
- 73:20/q23/p18 Al-Rida says manageable recitation brings humility and purity of inner self. — TABRISI: Al-Rida says manageable recitation brings humility and purity of inner self. [1 notes: TABRISI(الرضا)]

## 73:20/q24 What broader rules about Quran recitation are cited with this verse?  [* · law]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q24/p1 The Prophet licensed the community to recite in seven ahruf; when Uthman feared division from differences in recitation, he unified them on the official codex, and the community accepted his sound guidance, leaving the other six. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): The Prophet licensed the community to recite in seven ahruf; when Uthman feared division from differences in recitation, he unified them on the official codex, and the community accepted his sound guidance, leaving the other six. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q24/p2 The six recitations left by the just Imam eventually became unknown and their traces disappeared, so no one today can recite them. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): The six recitations left by the just Imam eventually became unknown and their traces disappeared, so no one today can recite them. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q24/p3 Answering how Muslims could leave readings taught by the Prophet, Ibn Kathir says the command was permission and ease, not obligation; if obligatory, every letter would have been transmitted through decisive chains, and their failure to… — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Answering how Muslims could leave readings taught by the Prophet, Ibn Kathir says the command was permission and ease, not obligation; if obligatory, every letter would have been transmitted through decisive chains, and their failure to do so shows choice. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q24/p4 Ordinary differences in inflection, vowelization, or replacing a letter while the written form agrees are not the seven-letter disputes whose contentiousness entails disbelief; no scholar deems such ordinary recitation debate disbelief. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Ordinary differences in inflection, vowelization, or replacing a letter while the written form agrees are not the seven-letter disputes whose contentiousness entails disbelief; no scholar deems such ordinary recitation debate disbelief. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q24/p5 Ibn Shibrima reasoned that because no sura has fewer than three verses, one should not recite fewer than three verses. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عبد الله بن شبرمة): Ibn Shibrima reasoned that because no sura has fewer than three verses, one should not recite fewer than three verses. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(عبد الله بن شبرمة)]
- 73:20/q24/p6 The Prophet said that reciting the last two verses of al-Baqara at night suffices the reciter; Ibn Kathir says this report is agreed upon by al-Bukhari and Muslim. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet said that reciting the last two verses of al-Baqara at night suffices the reciter; Ibn Kathir says this report is agreed upon by al-Bukhari and Muslim. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q24/p7 In the clearer report for the chapter heading, the Prophet counsels Abdullah ibn Amr, who fasted daily and completed the Quran nightly, to read it over a month, then weekly, and settle on the fast of… — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): In the clearer report for the chapter heading, the Prophet counsels Abdullah ibn Amr, who fasted daily and completed the Quran nightly, to read it over a month, then weekly, and settle on the fast of David. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q24/p8 After growing old and weak, Abdullah regretted not accepting the Prophet’s concession; he maintained his Quran and fasting routine by reciting a seventh by day and making up fasts. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عبد الله بن عمرو): After growing old and weak, Abdullah regretted not accepting the Prophet’s concession; he maintained his Quran and fasting routine by reciting a seventh by day and making up fasts. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(عبد الله بن عمرو)]
- 73:20/q24/p9 The report adds that some recited the Quran every three or five days, while most followed a seven-day completion. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (بعضهم): The report adds that some recited the Quran every three or five days, while most followed a seven-day completion. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(بعضهم)]
- 73:20/q24/p10 The Prophet told Abdullah to read the Quran in a month, then in seven days and not less; Ibn Kathir says this wording apparently bars a completion in fewer than seven days. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عبد الله بن عمرو): The Prophet told Abdullah to read the Quran in a month, then in seven days and not less; Ibn Kathir says this wording apparently bars a completion in fewer than seven days. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(عبد الله بن عمرو)]
- 73:20/q24/p11 Abdullah ibn Masud used to complete the Quran from one Friday to the next outside Ramadan. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عبد الله بن مسعود): Abdullah ibn Masud used to complete the Quran from one Friday to the next outside Ramadan. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(عبد الله بن مسعود)]
- 73:20/q24/p12 Ubay ibn Kaab used to complete the Quran every eight days. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (أبي بن كعب): Ubay ibn Kaab used to complete the Quran every eight days. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(أبي بن كعب)]
- 73:20/q24/p13 Tamim al-Dari used to complete the Quran every seven days. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (تميم الداري): Tamim al-Dari used to complete the Quran every seven days. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(تميم الداري)]
- 73:20/q24/p14 Ibrahim al-Nakha‘i used to complete the Quran every seven days. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (إبراهيم): Ibrahim al-Nakha‘i used to complete the Quran every seven days. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(إبراهيم)]
- 73:20/q24/p15 Ibrahim reports that al-Aswad completed the Quran every six days and Alqama every five days. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (إبراهيم): Ibrahim reports that al-Aswad completed the Quran every six days and Alqama every five days. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(إبراهيم)]
- 73:20/q24/p16 Saad ibn Mundhir asked whether he could read the Quran in three days; the Prophet answered yes, adding in Abu Ubayd’s version, if he was able, and Saad kept to it until death. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Saad ibn Mundhir asked whether he could read the Quran in three days; the Prophet answered yes, adding in Abu Ubayd’s version, if he was able, and Saad kept to it until death. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q24/p17 The Prophet said that one who completes the Quran in fewer than three days does not understand it; al-Tirmidhi grades the report hasan sahih. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet said that one who completes the Quran in fewer than three days does not understand it; al-Tirmidhi grades the report hasan sahih. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q24/p18 A report from Aisha says the Prophet never completed the Quran in fewer than three days; Ibn Kathir calls it unusual and weak because al-Tayyib ibn Sulayman was weakened by al-Daraqutni and was little known. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عائشة): A report from Aisha says the Prophet never completed the Quran in fewer than three days; Ibn Kathir calls it unusual and weak because al-Tayyib ibn Sulayman was weakened by al-Daraqutni and was little known. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(عائشة)]
- 73:20/q24/p19 A number of early and later authorities, including Abu Ubayd, Ishaq, and Ibn Rahawayh, disliked completing the Quran in less than three days; Ibn Kathir says the report from Muadh is sound. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (معاذ بن جبل): A number of early and later authorities, including Abu Ubayd, Ishaq, and Ibn Rahawayh, disliked completing the Quran in less than three days; Ibn Kathir says the report from Muadh is sound. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(معاذ بن جبل)]
- 73:20/q24/p20 Abdullah ibn Masud said that whoever reads the Quran in fewer than three days is a rajiz, a hurried or metrical reciter. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عبد الله): Abdullah ibn Masud said that whoever reads the Quran in fewer than three days is a rajiz, a hurried or metrical reciter. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(عبد الله)]
- 73:20/q24/p21 Abdullah ibn Masud used to complete the Quran in three days during Ramadan; Ibn Kathir grades this report’s chain sound. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عبد الله بن مسعود): Abdullah ibn Masud used to complete the Quran in three days during Ramadan; Ibn Kathir grades this report’s chain sound. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(عبد الله بن مسعود)]
- 73:20/q24/p22 A report raised to the Prophet instructs readers to avoid excess and neglect, and not to earn from or amass wealth through the Quran. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عبد الرحمن بن شبل): A report raised to the Prophet instructs readers to avoid excess and neglect, and not to earn from or amass wealth through the Quran. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(عبد الرحمن بن شبل)]
- 73:20/q24/p23 Ibn Kathir explains that excess means racing through recitation in the shortest time, which usually prevents reflection; neglect means abandoning recitation. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir explains that excess means racing through recitation in the shortest time, which usually prevents reflection; neglect means abandoning recitation. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q24/p24 Anas reports that a man who recited al-Baqara and Āl ʿImrān was held in high regard among them. — BURHAN, d. 794 (أنس): Anas reports that a man who recited al-Baqara and Āl ʿImrān was held in high regard among them. [1 notes: BURHAN(أنس)]
- 73:20/q24/p25 Mastering a sūra makes a memorizer feel he has acquired an independent portion of God's book, magnifying what he learned; hence sūra recitation in prayer is preferable. — BURHAN, d. 794 (الزمخشرى): Mastering a sūra makes a memorizer feel he has acquired an independent portion of God's book, magnifying what he learned; hence sūra recitation in prayer is preferable. [1 notes: BURHAN(الزمخشرى)]
- 73:20/q24/p26 Aḥmad forbids departing from the ʿUthmānic codex's orthography, whether in a yāʾ, wāw, alif, or any other letter. — BURHAN, d. 794 (أحمد): Aḥmad forbids departing from the ʿUthmānic codex's orthography, whether in a yāʾ, wāw, alif, or any other letter. [1 notes: BURHAN(أحمد)]
- 73:20/q24/p27 treats adherence to the codex's letters as an established sunna that no one may transgress. — BURHAN, d. 794 (author): The author treats adherence to the codex's letters as an established sunna that no one may transgress. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 73:20/q24/p28 Zayd says that recitation is a sunna. — BURHAN, d. 794 (زيد): Zayd says that recitation is a sunna. [1 notes: BURHAN(زيد)]
- 73:20/q24/p29 Readers do not follow Arabic grammar in recitation when it conflicts with the codex's written form. — BURHAN, d. 794 (القراء): Readers do not follow Arabic grammar in recitation when it conflicts with the codex's written form. [1 notes: BURHAN(القراء)]
- 73:20/q24/p30 Ibn al-Jazarī says his verified reading chains comprise about a thousand routes, the soundest and highest available, and include only transmitters whose integrity, meeting, and contemporaneity he or earlier authorities confirmed; he claims no prior work… — NASHR, d. 833 (author): Ibn al-Jazarī says his verified reading chains comprise about a thousand routes, the soundest and highest available, and include only transmitters whose integrity, meeting, and contemporaneity he or earlier authorities confirmed; he claims no prior work made this commitment. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 73:20/q24/p31 Al-Quṭayʿī died in 368 and is described as a reliable, noble, righteous transmitter with a high chain who uniquely transmitted this reading. — NASHR, d. 833 (author): Al-Quṭayʿī died in 368 and is described as a reliable, noble, righteous transmitter with a high chain who uniquely transmitted this reading. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 73:20/q24/p32 Ordinary Arabic spelling follows letter rules and pronunciation at starts and pauses, while the early Qur'anic codex sometimes departs from those rules. — ITQAN, d. 911 (author): Ordinary Arabic spelling follows letter rules and pronunciation at starts and pauses, while the early Qur'anic codex sometimes departs from those rules. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 73:20/q24/p33 Malik also rejects changing letters such as written alif and wāw in the Qur'anic codex. — ITQAN, d. 911 (مالك): Malik also rejects changing letters such as written alif and wāw in the Qur'anic codex. [1 notes: ITQAN(مالك)]
- 73:20/q24/p34 Aḥmad prohibits departing from the codex in any wāw, yāʾ, alif, or other letter. — ITQAN, d. 911 (أحمد): Aḥmad prohibits departing from the codex in any wāw, yāʾ, alif, or other letter. [1 notes: ITQAN(أحمد)]
- 73:20/q24/p35 organizes orthographic variants under deletion, addition, hamza, substitution, separation, and places where one of two readings was written. — ITQAN, d. 911 (author): The author organizes orthographic variants under deletion, addition, hamza, substitution, separation, and places where one of two readings was written. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 73:20/q24/p36 The Prophet says Quran was revealed in seven wholesome sufficient ahruf and believers may recite as they wish. — IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL: The Prophet says Quran was revealed in seven wholesome sufficient ahruf and believers may recite as they wish. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL(رسول الله، صلى الله عليه وآله وسلم)]
- 73:20/q24/p37 Rejected view: seven ahruf are promise, warning, lawful, unlawful, exhortation, parables, and argument. — IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL: Rejected view: seven ahruf are promise, warning, lawful, unlawful, exhortation, parables, and argument. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL(قوم)]
- 73:20/q24/p38 Rejected view: seven ahruf are seven dialects within one word. — IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL: Rejected view: seven ahruf are seven dialects within one word. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL(آخرون)]
- 73:20/q24/p39 Rejected view: seven ahruf are legal commands, prohibitions, histories, future reports, and parables. — IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL: Rejected view: seven ahruf are legal commands, prohibitions, histories, future reports, and parables. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL(قوم)]
- 73:20/q24/p40 Reader names such as Abu Amr or Asim do not mean those categories. — IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL: Reader names such as Abu Amr or Asim do not mean those categories. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL]
- 73:20/q24/p41 No Quranic word is validly recited in seven different ways, as far as the author knows. — IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL: No Quranic word is validly recited in seven different ways, as far as the author knows. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL]
- 73:20/q24/p42 The seven ahruf are seven linguistic forms distributed across the Quran, supported by recite what is easy. — IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL: The seven ahruf are seven linguistic forms distributed across the Quran, supported by recite what is easy. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL]
- 73:20/q24/p43 Umar reports the Prophet approved his and Hisham's different recitations of al-Furqan as revealed. — IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL: Umar reports the Prophet approved his and Hisham's different recitations of al-Furqan as revealed. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL(عمر)]
- 73:20/q24/p44 Reciting the readings of Ibn Masud, Ubayy, or Zayd counts as their harf. — IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL: Reciting the readings of Ibn Masud, Ubayy, or Zayd counts as their harf. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL]
- 73:20/q24/p45 Harf can mean letter, word, utterance, sermon, or poem. — IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL: Harf can mean letter, word, utterance, sermon, or poem. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL]
- 73:20/q24/p46 Gabriel asked one harf, Michael urged more, and allowance rose to six or seven without mismatching mercy and punishment. — TAB-FULL: Gabriel asked one harf, Michael urged more, and allowance rose to six or seven without mismatching mercy and punishment. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(النبي)]
- 73:20/q24/p47 Tabari says ahruf differ in wording but agree in meaning, as hallum and ta'al. — TAB-FULL: Tabari says ahruf differ in wording but agree in meaning, as hallum and ta'al. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 73:20/q24/p48 Ibn Masud says readings he heard were close; read as taught without affectation. — TAB-FULL: Ibn Masud says readings he heard were close; read as taught without affectation. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن مسعود)]
- 73:20/q24/p49 Ibn Masud says a reader should not switch from one harf, and would seek someone more knowledgeable. — TAB-FULL: Ibn Masud says a reader should not switch from one harf, and would seek someone more knowledgeable. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن مسعود)]
- 73:20/q24/p50 An unnamed companion reports Ibn Masud's instruction not to switch harf. — TAB-FULL: An unnamed companion reports Ibn Masud's instruction not to switch harf. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(رجل من أصحاب عبد الله)]
- 73:20/q24/p51 Tabari defines harf here as a person's recitation, akin to a poet's word. — TAB-FULL: Tabari defines harf here as a person's recitation, akin to a poet's word. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 73:20/q24/p52 Do not abandon a Companion's transmitted harf out of aversion; rejecting one rejects the Quran. — TAB-FULL: Do not abandon a Companion's transmitted harf out of aversion; rejecting one rejects the Quran. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 73:20/q24/p53 Disbelief in one transmitted harf is disbelief in all. — TAB-FULL: Disbelief in one transmitted harf is disbelief in all. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 73:20/q24/p54 Prophet says Quran guides, judges, informs, and never tires scholars or devout. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Prophet says Quran guides, judges, informs, and never tires scholars or devout. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q24/p55 Anas identifies Quran as firm handhold of 2:256 and 31:22. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Anas identifies Quran as firm handhold of 2:256 and 31:22. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(أنس بن مالك)]
- 73:20/q24/p56 Prophet says seek earlier and later knowledge in Quran. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Prophet says seek earlier and later knowledge in Quran. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q24/p57 Prophet says each Quranic letter earns ten deeds; alif lam mim count separately. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Prophet says each Quranic letter earns ten deeds; alif lam mim count separately. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q24/p58 Prophet leaves Quran and family as two weighty trusts in final sermon. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Prophet leaves Quran and family as two weighty trusts in final sermon. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q24/p59 Ja'far says Quran stays fresh across generations through reflection. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Ja'far says Quran stays fresh across generations through reflection. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(جعفر بن محمد الصادق)]
- 73:20/q24/p60 Muhammad ibn Sa'id says stories recur so readers of any accessible portion receive admonition. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Muhammad ibn Sa'id says stories recur so readers of any accessible portion receive admonition. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(محمد بن سعيد)]

## 73:20/q25 What broader Quranic or religious discussions are attached to this verse?  [* · interpretation]
turns on: The sources differ in the readings, referents, chronology, legal scope, or explanation shown by the positions.
- 73:20/q25/p1 ʿAṭāʾ says the meaning is that God misses none of what people do. — TABRISI, d. 548 (عطاء): ʿAṭāʾ says the meaning is that God misses none of what people do. [1 notes: TABRISI(عطاء)]
- 73:20/q25/p2 The verse means God knows how much of the night people will stand in prayer. — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): The verse means God knows how much of the night people will stand in prayer. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 73:20/q25/p3 Qiyām can mean ordinary standing upright. — IBNJAWZI-NUZHA, d. 597 (author): Qiyām can mean ordinary standing upright. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-NUZHA]
- 73:20/q25/p4 Whatever good people send ahead, they will find its reward in the afterlife. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): Whatever good people send ahead, they will find its reward in the afterlife. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD]
- 73:20/q25/p5 Ibn Kathir and the Kufans read the fāʾ of nisfahu and thāʾ of thuluthahu with fatḥa; the remaining readers use kasra. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن كثير وأهل الكوفة): Ibn Kathir and the Kufans read the fāʾ of nisfahu and thāʾ of thuluthahu with fatḥa; the remaining readers use kasra. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن كثير وأهل الكوفة)]
- 73:20/q25/p6 Al-Farraʾ says they could not keep track of the night’s exact timings. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (الفراء): Al-Farraʾ says they could not keep track of the night’s exact timings. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الفراء)]
- 73:20/q25/p7 The rebuttal compares it to saying one cannot bear to look at someone when the intended sense is that the sight is burdensome. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The rebuttal compares it to saying one cannot bear to look at someone when the intended sense is that the sight is burdensome. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p8 Al-Hasan specifies the prayer meant by the phrase as the Maghrib and ʿIshāʾ prayers. — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الحسن): Al-Hasan specifies the prayer meant by the phrase as the Maghrib and ʿIshāʾ prayers. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(الحسن)]
- 73:20/q25/p9 The remaining obligation may have applied to the Prophet alone, with its amount left to his choice. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The remaining obligation may have applied to the Prophet alone, with its amount left to his choice. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p10 A Prophetic report says Satan ties three knots at a sleeper's head; remembrance, ablution, and prayer untie them, while the sleeper otherwise wakes sluggish and ill-tempered. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A Prophetic report says Satan ties three knots at a sleeper's head; remembrance, ablution, and prayer untie them, while the sleeper otherwise wakes sluggish and ill-tempered. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q25/p11 A report from Ibn Masud says the Prophet described a man who slept all night as having Satan urinate in his ears. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عبد الله بن مسعود): A report from Ibn Masud says the Prophet described a man who slept all night as having Satan urinate in his ears. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(عبد الله بن مسعود)]
- 73:20/q25/p12 The Prophet told Abdullah ibn Amr not to imitate a man who used to pray at night and then stopped. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet told Abdullah ibn Amr not to imitate a man who used to pray at night and then stopped. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q25/p13 Abdullah ibn Umar dreamed angels took him toward Hell; after Hafsa relayed it, the Prophet called him a good man who should pray at night. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abdullah ibn Umar dreamed angels took him toward Hell; after Hafsa relayed it, the Prophet called him a good man who should pray at night. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q25/p14 Some interpret the command as voluntary charity. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (قوم): Some interpret the command as voluntary charity. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(قوم)]
- 73:20/q25/p15 Some interpret it as all acts of goodness. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (قوم): Some interpret it as all acts of goodness. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(قوم)]
- 73:20/q25/p16 Ibn Abbas interprets it as obedience to God and sincerity toward Him. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas interprets it as obedience to God and sincerity toward Him. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 73:20/q25/p17 notes that this wording also occurs in al-Baqara (2:110). — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The author notes that this wording also occurs in al-Baqara (2:110). | mentions 2:110 [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p18 Umar gave a poor man a dish of dates mixed with milk; when someone said the man would not know its value, Umar replied that the man's Lord knew. — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عمر بن الخطاب): Umar gave a poor man a dish of dates mixed with milk; when someone said the man would not know its value, Umar replied that the man's Lord knew. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(عمر بن الخطاب)]
- 73:20/q25/p19 The command is to perform the obligatory prayer. — BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The command is to perform the obligatory prayer. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p20 This means what is available and not difficult for you. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): This means what is available and not difficult for you. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p21 The lām in thuluthay is read with ḍamma, except by Hishām. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The lām in thuluthay is read with ḍamma, except by Hishām. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p22 God conceals the sins and shortcomings of those who sin. — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): God conceals the sins and shortcomings of those who sin. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p23 The verse next commands the two pillars of Islam, one bodily and the other financial. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The verse next commands the two pillars of Islam, one bodily and the other financial. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p24 Abu Hanifa is reported by al-Mawardi to set the minimum at one verse; Ibn al-Arabi reports a three-verse minimum. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa is reported by al-Mawardi to set the minimum at one verse; Ibn al-Arabi reports a three-verse minimum. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو حنيفة)]
- 73:20/q25/p25 Both أن لن and أن سيكون are weakened forms of أن الثقيلة, separated from the following clause by negation and the future particle respectively. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Both أن لن and أن سيكون are weakened forms of أن الثقيلة, separated from the following clause by negation and the future particle respectively. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 73:20/q25/p26 The second وآخرون coordinates with the first وآخرون, while يقاتلون is its adjective. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The second وآخرون coordinates with the first وآخرون, while يقاتلون is its adjective. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 73:20/q25/p27 The common reading has damma on ل, its original vowel as in ربع and سدس; Hisham reads it quiescent for ease. — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The common reading has damma on ل, its original vowel as in ربع and سدس; Hisham reads it quiescent for ease. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 73:20/q25/p28 A report from Abu Said says prayer is not sufficient without al-Hamd and a sura in every rakah, whether obligatory or voluntary; Ibn Kathir questions its soundness. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report from Abu Said says prayer is not sufficient without al-Hamd and a sura in every rakah, whether obligatory or voluntary; Ibn Kathir questions its soundness. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q25/p29 Ibn Kathir says al-Bukhari’s joined transmission is sound: Abd al-Rahman first heard it from Alqama, then directly from Abu Masud while circumambulating the Kaaba; Ali is Ibn al-Madini and his teacher Sufyan ibn Uyayna. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir says al-Bukhari’s joined transmission is sound: Abd al-Rahman first heard it from Alqama, then directly from Abu Masud while circumambulating the Kaaba; Ali is Ibn al-Madini and his teacher Sufyan ibn Uyayna. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p30 Ibn Shibrima was the leading jurist of Kufa in his time, and Ibn Kathir calls his inference a good one. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عبد الله بن شبرمة): Ibn Shibrima was the leading jurist of Kufa in his time, and Ibn Kathir calls his inference a good one. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(عبد الله بن شبرمة)]
- 73:20/q25/p31 A Sunan report says prayer requires the Opening and three verses; Ibn Kathir judges the Abu Masud report more authentic, famous, and specific, while finding its fit with al-Bukhari’s chapter heading questionable. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): A Sunan report says prayer requires the Opening and three verses; Ibn Kathir judges the Abu Masud report more authentic, famous, and specific, while finding its fit with al-Bukhari’s chapter heading questionable. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p32 When Qays ibn Sasaa asked how quickly to read, the Prophet first said every fifteen days, then allowed a weekly completion when Qays said he could do more. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): When Qays ibn Sasaa asked how quickly to read, the Prophet first said every fifteen days, then allowed a weekly completion when Qays said he could do more. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q25/p33 Taken alone, reports limiting completion to seven days would make the matter clear, but other reports establish that completing it sooner is permissible. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Taken alone, reports limiting completion to seven days would make the matter clear, but other reports establish that completing it sooner is permissible. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p34 Ibn Kathir grades Ahmad’s chain for Saad ibn Mundhir’s report good, strong, and hasan: Hasan ibn Musa is reliable, Ibn Lahia explicitly heard it, and the other named transmitters are trusted. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir grades Ahmad’s chain for Saad ibn Mundhir’s report good, strong, and hasan: Hasan ibn Musa is reliable, Ibn Lahia explicitly heard it, and the other named transmitters are trusted. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p35 Ibn Kathir calls the hundred-verse report very strange and says he found it only in al-Tabarani’s Mu'jam. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir calls the hundred-verse report very strange and says he found it only in al-Tabarani’s Mu'jam. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p36 The verse commands the obligatory prayer and zakah; this indicates zakah was prescribed in Mecca, while its thresholds and payment details were specified in Medina. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): The verse commands the obligatory prayer and zakah; this indicates zakah was prescribed in Mecca, while its thresholds and payment details were specified in Medina. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p37 In the Sahihayn, the Prophet tells a questioner that five daily prayers are required and there is no other obligation unless one volunteers. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (النبي): In the Sahihayn, the Prophet tells a questioner that five daily prayers are required and there is no other obligation unless one volunteers. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(النبي)]
- 73:20/q25/p38 Whatever good people send ahead will be preserved with God and is better than what they keep for themselves in worldly life. — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Whatever good people send ahead will be preserved with God and is better than what they keep for themselves in worldly life. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p39 Al-Naysābūrī calls knowledge of revelation's settings and sequence among the noblest Qur'anic sciences, including early, middle, and late Meccan and Medinan revelation. — BURHAN, d. 794 (أبو القاسم الحسن بن محمد بن حبيب النيسابورى): Al-Naysābūrī calls knowledge of revelation's settings and sequence among the noblest Qur'anic sciences, including early, middle, and late Meccan and Medinan revelation. [1 notes: BURHAN(أبو القاسم الحسن بن محمد بن حبيب النيسابورى)]
- 73:20/q25/p40 He distinguishes Meccan passages with Medinan rulings and the reverse, revelations addressed to the other community, and passages resembling the opposite revelation context. — BURHAN, d. 794 (أبو القاسم الحسن بن محمد بن حبيب النيسابورى): He distinguishes Meccan passages with Medinan rulings and the reverse, revelations addressed to the other community, and passages resembling the opposite revelation context. [1 notes: BURHAN(أبو القاسم الحسن بن محمد بن حبيب النيسابورى)]
- 73:20/q25/p41 His catalogue also classifies revelations by named location, night or day, accompanied or separately, and Meccan or Medinan verses embedded in sūras of the other kind. — BURHAN, d. 794 (أبو القاسم الحسن بن محمد بن حبيب النيسابورى): His catalogue also classifies revelations by named location, night or day, accompanied or separately, and Meccan or Medinan verses embedded in sūras of the other kind. [1 notes: BURHAN(أبو القاسم الحسن بن محمد بن حبيب النيسابورى)]
- 73:20/q25/p42 He includes material carried between Mecca, Medina, and Abyssinia, revelation given in summary, explanation, or coded form, and disputed classifications. — BURHAN, d. 794 (أبو القاسم الحسن بن محمد بن حبيب النيسابورى): He includes material carried between Mecca, Medina, and Abyssinia, revelation given in summary, explanation, or coded form, and disputed classifications. [1 notes: BURHAN(أبو القاسم الحسن بن محمد بن حبيب النيسابورى)]
- 73:20/q25/p43 He concludes that anyone unable to know and distinguish these twenty-five facets has no right to speak about God's Book. — BURHAN, d. 794 (أبو القاسم الحسن بن محمد بن حبيب النيسابورى): He concludes that anyone unable to know and distinguish these twenty-five facets has no right to speak about God's Book. [1 notes: BURHAN(أبو القاسم الحسن بن محمد بن حبيب النيسابورى)]
- 73:20/q25/p44 Earlier scriptures lacked the Qur'an's miraculous arrangement and were not made easy to memorize, unlike the reasons offered for its division into sūras. — BURHAN, d. 794 (author): Earlier scriptures lacked the Qur'an's miraculous arrangement and were not made easy to memorize, unlike the reasons offered for its division into sūras. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 73:20/q25/p45 Al-Zamakhsharī notes that earlier scriptures too were revealed in sūras, and that authors divide books into titled chapters. — BURHAN, d. 794 (الزمخشرى): Al-Zamakhsharī notes that earlier scriptures too were revealed in sūras, and that authors divide books into titled chapters. [1 notes: BURHAN(الزمخشرى)]
- 73:20/q25/p46 Dividing a broad subject into types and classes is more impressive and grand than presenting it as one undivided chapter. — BURHAN, d. 794 (الزمخشرى): Dividing a broad subject into types and classes is more impressive and grand than presenting it as one undivided chapter. [1 notes: BURHAN(الزمخشرى)]
- 73:20/q25/p47 Finishing a sūra or chapter renews a reader's energy, like reaching a milestone on a journey; this also motivates dividing the Qur'an into parts and fifths. — BURHAN, d. 794 (الزمخشرى): Finishing a sūra or chapter renews a reader's energy, like reaching a milestone on a journey; this also motivates dividing the Qur'an into parts and fifths. [1 notes: BURHAN(الزمخشرى)]
- 73:20/q25/p48 Division brings similar forms and counterparts together, allowing meanings and composition to be noticed in relation to one another. — BURHAN, d. 794 (الزمخشرى): Division brings similar forms and counterparts together, allowing meanings and composition to be noticed in relation to one another. [1 notes: BURHAN(الزمخشرى)]
- 73:20/q25/p49 The strict preservation rule belonged to the early period, when knowledge of the script was fresh; today, deviation risks confusing readers. — BURHAN, d. 794 (author): The strict preservation rule belonged to the early period, when knowledge of the script was fresh; today, deviation risks confusing readers. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 73:20/q25/p50 Ibn ʿAbd al-Salām says the Qur'an should no longer be written in the early orthography, lest ignorant readers alter it. — BURHAN, d. 794 (عز الدين بن عبد السلام): Ibn ʿAbd al-Salām says the Qur'an should no longer be written in the early orthography, lest ignorant readers alter it. [1 notes: BURHAN(عز الدين بن عبد السلام)]
- 73:20/q25/p51 Al-Bayhaqī urges preserving the letters used in early codices, whose writers were more learned, sincere, and trustworthy, and cautions against presuming to correct them. — BURHAN, d. 794 (البيهقى): Al-Bayhaqī urges preserving the letters used in early codices, whose writers were more learned, sincere, and trustworthy, and cautions against presuming to correct them. [1 notes: BURHAN(البيهقى)]
- 73:20/q25/p52 Sulaymān explains the report as a warning not to oppose the community through one's own opinion in matters of following precedent. — BURHAN, d. 794 (سليمان بن داود الهاشمى): Sulaymān explains the report as a warning not to oppose the community through one's own opinion in matters of following precedent. [1 notes: BURHAN(سليمان بن داود الهاشمى)]
- 73:20/q25/p53 A similar explanation of Zayd's statement is attributed to Abū ʿUbayd. — BURHAN, d. 794 (أبو عبيد): A similar explanation of Zayd's statement is attributed to Abū ʿUbayd. [1 notes: BURHAN(أبو عبيد)]
- 73:20/q25/p54 An inna-clause may provide the reason for the preceding command or statement, as in seeking forgiveness, praying for people, and disclaiming a blameless self. — BURHAN, d. 794 (author): An inna-clause may provide the reason for the preceding command or statement, as in seeking forgiveness, praying for people, and disclaiming a blameless self. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 73:20/q25/p55 He explains أن سيكون منكم مرضى by treating the illness as continuing in sense, just as ما علم الله زيد يخرج can mean that Zayd will not leave as far as God knows. — BURHAN, d. 794 (author): He explains أن سيكون منكم مرضى by treating the illness as continuing in sense, just as ما علم الله زيد يخرج can mean that Zayd will not leave as far as God knows. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 73:20/q25/p56 records deaths and credentials: al-Susanjardī died in 402, Bakr in 405, al-Burṣāṭī around 360, and al-Shaṭṭī around 370; each is described as reliable, exact, or accomplished. — NASHR, d. 833 (author): The author records deaths and credentials: al-Susanjardī died in 402, Bakr in 405, al-Burṣāṭī around 360, and al-Shaṭṭī around 370; each is described as reliable, exact, or accomplished. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 73:20/q25/p57 identifies al-Dānī as the leading authority on transmitters in the western lands and Abū al-ʿAlāʾ al-Hamadhānī as the leading authority in the eastern lands. — NASHR, d. 833 (author): The author identifies al-Dānī as the leading authority on transmitters in the western lands and Abū al-ʿAlāʾ al-Hamadhānī as the leading authority in the eastern lands. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 73:20/q25/p58 Hishām has sukun on the lām in thuluthay al-layl through all his routes; an isolated route reports ḍamm, which al-Dānī calls an error and the author excludes from his transmission paths. — NASHR, d. 833 (هشام): Hishām has sukun on the lām in thuluthay al-layl through all his routes; an isolated route reports ḍamm, which al-Dānī calls an error and the author excludes from his transmission paths. [1 notes: NASHR(هشام)]
- 73:20/q25/p59 Ibn Kathīr and the Kūfans use accusative fatḥ on fāʾ and thāʾ with ḍamm on both hāʾs; the other readers use genitive kasra on all four endings. — NASHR, d. 833 (ابن كثير والكوفيون): Ibn Kathīr and the Kūfans use accusative fatḥ on fāʾ and thāʾ with ḍamm on both hāʾs; the other readers use genitive kasra on all four endings. [1 notes: NASHR(ابن كثير والكوفيون)]
- 73:20/q25/p60 The verse teaches moderation in worship to spare people hardship, since life is the opportunity to gather good before death. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The verse teaches moderation in worship to spare people hardship, since life is the opportunity to gather good before death. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p61 Since the surah is among the earliest revelations, it foretells that the Muslim community will grow greatly. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Since the surah is among the earliest revelations, it foretells that the Muslim community will grow greatly. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p62 The surah explains the Prophet's comprehensive supplication: make religion, worldly life, and afterlife good; make life increase good and death relief from evil. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The surah explains the Prophet's comprehensive supplication: make religion, worldly life, and afterlife good; make life increase good and death relief from evil. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 73:20/q25/p63 Every good deed is repaid, whether done near death or earlier and whether by believer or disbeliever; sincere deeds are rewarded in the afterlife, others in this life. — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Every good deed is repaid, whether done near death or earlier and whether by believer or disbeliever; sincere deeds are rewarded in the afterlife, others in this life. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p64 Ibn ʿAbbās says Moses received seven emerald tablets containing all things, then shattered them when he saw the Israelites worshipping the calf; God raised six-sevenths and one-seventh remained. — ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās says Moses received seven emerald tablets containing all things, then shattered them when he saw the Israelites worshipping the calf; God raised six-sevenths and one-seventh remained. [1 notes: ITQAN(ابن عباس)]
- 73:20/q25/p65 A report traced through Jaʿfar b. Muḥammad, his father, and grandfather says Moses' tablets were made of lote trees from Paradise and measured twelve cubits long. — ITQAN, d. 911 (جعفر بن محمد): A report traced through Jaʿfar b. Muḥammad, his father, and grandfather says Moses' tablets were made of lote trees from Paradise and measured twelve cubits long. [1 notes: ITQAN(جعفر بن محمد)]
- 73:20/q25/p66 In the report of al-Futūn, Ibn ʿAbbās says Israel first resisted the obligations Moses brought, then accepted when the mountain loomed above them like a canopy. — ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن عباس): In the report of al-Futūn, Ibn ʿAbbās says Israel first resisted the obligations Moses brought, then accepted when the mountain loomed above them like a canopy. [1 notes: ITQAN(ابن عباس)]
- 73:20/q25/p67 Thābit b. al-Ḥajjāj says the Torah came all at once; Israel initially refused it as burdensome and accepted only after God shaded them with the mountain. — ITQAN, d. 911 (ثابت بن الحجاج): Thābit b. al-Ḥajjāj says the Torah came all at once; Israel initially refused it as burdensome and accepted only after God shaded them with the mountain. [1 notes: ITQAN(ثابت بن الحجاج)]
- 73:20/q25/p68 calls reports about the Torah's one-time revelation sound and explicit; he infers that gradual Qur'anic revelation made acceptance easier than a single delivery of many duties and prohibitions. — ITQAN, d. 911 (author): The author calls reports about the Torah's one-time revelation sound and explicit; he infers that gradual Qur'anic revelation made acceptance easier than a single delivery of many duties and prohibitions. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 73:20/q25/p69 ʿĀʾisha says early revelation described Paradise and Hell; legal commands came after people embraced Islam, since early prohibitions of wine or adultery would have been rejected. — ITQAN, d. 911 (عائشة): ʿĀʾisha says early revelation described Paradise and Hell; legal commands came after people embraced Islam, since early prohibitions of wine or adultery would have been rejected. [1 notes: ITQAN(عائشة)]
- 73:20/q25/p70 Knowing whether a revelation is earlier or later helps identify abrogation or specification, according to scholars who allow later specification. — ITQAN, d. 911 (author): Knowing whether a revelation is earlier or later helps identify abrogation or specification, according to scholars who allow later specification. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 73:20/q25/p71 Al-Naysābūrī lists twenty-five dimensions of revelation history and classification, including place, time, audience, travel, and disputed Makkan or Medinan status; he says one who cannot distinguish them may not speak about God's Book. — ITQAN, d. 911 (النيسابوري): Al-Naysābūrī lists twenty-five dimensions of revelation history and classification, including place, time, audience, travel, and disputed Makkan or Medinan status; he says one who cannot distinguish them may not speak about God's Book. [1 notes: ITQAN(النيسابوري)]
- 73:20/q25/p72 Ibn al-ʿArabī classifies revelation as Makkan, Medinan, travel-related, settled, night, day, heavenly, earthly, between heaven and earth, or beneath the earth in a cave. — ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن العربي): Ibn al-ʿArabī classifies revelation as Makkan, Medinan, travel-related, settled, night, day, heavenly, earthly, between heaven and earth, or beneath the earth in a cave. [1 notes: ITQAN(ابن العربي)]
- 73:20/q25/p73 Ibn al-Naqīb divides the Qur'an into Makkan, Medinan, mixed Makkan and Medinan, and neither Makkan nor Medinan passages. — ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن النقيب): Ibn al-Naqīb divides the Qur'an into Makkan, Medinan, mixed Makkan and Medinan, and neither Makkan nor Medinan passages. [1 notes: ITQAN(ابن النقيب)]
- 73:20/q25/p74 The best-known convention defines Makkan as revealed before the Hijra and Medinan as revealed afterward, regardless of location; Yaḥyā b. Sallām likewise classifies revelation before reaching Medina during the Hijra as Makkan, and later travel revelations… — ITQAN, d. 911 (author): The best-known convention defines Makkan as revealed before the Hijra and Medinan as revealed afterward, regardless of location; Yaḥyā b. Sallām likewise classifies revelation before reaching Medina during the Hijra as Makkan, and later travel revelations as Medinan. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 73:20/q25/p75 The form أن in “He knew that there would be” can be the lightened form of the emphatic particle, occurring after a verb of certainty or its equivalent. — ITQAN, d. 911 (author): The form أن in “He knew that there would be” can be the lightened form of the emphatic particle, occurring after a verb of certainty or its equivalent. | mentions 17:42, 9:118 [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 73:20/q25/p76 The end of Surat al-Muzzammil abrogated its opening night-prayer obligation, and the latter ruling was itself superseded by the obligation of the five prayers. — ITQAN, d. 911 (author): The end of Surat al-Muzzammil abrogated its opening night-prayer obligation, and the latter ruling was itself superseded by the obligation of the five prayers. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 73:20/q25/p77 Malik rejects writing the Qur'an in newer orthography and requires retaining the original script; al-Dānī says no scholar opposed this view. — ITQAN, d. 911 (مالك): Malik rejects writing the Qur'an in newer orthography and requires retaining the original script; al-Dānī says no scholar opposed this view. [1 notes: ITQAN(مالك)]
- 73:20/q25/p78 Abū ʿAmr explains that the extra written wāw and alif are absent in speech, as the wāw in أولوا. — ITQAN, d. 911 (أبو عمرو): Abū ʿAmr explains that the extra written wāw and alif are absent in speech, as the wāw in أولوا. [1 notes: ITQAN(أبو عمرو)]
- 73:20/q25/p79 Al-Bayhaqī says copyists must preserve the original orthography because its writers surpassed later people in knowledge, sincerity, and trustworthiness; one should not presume to correct them. — ITQAN, d. 911 (البيهقي): Al-Bayhaqī says copyists must preserve the original orthography because its writers surpassed later people in knowledge, sincerity, and trustworthiness; one should not presume to correct them. [1 notes: ITQAN(البيهقي)]
- 73:20/q25/p80 The Prophet's saying 'Be upright, and you will not attain it' is cited to illustrate that complete attainment is beyond reach. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (النبي): The Prophet's saying 'Be upright, and you will not attain it' is cited to illustrate that complete attainment is beyond reach. [1 notes: BURSEVI(النبي)]
- 73:20/q25/p81 A Persian couplet exhorts readers to send good ahead before death because there is no route to lasting life. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): A Persian couplet exhorts readers to send good ahead before death because there is no route to lasting life. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q25/p82 When Umar gave a poor man dates mixed with milk, he answered critics that the poor man's Lord knew the value of the gift. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (عمر رضي الله عنه): When Umar gave a poor man dates mixed with milk, he answered critics that the poor man's Lord knew the value of the gift. [1 notes: BURSEVI(عمر رضي الله عنه)]
- 73:20/q25/p83 Another grammarian takes وجد here as 'to find' rather than 'to know,' hence it governs one object and khayran is circumstantial from the pronoun. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (بعضهم): Another grammarian takes وجد here as 'to find' rather than 'to know,' hence it governs one object and khayran is circumstantial from the pronoun. [1 notes: BURSEVI(بعضهم)]
- 73:20/q25/p84 Ajr denotes the return or recompense yielded by an action, whether worldly or in the hereafter. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Ajr denotes the return or recompense yielded by an action, whether worldly or in the hereafter. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q25/p85 He says night worship came easily to many Successors, naming Abu Hanifa, Sa'id ibn al-Musayyib, Fudayl ibn Iyad, Abu Sulayman al-Darani, Malik ibn Dinar, and Ali ibn Bakkar. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): He says night worship came easily to many Successors, naming Abu Hanifa, Sa'id ibn al-Musayyib, Fudayl ibn Iyad, Abu Sulayman al-Darani, Malik ibn Dinar, and Ali ibn Bakkar. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q25/p86 A Persian couplet says to cast good into the water: even if the fish does not know, God knows, supporting hidden charitable giving. — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): A Persian couplet says to cast good into the water: even if the fish does not know, God knows, supporting hidden charitable giving. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 73:20/q25/p87 The differing readings seem to describe incompatible amounts; one answer is that the amounts occurred at different times and were both known to God. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The differing readings seem to describe incompatible amounts; one answer is that the amounts occurred at different times and were both known to God. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p88 If the original command required the longer vigil, the Prophet either disobeyed or erred in ijtihād, though those options are unacceptable under the positions described. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): If the original command required the longer vigil, the Prophet either disobeyed or erred in ijtihād, though those options are unacceptable under the positions described. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p89 The phrase is coordinated with the hidden subject of taqūmu; the intervening words make the coordination acceptable. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The phrase is coordinated with the hidden subject of taqūmu; the intervening words make the coordination acceptable. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p90 distinguishes the earlier passage's vigil for the Prophet alone from this passage's voluntary vigil for him and the community. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The author distinguishes the earlier passage's vigil for the Prophet alone from this passage's voluntary vigil for him and the community. | mentions 17:79 [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL+]
- 73:20/q25/p91 A variant report from ʿAʾisha says the obligatory practice lasted eight months. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (عائشة): A variant report from ʿAʾisha says the obligatory practice lasted eight months. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(عائشة)]
- 73:20/q25/p92 The Shāfiʿī-Mālikī proof from “no prayer except with al-Fātiḥa” takes the negation as invalidity; opponents suggest it means no complete prayer. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الشافعي ومالك): The Shāfiʿī-Mālikī proof from “no prayer except with al-Fātiḥa” takes the negation as invalidity; opponents suggest it means no complete prayer. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(الشافعي ومالك)]
- 73:20/q25/p93 The response argues that if al-Fātiḥa is part of prayer’s essence, omitting it permits negating prayer itself; even if literal negation is impossible, invalidity is a nearer metaphor than incompleteness, and consensus supports it. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The response argues that if al-Fātiḥa is part of prayer’s essence, omitting it permits negating prayer itself; even if literal negation is impossible, invalidity is a nearer metaphor than incompleteness, and consensus supports it. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p94 The response further cites consensus, the closeness of an invalid prayer to nonexistence, the continuing duty, and greater caution as reasons to read the Fātiḥa. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The response further cites consensus, the closeness of an invalid prayer to nonexistence, the continuing duty, and greater caution as reasons to read the Fātiḥa. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p95 A further argument says reading al-Fātiḥa certainly discharges the duty and avoids doubt, while leaving it creates fear of liability; the practical precaution is to read it. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الشافعي ومالك): A further argument says reading al-Fātiḥa certainly discharges the duty and avoids doubt, while leaving it creates fear of liability; the practical precaution is to read it. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(الشافعي ومالك)]
- 73:20/q25/p96 The early pious Muslims kept vigil as consistently as they performed Islam’s obligatory duties, cherishing private intimacy with God. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The early pious Muslims kept vigil as consistently as they performed Islam’s obligatory duties, cherishing private intimacy with God. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p97 Al-Shāfiʿī and Mālik specify al-Fātiḥa as obligatory in prayer, including a report that prayer without it is deficient; Ḥanafīs object that deficiency alone does not prove invalidity. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الشافعي ومالك): Al-Shāfiʿī and Mālik specify al-Fātiḥa as obligatory in prayer, including a report that prayer without it is deficient; Ḥanafīs object that deficiency alone does not prove invalidity. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(الشافعي ومالك)]
- 73:20/q25/p98 Al-Ālūsī replies that min can be explanatory rather than partitive, allowing early interpreters to understand the obligation as applying to everyone. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Ālūsī replies that min can be explanatory rather than partitive, allowing early interpreters to understand the obligation as applying to everyone. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p99 Although one might read “even al-Fātiḥa” as saying it suffices, al-Ālūsī says the more natural idiom makes it the minimum required and thus evidence for its obligation. — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Although one might read “even al-Fātiḥa” as saying it suffices, al-Ālūsī says the more natural idiom makes it the minimum required and thus evidence for its obligation. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p100 God turns again to them with grace and mercy, as He accepts the repentance of those who return to Him, and relieves the burden of the heavy vigil. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): God turns again to them with grace and mercy, as He accepts the repentance of those who return to Him, and relieves the burden of the heavy vigil. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q25/p101 Some exegetes take the command as recommendation or permission because the wording delegates the amount to what is easy; Abu Hayyan calls this the majority position. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Ebû Hayyân): Some exegetes take the command as recommendation or permission because the wording delegates the amount to what is easy; Abu Hayyan calls this the majority position. [1 notes: ELMALILI(Ebû Hayyân)]
- 73:20/q25/p102 Taken absolutely, the easy amount cannot be fixed, since what is bearable without fatigue varies with each person and circumstance. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Taken absolutely, the easy amount cannot be fixed, since what is bearable without fatigue varies with each person and circumstance. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q25/p103 For a twelve-hour night, he estimates the least vigil at three or four hours, the middle at four or five, and the greatest at six or seven. — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): For a twelve-hour night, he estimates the least vigil at three or four hours, the middle at four or five, and the greatest at six or seven. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 73:20/q25/p104 He considers it likely that revelation came in both or more forms, if all well-known readings are accepted as transmitted from the Prophet, so the readings multiply meanings. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): He considers it likely that revelation came in both or more forms, if all well-known readings are accepted as transmitted from the Prophet, so the readings multiply meanings. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p105 Ibn ʿUmar reports that the Prophet told Hafsa that ʿAbd Allah was righteous and would be better if he prayed at night. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (ابن عمر): Ibn ʿUmar reports that the Prophet told Hafsa that ʿAbd Allah was righteous and would be better if he prayed at night. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(ابن عمر)]
- 73:20/q25/p106 His near-two-thirds vigil represents the utmost limit, as with resolute messengers; the Prophet compared Moses's completion of the term to choosing the longer ten-year period in 28:29. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): His near-two-thirds vigil represents the utmost limit, as with resolute messengers; the Prophet compared Moses's completion of the term to choosing the longer ten-year period in 28:29. | mentions 28:29 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p107 Ali reports that the Prophet divided his home time among God, family, and himself, then devoted his own share to people, meeting their needs and guiding them. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (علي بن أبي طالب): Ali reports that the Prophet divided his home time among God, family, and himself, then devoted his own share to people, meeting their needs and guiding them. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(علي بن أبي طالب)]
- 73:20/q25/p108 The imperfect يعلم suggests continuing, renewed knowledge and thereby signals that the Prophet’s conduct is pleasing to God. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The imperfect يعلم suggests continuing, renewed knowledge and thereby signals that the Prophet’s conduct is pleasing to God. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p109 By contrast, يعلم in 33:18 occurs in a reproach: God has continually known the obstructors’ conduct, and no part of it is hidden from Him. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): By contrast, يعلم in 33:18 occurs in a reproach: God has continually known the obstructors’ conduct, and no part of it is hidden from Him. | mentions 33:18 [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p110 The cited hadith says to be steadfast, since full steadfastness cannot be attained; take on as much as one can bear. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The cited hadith says to be steadfast, since full steadfastness cannot be attained; take on as much as one can bear. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p111 God’s knowledge that the Prophet stands is an indirect expression of divine approval of their practice. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): God’s knowledge that the Prophet stands is an indirect expression of divine approval of their practice. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p112 Ibn Masʿud says that one who brings goods to a Muslim city seeking God’s approval and sells them at the day’s price has a standing with God like the martyrs; he cites this verse. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (عبد الله بن مسعود): Ibn Masʿud says that one who brings goods to a Muslim city seeking God’s approval and sells them at the day’s price has a standing with God like the martyrs; he cites this verse. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(عبد الله بن مسعود)]
- 73:20/q25/p113 The verse grounds legal reasoning from a broadly likely condition and supports general concessions for hardship common to most people, such as advance-payment sale, rather than isolated personal cases. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The verse grounds legal reasoning from a broadly likely condition and supports general concessions for hardship common to most people, such as advance-payment sale, rather than isolated personal cases. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p114 Abu Bakr told ʿUmar that he would fight anyone who distinguished between prayer and zakah. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أبو بكر): Abu Bakr told ʿUmar that he would fight anyone who distinguished between prayer and zakah. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(أبو بكر)]
- 73:20/q25/p115 The waw may coordinate this command with the preceding statement, making it a concluding exhortation to repair lapses in commanded good, including repentance for neglected obligations. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The waw may coordinate this command with the preceding statement, making it a concluding exhortation to repair lapses in commanded good, including repentance for neglected obligations. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p116 The separating pronoun here intensifies certainty. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The separating pronoun here intensifies certainty. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p117 ما is conditional. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): ما is conditional. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p118 من خير explains the otherwise indefinite ما. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): من خير explains the otherwise indefinite ما. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p119 Good is what religion identifies as praiseworthy and promises to reward. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Good is what religion identifies as praiseworthy and promises to reward. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p120 تجدوه means finding the deed’s reward and recompense; scripture often names compensation by the thing for which it is given. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): تجدوه means finding the deed’s reward and recompense; scripture often names compensation by the thing for which it is given. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p121 A Prophetic hadith says a person who hoards wealth without paying its due will meet a bald snake on Judgment Day saying, I am your wealth, your treasure. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A Prophetic hadith says a person who hoards wealth without paying its due will meet a bald snake on Judgment Day saying, I am your wealth, your treasure. - 73:20/q25/p122 The intervening pronoun is a separating pronoun even before an indefinite; this is allowed because أفعل من كذا resembles a definite noun, which cannot take the definite article. — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The intervening pronoun is a separating pronoun even before an indefinite; this is allowed because أفعل من كذا resembles a definite noun, which cannot take the definite article. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 73:20/q25/p123 A third view makes it obligatory for the Prophet and recommended for others. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (القوم): A third view makes it obligatory for the Prophet and recommended for others. [1 notes: TABATABAI(القوم)]
- 73:20/q25/p124 Calculating fixed portions through nights of changing annual length is hard for ordinary people, especially those sleeping early without staying awake all night to be certain. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Calculating fixed portions through nights of changing annual length is hard for ordinary people, especially those sleeping early without staying awake all night to be certain. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q25/p125 God's turning in mercy can enable repentance or obedience, lift some duties, or ease them; 9:118 describes Him turning to people so that they might repent. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): God's turning in mercy can enable repentance or obedience, lift some duties, or ease them; 9:118 describes Him turning to people so that they might repent. | mentions 9:118 [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q25/p126 Min khayr explains the relative pronoun, and khayr means all obedience, obligatory and recommended. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Min khayr explains the relative pronoun, and khayr means all obedience, obligatory and recommended. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q25/p127 Prayer should be performed in a way suited to the amount of time spent standing. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Prayer should be performed in a way suited to the amount of time spent standing. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q25/p128 Another parsing takes min as explanatory; the author dismisses this reading. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (قيل): Another parsing takes min as explanatory; the author dismisses this reading. [1 notes: TABATABAI(قيل)]
- 73:20/q25/p129 Some propose that the verse was revealed earlier but its legal ruling took effect later; the author calls this unsupported. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (قيل): Some propose that the verse was revealed earlier but its legal ruling took effect later; the author calls this unsupported. [1 notes: TABATABAI(قيل)]
- 73:20/q25/p130 Addressing God as your Lord intimates divine mercy and care; noting that He knows the Prophet's vigil also suggests gratitude for his effort, as in 17:19. — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Addressing God as your Lord intimates divine mercy and care; noting that He knows the Prophet's vigil also suggests gratitude for his effort, as in 17:19. | mentions 17:19 [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 73:20/q25/p131 Asad's literal gloss is those who are with the Prophet. — ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): Asad's literal gloss is those who are with the Prophet. [1 notes: ASAD-NOTES]
- 73:20/q25/p132 The passage emphasizes avoiding exaggeration even in devotional practice. — ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): The passage emphasizes avoiding exaggeration even in devotional practice. [1 notes: ASAD-NOTES]

- 73:20/q25/p137 Verse 20, revealed in Madinah, reduces the earlier night-prayer requirement and offers alternatives that preserve its purpose in changed circumstances. — ISLAHI-TADABBUR (author): Verse 20, revealed in Madinah, reduces the earlier night-prayer requirement and offers alternatives that preserve its purpose in changed circumstances. [1 notes: ISLAHI-TADABBUR]

[1 notes: ISLAHI-TADABBUR]
- 73:20/q25/p139 Journeys for a noble cause include seeking knowledge, performing hajj, or doing business. — ISLAHI-TADABBUR (author): Journeys for a noble cause include seeking knowledge, performing hajj, or doing business. [1 notes: ISLAHI-TADABBUR]
- 73:20/q25/p140 The hmz canonical variant drops hamza in Qurāni for Qurʾāni, removing the glottal stop. — QIRAAT-ER (hmz): The hmz canonical variant drops hamza in Qurāni for Qurʾāni, removing the glottal stop. [1 notes: QIRAAT-ER(hmz)]
- 73:20/q25/p141 “Perform the prayer” refers to the obligatory daily prayers. — STUDYQURAN (Ṭ): “Perform the prayer” refers to the obligatory daily prayers. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN(Ṭ)]

- 73:20/q25/p143 Nisba of a three-letter defective noun after fatha changes final ya or waw to waw, as huda/hadawi. — SIBAWAYH: Nisba of a three-letter defective noun after fatha changes final ya or waw to waw, as huda/hadawi. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]

- 73:20/q25/p145 After kasra, nisbas such as umawi and radawi preserve the ya by analogy. — SIBAWAYH: After kasra, nisbas such as umawi and radawi preserve the ya by analogy. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 73:20/q25/p146 Nimri and related forms illustrate the pattern; Taghlibi is nonbinding and Hanawi analogical. — SIBAWAYH: Nimri and related forms illustrate the pattern; Taghlibi is nonbinding and Hanawi analogical. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 73:20/q25/p147 A noun on fi'l is unchanged in nisba because it has only one kasra. — SIBAWAYH: A noun on fi'l is unchanged in nisba because it has only one kasra. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]

- 73:20/q25/p149 Albati and Jandali stay unchanged because they lack the repeated kasra. — SIBAWAYH: Albati and Jandali stay unchanged because they lack the repeated kasra. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 73:20/q25/p150 A verb form used as a name is declined when lacking a verbal prefix and resembling a noun pattern. — SIBAWAYH: A verb form used as a name is declined when lacking a verbal prefix and resembling a noun pattern. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 73:20/q25/p151 Yunus, Abu Amr, and Khalil decline noun-like names such as Darib. — SIBAWAYH: Yunus, Abu Amr, and Khalil decline noun-like names such as Darib. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH(يونس وأبو عمرو والخليل)]
- 73:20/q25/p152 Sibawayh rejects Isa's indeclinable view against Arab declension of Ka'sab. — SIBAWAYH: Sibawayh rejects Isa's indeclinable view against Arab declension of Ka'sab. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH(عيسى)]
- 73:20/q25/p153 Ka'saba denotes rapid short-stepped running and Ka'sab is a verb form. — SIBAWAYH: Ka'saba denotes rapid short-stepped running and Ka'sab is a verb form. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 73:20/q25/p154 Ibn Jala in poetry is a quotation and not evidence for an ordinary proper name. — SIBAWAYH: Ibn Jala in poetry is a quotation and not evidence for an ordinary proper name. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 73:20/q25/p155 Names from Daraba and Dahr-like forms without noun patterns are indeclinable; other forms vary. — SIBAWAYH: Names from Daraba and Dahr-like forms without noun patterns are indeclinable; other forms vary. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 73:20/q25/p156 Buqqam and Shalam are indeclinable because Arabic has no noun in their pattern; diminutives are declined. — SIBAWAYH: Buqqam and Shalam are indeclinable because Arabic has no noun in their pattern; diminutives are declined. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 73:20/q25/p157 Names resembling prefixed verbs are indeclinable while noun-like diminutives decline. — SIBAWAYH: Names resembling prefixed verbs are indeclinable while noun-like diminutives decline. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 73:20/q25/p158 If nun is a case ending rather than pronoun, the name declines like Muslimin. — SIBAWAYH: If nun is a case ending rather than pronoun, the name declines like Muslimin. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 73:20/q25/p159 Treating nun as plural marker allows noun-like reanalysis; feminine plurals remain indeclinable. — SIBAWAYH: Treating nun as plural marker allows noun-like reanalysis; feminine plurals remain indeclinable. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 73:20/q25/p160 Names from Darabna and Yadribna remain indeclinable under either account. — SIBAWAYH: Names from Darabna and Yadribna remain indeclinable under either account. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
